338	      'C’s cached keys now sign abandoned names. Cost O(subtree) + cascade — NOT cheap under (c).',
339	    ],

691	      L(']'),

697	      'no node re-seal, no IPNS republish — file content already self-seals under f3.readKey',

705	    desc: 'The other half of single-file sharing. Revoking a single-file grant rotates exactly ONE node: file3’s readKey + fileKey rotate, generation 0→1, the Sub parent ref is re-sealed, and D’s grant row is deleted. ipnsName unchanged; TEE untouched.',
706	    before: { label: 'before — file3 + D grant', lines: [

715	      L(`ipnsName: ${V.f3Ipns}   // UNCHANGED`, 'cmt'),
716	      L('shares: []', 'mod'),

1048	      L(']'),
1049	      L(`file3 reachable by B(read)+C(write) via Folder A -> Sub chain`, 'cmt'),
1050	      L(`bin.entries: [ ... ]`, 'cmt'),
1051	    ] },
1052	    after: { label: 'after — Sub read-body + bin', lines: [
1053	      L('Sub.children: []', 'mod'),
1054	      L(`removed SealedChildRef for ${V.f3Id}`, 'del'),
1055	      L(`file3 ROTATED: ${V.f3Read}->${V.f3ReadV2}, ${V.f3File}->${V.f3FileV2}, generation++`, 'mod'),
1056	      L(`content re-encrypted: ${V.f3Cid} -> ${V.f3CidV2}`, 'mod'),
1057	      L('bin.entries: [', 'add'),
1058	      L(`  BinEntry { childId:"${V.f3Id}", ipnsName:"${V.f3Ipns}", metaCid:"${V.f3MetaCid}" }`, 'add'),
1059	      L(']', 'add'),
1060	      L('shares: []', 'mod'),
1061	      L('  D grant on file3 (the single-file share): DELETED', 'del'),
1062	    ] },
1063	    steps: [
1064	      'client: unlink file3 from Sub; re-seal Sub read-body under SAME subRead; Folder A untouched',
1065	      'client: ROTATE file3 (mint f3ReadV2/f3FileV2, generation++, re-encrypt content to f3CidV2) — it left B/C scope (revocation)',
1066	      'IPFS: upload rotated file3 content + meta; add BinEntry to the bin (binIpns)',
1067	      'DB: DELETE D\'s grant row on file3 — deleting a shared node revokes its grant (the rotation already cut D cryptographically)',
1068	      'IPNS: publish Sub (subIpns seq++) then file3 (f3Ipns seq++) then bin (binIpns seq++); relay CAS gate. O(1) for one file + O(grants on it)',
1069	    ],
1070	  },
1071	  {
1072	    id: 'write-recipient-c-upload-notes',
1073	    title: 'Write-recipient C uploads "notes.txt" into shared Folder A',
1074	    tag: 'shared write add',
1075	    desc: 'C holds Folder A\'s writeKey (write grant), so C mints the new node and seals it into BOTH chains: readKey under faRead (owner + B + C reach it) and writeKey under faWrite (owner + writers reach the signer). C signs the Folder A publish with the folder Ed25519 it recovers via the write chain. B AUTO-sees notes.txt with NO re-grant.',
1076	    before: { label: 'before — Folder A read-body', lines: [
1077	      L('children: [ child[file1], child[file2], child[Sub] ]', 'cmt'),
1078	      L(`fa.generation: 0`, 'cmt'),
1079	    ] },
1080	    after: { label: 'after — Folder A read-body + write-body', lines: [
1081	      L('children: ['),
1082	      L('  child[file1], child[file2], child[Sub],', 'cmt'),
1083	      L('  SealedChildRef {', 'add'),
1084	      L(`    childId:"${V.cpId}", kind:"file", name:"notes.txt",`, 'add'),
1085	      L(`    ipnsName:"${V.cpIpns}", generation: 0,`, 'add'),
1086	      L(`    readKeySealed: AES-GCM(${V.cpRead}, ${V.faRead}, aad)`, 'add'),
1087	      L('  }', 'add'),
1088	      L(']'),
1089	      L(`fa write-body: + writeKeySealed: AES-GCM(cp.writeKey, ${V.faWrite}, aad)`, 'add'),
1090	      L(`fa.generation: 0  // add is not a rotation; NO re-grant for B`, 'cmt'),
1091	    ] },
1092	    steps: [
1093	      'client(C): mint notes.txt node — cpRead/cpFile, writeKey, ed25519 -> cpIpns; seal read-body (content) + write-body (its signer)',
1094	      'client(C): add SealedChildRef sealing cpRead under faRead AND cpWriteKey under faWrite; re-seal both Folder A bodies under SAME faRead/faWrite',
1095	      'IPFS: upload notes.txt blocks + Folder A bodies; IPNS: publish notes.txt then Folder A, C signs with the Folder A Ed25519 from the write chain',
1096	      'relay/TEE: CAS gate + anti-rollback on faIpns; owner + B reach notes.txt by chaining faRead — NO reWrap/re-grant fan-out. O(1)',
1097	    ],

1099	  {
1100	    id: 'write-recipient-c-mkdir-drafts',
1101	    title: 'Write-recipient C creates subfolder "drafts" in Folder A',
1102	    tag: 'shared write add',
1103	    desc: 'Same shared-write add as an upload: C mints the folder node and seals it into BOTH chains (readKey under faRead so owner+B+C read it; writeKey under faWrite so writers reach the signer), then signs the Folder A publish with the folder Ed25519. No rotation, no re-grant — B auto-reaches drafts.',
1104	    before: { label: 'before — Folder A read-body', lines: [

1106	      L(`fa.generation: 0`, 'cmt'),
1107	    ] },
1108	    after: { label: 'after — Folder A read-body + write-body', lines: [

1110	      L('  child[file1], child[file2], child[Sub],', 'cmt'),
1111	      L('  SealedChildRef {', 'add'),
1112	      L(`    childId:"${V.cpId}", kind:"folder", name:"drafts",`, 'add'),
1113	      L(`    ipnsName:"${V.cpIpns}", generation: 0,`, 'add'),
1114	      L(`    readKeySealed: AES-GCM(${V.cpRead}, ${V.faRead}, aad)`, 'add'),
1115	      L('  }', 'add'),
1116	      L(']'),
1117	      L(`fa write-body: + writeKeySealed: AES-GCM(drafts.writeKey, ${V.faWrite}, aad)`, 'add'),
1118	      L(`fa.generation: 0  // unchanged; B auto-reaches drafts via chain`, 'cmt'),
1119	    ] },
1120	    steps: [
1121	      'client(C): mint drafts folder node — cpRead, writeKey, ed25519 -> cpIpns; seal empty read-body (children:[]) + write-body (its signer)',
1122	      'client(C): add SealedChildRef sealing cpRead under faRead AND drafts.writeKey under faWrite; re-seal both Folder A bodies under SAME faRead/faWrite',
1123	      'IPFS/IPNS: publish drafts then Folder A; C signs Folder A with the folder Ed25519 from the write chain',
1124	      'relay/TEE: CAS gate on faIpns; owner + B chain to drafts with NO re-grant. O(1)',
1125	    ],
1126	  },
1127	  {
1128	    id: 'write-recipient-c-delete-file1',
1129	    title: 'Write-recipient C deletes file1 inside Folder A',
1130	    tag: 'shared write delete',
1131	    desc: 'C holds Folder A\'s writeKey, so C can unlink file1 (re-seal Folder A read-body under SAME faRead, sign with the folder Ed25519) and bin the node. NUANCE: C cannot ROTATE file1\'s OWN external sub-shares — revoking file1\'s independent grants is an OWNER-scoped action and is flagged, not performed by C.',
1132	    before: { label: 'before — Folder A read-body', lines: [
1133	      L('children: ['),
1134	      L(`  SealedChildRef { childId:"${V.f1Id}", name:"file1", readKeySealed: AES-GCM(${V.f1Read}, ${V.faRead}, aad) },`),
1135	      L('  child[file2], child[Sub]', 'cmt'),
1136	      L(']'),
1137	      L(`bin.entries: [ ... ]`, 'cmt'),
1138	    ] },
1139	    after: { label: 'after — Folder A read-body + bin', lines: [
1140	      L('children: [ child[file2], child[Sub] ]', 'mod'),
1141	      L(`removed SealedChildRef for ${V.f1Id}`, 'del'),
1142	      L('bin.entries: [', 'add'),
1143	      L(`  BinEntry { childId:"${V.f1Id}", ipnsName:"${V.f1Ipns}" }`, 'add'),
1144	      L(']', 'add'),
1145	      L(`file1 keys NOT rotated by C; external sub-shares = OWNER-scoped, flagged`, 'cmt'),
1146	      L(`fa.generation: 0  // unlink is not a rotation`, 'cmt'),
1147	    ] },
1148	    steps: [
1149	      'client(C): unlink file1 from Folder A; re-seal Folder A read-body under SAME faRead; sign Folder A publish with the folder Ed25519 from the write chain',
1150	      'IPFS/IPNS: add BinEntry for file1 to the bin (binIpns); publish Folder A (faIpns seq++)',
1151	      'relay/TEE: CAS gate + anti-rollback on faIpns; B/owner see file1 gone by chaining',
1152	      'FLAG (owner-scoped): if file1 had its OWN external sub-shares, revoking those requires ROTATE by the owner — C cannot perform it. O(1) for the unlink',
1153	    ],
1154	  },
1155	  {
1156	    id: 'owner-delete-folder-a-cascade',
1157	    title: 'Owner deletes Folder A (parent of shared + sub-shared nodes)',
1158	    tag: 'delete · cascade revoke',
1159	    desc: 'Deleting a node deletes its whole subtree, so EVERY grant rooted anywhere in that subtree must be revoked. Folder A is shared with B(read) + C(write); file3 inside it is independently shared with D. Deleting Folder A unlinks it from root, rotates the subtree, bins it, and DELETES all three grants (B, C, D).',
1160	    before: { label: 'before — root + grants', lines: [
1161	      L('root.children: [ Folder A, Folder S2 ]'),
1162	      L('shares: ['),
1163	      L(`  B(read  @ Folder A ${V.faIpns}),`, 'cmt'),
1164	      L(`  C(write @ Folder A ${V.faIpns}),`, 'cmt'),
1165	      L(`  D(read  @ file3 ${V.f3Ipns}),   // nested sub-share`, 'cmt'),
1166	      L(']'),
1167	    ] },
1168	    after: { label: 'after — root + grants + bin', lines: [
1169	      L('root.children: [ Folder S2 ]', 'mod'),
1170	      L('  removed SealedChildRef for Folder A', 'del'),
1171	      L('subtree ROTATED: Folder A, file1, file2, Sub, file3 (generation++)', 'mod'),
1172	      L('shares: []', 'mod'),
1173	      L('  B grant: DELETED', 'del'),
1174	      L('  C grant: DELETED', 'del'),
1175	      L('  D grant (nested, on file3): DELETED', 'del'),
1176	      L('bin.entries: [ BinEntry { Folder A subtree } ]', 'add'),
1177	    ] },
1178	    steps: [
1179	      'client: unlink Folder A from root; re-seal root read-body under SAME rootRead',
1180	      'client: enumerate ALL grants whose rootNodeId is in the subtree {Folder A, file1, file2, Sub, file3} — finds B, C (Folder A) + D (file3)',
1181	      'client: ROTATE the subtree (scope-root first) + move nodes/content to the bin (binIpns)',
1182	      'DB: DELETE the B, C and D grant rows; IPNS: publish root + bin (seq++). Cost O(subtree) — a parent delete cascades to every descendant grant',
1183	    ],
1184	  },
1185	];
1186	
1187	const groups = [
1188	  { title: 'Foundations', desc: 'The lifecycle of one folder + file and the two share types — the spine the permutations build on.', flows: foundationsFlows },
1189	  { title: 'Core operations', desc: 'Everyday filesystem ops, mostly in a private (unshared) part of the vault.', flows: coreFlows },
1190	  { title: 'Sharing controls', desc: 'Grant-plane changes — usually one DB row, no node re-sealed. Includes single-file sharing.', flows: sharingFlows },
1191	  { title: 'Permutations · owner moves under sharing', desc: 'The same move is O(1), a grant, or a rotation depending on which scope boundary it crosses.', flows: permMoveFlows },
1192	  { title: 'Permutations · rename, delete & write-recipient actions', desc: 'Overwriting/destroying shared nodes, and a write-recipient mutating inside the share.', flows: permRenameFlows },
1193	];
1194	
1195	let __n = 0;
1196	const numberedGroups = groups
1197	  .filter((g) => g.flows.length)
1198	  .map((g) => ({ ...g, flows: g.flows.map((f) => ({ ...f, n: String(++__n).padStart(2, '0') })) }));
1199	
1200	// ---- operating-rules cheat-sheet ----
1201	const rules = [
1202	  { op: 'Create / upload / restore', ch: 'new node + parent read-body re-seal', rg: 'no (auto-reach)', rot: 'no', ipns: 'new node’s own', cost: 'O(1)' },
1203	  { op: 'Copy', ch: 'decrypt + RE-ENCRYPT under fresh key → new CID (can’t reuse source CID)', rg: 'no (auto-reach)', rot: 'no', ipns: 'new node’s own', cost: 'O(content)' },
1204	  { op: 'Rename (file / folder)', ch: 'name on the EDGE → parent read-body re-seal', rg: 'no', rot: 'no', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(1)' },
1205	  { op: 'Move within same scope', ch: 're-link both parents (folder = O(1))', rg: 'no', rot: 'no', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(1)' },
1206	  { op: 'Move INTO a share', ch: 're-link; key now reachable by recipients', rg: 'grant via chain', rot: 'no', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(1)' },
1207	  { op: 'Move OUT of a share', ch: 're-link + rotate (leaves scope)', rg: 're-mint remaining', rot: 'yes · read', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(subtree)' },
1208	  { op: 'Move BETWEEN shares', ch: 'rotate (cut A) then link (grant B)', rg: 're-mint + grant', rot: 'yes · read', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(subtree)' },
1209	  { op: 'Edit / new version', ch: 'content swap; old kept in versions[]', rg: 'no', rot: 'no', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(1)' },
1210	  { op: 'Delete private → bin', ch: 'unlink + BinEntry', rg: 'no', rot: 'no', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(1)' },
1211	  { op: 'Delete SHARED → bin', ch: 'unlink + BinEntry + rotate + delete grants (cascades to nested sub-shares)', rg: 'delete subtree grants', rot: 'yes · read', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(subtree)' },
1212	  { op: 'Rename OVER shared dest', ch: 'rotate victim (revoke) + parent re-seal', rg: 're-mint', rot: 'yes · read', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(victim)' },
1213	  { op: 'Add recipient / upgrade r→w', ch: 'one grant row (ECIES to recipient)', rg: 'new grant', rot: 'no', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(1)' },
1214	  { op: 'Revoke read', ch: 'rotate readKey + fileKey of subtree', rg: 're-mint remaining', rot: 'yes · read', ipns: 'unchanged', cost: 'O(items)' },
1215	  { op: 'Revoke write / downgrade', ch: 'Ed25519 cascade + parent re-point', rg: 'update grants', rot: 'yes · write', ipns: 'CHANGES (new k51)', cost: 'O(subtree)' },
1216	  { op: 'Write-recipient adds in share', ch: 'new node sealed into BOTH chains', rg: 'no (auto-reach)', rot: 'no', ipns: 'new node’s own', cost: 'O(1)' },
1217	];
1218	---
1219	
1220	<Base
1221	  title="CipherBox — The Node Model (Explainer)"
1222	  description="How encrypted folder/file metadata, key-chaining, sharing, and revocation work in the proposed CipherBox Node model."
1223	>
1224	  <!-- Intro -->
1225	  <section class="section intro">
1226	    <p class="kicker">{'// proposed design — approach (c) — not yet shipped'}</p>
1227	    <h1 class="intro-title">The Node Model</h1>
1228	    <p class="intro-lede">
1229	      One <strong>Node</strong> type for folders, files, and the vault root. Read access flows
1230	      down a <strong>key chain</strong> sealed into the metadata itself — so sharing a subtree is
1231	      <em>one</em> wrapped key, not a per-item table. Write access is a separate chain of Ed25519
1232	      signing keys. The server stays a zero-knowledge relay throughout.
1233	    </p>
1234	    <div class="planes">
1235	      <div class="plane card">
1236	        <h3 class="plane-h">{'> read plane'}</h3>
1237	        <p>Metadata key-chaining. Revocation = rotate the subtree (expensive, O(items)).</p>
1238	      </div>
1239	      <div class="plane card">
1240	        <h3 class="plane-h">{'> write plane'}</h3>
1241	        <p>Ed25519 signing keys. Revocation = rotate the keypairs / re-point parents.</p>
1242	      </div>
1243	    </div>
1244	  </section>
1245	
1246	  <!-- Anatomy -->
1247	  <section class="section">
1248	    <h2 class="section-title">Anatomy of a Node</h2>
1249	    <p class="section-desc">
1250	      What the client holds (decrypted) vs. what IPFS and the relay store (sealed). The relay only
1251	      ever sees the right-hand object.
1252	    </p>
1253	    <div class="anatomy-grid">
1254	      <div>
1255	        <div class="meta-label">decrypted — client only</div>
1256	        <div class="meta">{anatomy.decrypted.map((l) => <div class={`ln ${l.k}`}>{l.t || ' '}</div>)}</div>
1257	      </div>
1258	      <div>
1259	        <div class="meta-label">published — relay / IPFS see this</div>
1260	        <div class="meta">{anatomy.published.map((l) => <div class={`ln ${l.k}`}>{l.t || ' '}</div>)}</div>
1261	      </div>
1262	    </div>
1263	    <div class="meta-label" style="margin-top: var(--spacing-lg)">the chain link</div>
1264	    <div class="meta">{anatomy.childref.map((l) => <div class={`ln ${l.k}`}>{l.t || ' '}</div>)}</div>
1265	
1266	    <div class="secrets">
1267	      {secrets.map((s) => (
1268	        <div class="secret">
1269	          <span class="secret-k">{s.k}</span>
1270	          <span class="secret-t">{s.t}</span>
1271	        </div>
1272	      ))}
1273	    </div>
1274	  </section>
1275	
1276	  <!-- Rules -->
1277	  <section class="section">
1278	    <h2 class="section-title">Operating rules</h2>
1279	    <p class="section-desc">
1280	      The whole model reduces to a handful of rules. Only operations that make a node LEAVE a
1281	      recipient’s scope trigger a key rotation; everything else is a parent re-seal or a single
1282	      grant row. k51 names change only on write-revocation.
1283	    </p>
1284	    <div class="rules-wrap">
1285	      <table class="rules-table">
1286	        <thead>
1287	          <tr><th>operation</th><th>what changes</th><th>re-grant?</th><th>rotation?</th><th>ipnsName</th><th>cost</th></tr>
1288	        </thead>
1289	        <tbody>
1290	          {rules.map((r) => (
1291	            <tr>
1292	              <td class="r-op">{r.op}</td>
1293	              <td>{r.ch}</td>
1294	              <td>{r.rg}</td>
1295	              <td class={r.rot.startsWith('yes') ? 'r-rot' : 'r-no'}>{r.rot}</td>
1296	              <td class={r.ipns.startsWith('CHANGES') ? 'r-rot' : ''}>{r.ipns}</td>
1297	              <td class="r-cost">{r.cost}</td>
1298	            </tr>
1299	          ))}
1300	        </tbody>
1301	      </table>
1302	    </div>
1303	  </section>
1304	
1305	  <!-- Flows -->
1306	  <section class="section">
1307	    <h2 class="section-title">Transforms</h2>
1308	    <p class="section-desc">
1309	      Each operation as a before → after on the affected metadata, with the per-layer steps.
1310	      Highlighted lines are what changed.
1311	    </p>
1312	
1313	    {numberedGroups.map((g) => (
1314	      <div class="flow-group">
1315	        <h3 class="group-title">{g.title}</h3>
1316	        <p class="group-desc">{g.desc}</p>
1317	        <div class="flows">
1318	          {g.flows.map((f) => (
1319	        <article class="flow" id={f.id}>
1320	          <header class="flow-head">
1321	            <span class="flow-n">{f.n}</span>
1322	            <h3 class="flow-title">{f.title}</h3>
1323	            <span class="flow-tag">{f.tag}</span>
1324	          </header>
1325	          <p class="flow-desc">{f.desc}</p>
1326	
1327	          <div class="panels">
1328	            <div class="panel">
1329	              <div class="meta-label">{f.before.label}</div>
1330	              <div class="meta">{f.before.lines.map((l) => <div class={`ln ${l.k}`}>{l.t || ' '}</div>)}</div>
1331	            </div>
1332	            <div class="panel-arrow" aria-hidden="true">{'→'}</div>
1333	            <div class="panel">
1334	              <div class="meta-label">{f.after.label}</div>
1335	              <div class="meta">{f.after.lines.map((l) => <div class={`ln ${l.k}`}>{l.t || ' '}</div>)}</div>
1336	            </div>
1337	          </div>
1338	
1339	          <ol class="flow-legend steps">
1340	            {f.steps.map((s, i) => (
1341	              <li class="legend-item">
1342	                <span class="legend-step">{String(i + 1).padStart(2, '0')}</span>
1343	                <span class="legend-label">{s}</span>
1344	              </li>
1345	            ))}
1346	          </ol>
1347	        </article>
1348	          ))}
1349	        </div>
1350	      </div>
1351	    ))}
1352	  </section>
1353	
1354	  <!-- Asymmetry summary -->
1355	  <section class="section">
1356	    <h2 class="section-title">The revocation asymmetry</h2>
1357	    <div class="asym-grid">
1358	      <div class="card asym">
1359	        <h3 class="asym-h">{'> read-revoke'}</h3>
1360	        <p>Rotate readKey + fileKey of every node in scope. k51 names unchanged; TEE untouched.
1361	          <strong>O(items)</strong> republishes. Already-fetched content is irreducibly readable (it’s on IPFS).</p>
1362	      </div>
1363	      <div class="card asym">
1364	        <h3 class="asym-h">{'> write-revoke (c)'}</h3>
1365	        <p>Rotate Ed25519 keypairs → new k51 names → parent re-point cascade + TEE re-enroll + grant
1366	          updates. <strong>O(subtree)</strong>, and heavier per node than read-revoke. The cheap
1367	          O(1) version only exists under approach (a) mediated writes.</p>
1368	      </div>
1369	    </div>
1370	    <p class="foot"><a href="/">{'← back'}</a></p>
1371	  </section>
1372	</Base>
1373	
1374	<style>
1375	  .intro {
1376	    padding-bottom: var(--spacing-2xl);
1377	  }
1378	  .kicker {
1379	    color: var(--color-text-dim);
1380	    font-size: var(--font-size-sm);
1381	    margin-bottom: var(--spacing-md);
1382	  }
1383	  .intro-title {
1384	    font-size: var(--font-size-3xl);
1385	    font-weight: var(--font-weight-bold);
1386	    letter-spacing: 0.04em;
1387	    text-shadow: var(--glow-green);
1388	    margin-bottom: var(--spacing-lg);
1389	  }
1390	  .intro-lede {
1391	    max-width: 760px;
1392	    color: var(--color-text-muted);
1393	    font-size: var(--font-size-md);
1394	    line-height: 1.7;
1395	  }
1396	  .intro-lede strong { color: var(--color-green-primary); }
1397	  .intro-lede em { color: var(--color-text-primary); font-style: normal; text-decoration: underline; }
1398	
1399	  .planes {
1400	    display: grid;
1401	    grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr;
1402	    gap: var(--spacing-lg);
1403	    margin-top: var(--spacing-xl);
1404	  }
1405	  .plane-h { font-size: var(--font-size-md); margin-bottom: var(--spacing-xs); }
1406	  .plane p { color: var(--color-text-muted); font-size: var(--font-size-sm); }
1407	
1408	  /* ---- metadata panels ---- */
1409	  .meta-label {
1410	    font-size: var(--font-size-xs);
1411	    text-transform: uppercase;
1412	    letter-spacing: 0.08em;
1413	    color: var(--color-text-secondary);
1414	    margin-bottom: var(--spacing-xs);

---
name: grilling
description: Interview the user relentlessly about a plan or design. Use when the user wants to stress-test a plan before building, or uses any 'grill' trigger phrases.
---

Interview me relentlessly about every aspect of this plan until we reach a shared understanding. Walk down each branch of the design tree, resolving dependencies between decisions one-by-one. For each question, provide your recommended answer.

Ask the questions one at a time, waiting for feedback on each question before continuing. Asking multiple questions at once is bewildering.

If a question can be answered by exploring the codebase, explore the codebase instead.
=====DOMAIN-MODELING=====
---
name: domain-modeling
description: Build and sharpen a project's domain model. Use when the user wants to pin down domain terminology or a ubiquitous language, record an architectural decision, or when another skill needs to maintain the domain model.
---

# Domain Modeling

Actively build and sharpen the project's domain model as you design. This is the *active* discipline — challenging terms, inventing edge-case scenarios, and writing the glossary and decisions down the moment they crystallise. (Merely *reading* `CONTEXT.md` for vocabulary is not this skill — that's a one-line habit any skill can do. This skill is for when you're changing the model, not just consuming it.)

## File structure

Most repos have a single context:

```
/
├── CONTEXT.md
├── docs/
│   └── adr/
│       ├── 0001-event-sourced-orders.md
│       └── 0002-postgres-for-write-model.md
└── src/
```

If a `CONTEXT-MAP.md` exists at the root, the repo has multiple contexts. The map points to where each one lives:

```
/
├── CONTEXT-MAP.md
├── docs/
│   └── adr/                          ← system-wide decisions
├── src/
│   ├── ordering/
│   │   ├── CONTEXT.md
│   │   └── docs/adr/                 ← context-specific decisions
│   └── billing/
│       ├── CONTEXT.md
│       └── docs/adr/
```

Create files lazily — only when you have something to write. If no `CONTEXT.md` exists, create one when the first term is resolved. If no `docs/adr/` exists, create it when the first ADR is needed.

## During the session

### Challenge against the glossary

When the user uses a term that conflicts with the existing language in `CONTEXT.md`, call it out immediately. "Your glossary defines 'cancellation' as X, but you seem to mean Y — which is it?"

### Sharpen fuzzy language

When the user uses vague or overloaded terms, propose a precise canonical term. "You're saying 'account' — do you mean the Customer or the User? Those are different things."

### Discuss concrete scenarios

When domain relationships are being discussed, stress-test them with specific scenarios. Invent scenarios that probe edge cases and force the user to be precise about the boundaries between concepts.

### Cross-reference with code

When the user states how something works, check whether the code agrees. If you find a contradiction, surface it: "Your code cancels entire Orders, but you just said partial cancellation is possible — which is right?"

### Update CONTEXT.md inline

When a term is resolved, update `CONTEXT.md` right there. Don't batch these up — capture them as they happen. Use the format in [CONTEXT-FORMAT.md](./CONTEXT-FORMAT.md).

`CONTEXT.md` should be totally devoid of implementation details. Do not treat `CONTEXT.md` as a spec, a scratch pad, or a repository for implementation decisions. It is a glossary and nothing else.

### Offer ADRs sparingly

Only offer to create an ADR when all three are true:

1. **Hard to reverse** — the cost of changing your mind later is meaningful
2. **Surprising without context** — a future reader will wonder "why did they do it this way?"
3. **The result of a real trade-off** — there were genuine alternatives and you picked one for specific reasons

If any of the three is missing, skip the ADR. Use the format in [ADR-FORMAT.md](./ADR-FORMAT.md).
```

Perfect. Now I have comprehensive coverage. Let me compile the findings:

---

## CURRENT DOCUMENTED STATE — CipherBox Sharing & Revocation

### 1. METADATA MODEL (SCHEMAS.md, v1.1)

**Core objects today:**
- **FolderMetadata (v2)**: `children: FolderChild[]` (folder/root only); encrypted under folder's own `folderKey` (AES-256-GCM)
- **FolderEntry** (subfolder child): ECIES-wraps **both** `folderKeyEncrypted` (read) **and** `ipnsPrivateKeyEncrypted` (write) to owner
- **FileMetadata (v1)**: sealed under **parent folder's** `folderKey` (not its own), containing `fileKeyEncrypted` (ECIES-wrapped), `cid`, `fileIv`, `size`, optional `versions`
- **FilePointer** (v2 slim file ref): `fileMetaIpnsName`, optional random `ipnsPrivateKeyEncrypted`; file metadata lives at separate IPNS
- **VaultKeyBlob (v2)**: separate IPFS blob at vault-key IPNS; wraps `rootFolderKey` via ECIES to owner's `publicKey`
- **Schema versions**: FolderMetadata is v2 (v1 removed pre-production); FileMetadata is v1 (additive fields: `encryptionMode`, `versions`)

**Key asymmetry being fixed:** file metadata keyed under parent `folderKey`, not its own `readKey`, prevents single-file sharing.

---

### 2. SHARING MODEL (SHARING.md, v1.0)

**Database tables:**
- **`shares` row** (one per active grant): `sharer_id`, `recipient_id`, `ipns_name`, `item_type` (folder/file), `encrypted_key` (ECIES-wrapped `folderKey` or parent `folderKey` for files), `permission` ('read'/'write'), `encrypted_ipns_key` (for write only), `revoked_at` (soft-delete flag)
- **`share_keys` rows** (`O(items × recipients)`, the table being deleted): `share_id`, `key_type` ('file'/'folder'/'file-ipns'/'folder-ipns'), `item_id`, `encrypted_key` (ECIES-wrapped child keys)

**Read flow:** sharer re-wraps keys client-side, POSTs to API; recipient unwraps with own `privateKey`; fan-out `reWrapForRecipients` adds per-child `share_keys` rows on new uploads (Section 3.4 in proposal).

**Write sharing:** `encrypted_ipns_key` column holds the Ed25519 IPNS key wrapped for recipient. Recipient can sign/publish with that raw key. No ownership/share check in publish auth (`ipns.service.ts:226` — key-possession only).

---

### 3. REVOCATION (SHARING.md § "Revocation and lazy key rotation")

**Read revocation (lazy, **unsound**):**
- `DELETE /shares/:shareId` sets `revoked_at` timestamp (soft-delete)
- `executeLazyRotation` (triggered before folder write) rotates **only the share-root `folderKey`**, never descendants
- Lazy rotation never walks the tree; revoked reader holding cached subtree keys keeps reading (CRIT gap)
- `share_keys` rows kept "for lazy rotation" but never re-wrapped

**Write revocation (non-existent):**
- Deleting `shares` row has zero crypto effect (recipient cached Ed25519 key, can publish forever)
- `ipns.service.ts:226` confirms "no ownership/share check" — only key-possession matters

**Documented gaps (SHARING.md Section "Revocation"):** lazy rotation is folder-coarse; per-file revocation not addressed.

---

### 4. IPNS/PUBLISH MODEL (METADATA_SCHEMAS.md §15, ARCHITECTURE.md)

**Key derivation:**
- **Root vault IPNS**: HKDF-SHA256(owner's `secp256k1 privateKey`, salt='CipherBox-v1', info='cipherbox-vault-ipns-v1') → Ed25519 keypair
- **Vault key blob IPNS**: Same seed, different info='cipherbox-vault-key-ipns-v1' (separate from root folder IPNS, prevents folder publishes overwriting key blob)
- **Per-file IPNS (legacy)**: HKDF with info='cipherbox-file-ipns-v1:{fileId}' (fallback if `FilePointer.ipnsPrivateKeyEncrypted` absent)
- **Subfolder & new-file IPNS**: Random Ed25519 keypair, ECIES-wrapped in parent metadata

**Publishing:** IPNS record `sequence` is incremented per publish; anti-rollback enforced by `ipns.service.ts:240` (strict sequence check). TEE republisher every 3–6 hours re-signs at `sequence + 1` without touching metadata content.

**No generation concept**: today's design has no per-node mutation/rotation epoch tracking. `keyEpoch` exists only for TEE pubkey rotation (write-plane), not read-key rotation.

---

### 5. KEY VOCABULARY (per docs)

| Term | Today's Meaning |
|---|---|
| `rootFolderKey` | 32-byte AES-256 master key, stored in `VaultKeyBlob` ECIES envelope |
| `folderKey` | 32-byte per-folder AES-256 key, ECIES-wrapped in parent's `FolderEntry` |
| `fileKey` | 32-byte per-file AES-256 key, ECIES-wrapped in file's own `FileMetadata` |
| `ipnsName` | CIDv1 libp2p-key multiaddr (k51...); derived from IPNS Ed25519 pubkey |
| `ipnsPrivateKey` | 64-byte Ed25519 seed, ECIES-wrapped in metadata or `ipns_republish_schedule` table |
| `keyEpoch` | TEE pubkey rotation counter (write-plane only), not per-node |

---

### 6. BIN/TRASH MODEL

**No documented bin/trash feature exists.** FILESYSTEM_SPECIFICATION.md filters macOS/Windows trash files (`.Trash`, `$recycle.bin`, etc.) from syncing but does not implement CipherBox-native trash. Deletes are direct unlinks; no BinEntry objects or recoverable delete model documented.

---

### 7. MIGRATION/EVOLUTION DISCIPLINE

**METADATA_EVOLUTION_PROTOCOL.md (v1.0):**
- Additive changes: optional fields with sensible defaults, version NOT bumped
- Breaking changes: version field bumped (e.g., FolderMetadata v1→v2 removed inline `FileEntry`, introduced `FilePointer`)
- Cross-platform rule: TypeScript and Rust must produce byte-identical JSON; both implementations move together
- Recovery tool compliance required for any breaking change
- Historical breakage: v1→v2 folder was clean-break pre-production vault wipe; v1 later removed entirely

**DATABASE_EVOLUTION_PROTOCOL.md (v1.0):**
- `synchronize: false` in all environments; migrations auto-run via `migrationsRun: true`
- Idempotency required (`IF NOT EXISTS`, column checks)
- FullSchema is point-in-time baseline; new tables use incremental migrations with `IF NOT EXISTS`
- Migration timestamps must respect table creation ordering (CREATE before ALTER on same table)

---

## WHAT THE PROPOSAL CONTRADICTS OR OMITS

| Item | Current Doc | Proposal § | Contradiction / Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Metadata schema** | Separate `FolderMetadata`, `FileMetadata`, `FilePointer` | §2.1–2.9 | **Unified `Node`** (kind: folder\|file\|root), collapsing all three schemas and `encryptedRootFolderKey`. Breaking change requiring new version. |
| **File metadata key** | Sealed under parent `folderKey` | §2.9 | **File content sealed under file's own `readKey`** — requires file node to have independent `readKey` (enables single-file share). Semantic change. |
| **Two sealed bodies** | One body per node (GCM-sealed metadata) | §2.2, §2.4 | **Separate `readSealed` and `writeSealed`** (two AES-GCM envelopes, different keys). Read grants skip write-body entirely. Structural separation, not in today's design. |
| **AAD binding** | No AAD in today's `sealAesGcm` | §2.5 | **AAD-bound seals** (SHA256 hash of node domain\|id\|kind\|generation\|role). Requires new crypto primitive (`sealAesGcmAad`/`unsealAesGcmAad`). |
| **Generation field** | Does not exist (only `keyEpoch` for TEE) | §2.3, §2.6, §2.7 | **Per-node `generation` counter** as read-key rotation clock; plaintext on envelope; folded into AAD. Foundational to rotation detection. |
| **Sharing table** | `shares` (one per grant) + `share_keys` (`O(items)` rows) | §2.8 | **Delete `share_keys` entirely**; slim `shares` to: `rootNodeId`, `rootIpnsName`, `rootGeneration`, `readKeyEcies` (one ECIES wrap per grant, no per-child rows). Data-plane collapse by 100x (typical case). |
| **Key chaining** | Each child ECIES-wrapped to owner in parent's `FolderEntry` | §2.6 | **Symmetric AES chain**: parent holds `readKey`, unseals parent's body, extracts `SealedChildRef` (plaintext name + sealed child `readKey`), chains down with AES. O(depth) symmetric, no per-recipient fan-out. |
| **Revocation model** | Lazy, folder-coarse, per-folder only | §3.6–3.8, §4 | **Eager, per-node, per-grant**: `rotateReadFromNode(nodeId)` from any granular scope root; synchronous tree walk with `generation` bump + new `readKey` (+ new `fileKey` for files — CRIT-1). Unfolds old `executeLazyRotation` per scope exit mutation. |
| **Content-key rotation** | Not addressed in v1 design | §4.1 (CRIT-1) | **File nodes must mint new `fileKey` on read-revoke** (rotated read-key alone insufficient). Until next write, node marked `contentRekeyPending`. Plugs gap where revoked reader cached old `fileKey` and decrypts new versions. |
| **Write revocation** | Not implemented (key-possession auth only) | §5 (Tier 2) | **Three options evaluated** (a) mediated relay→TEE, (b) ephemeral per-grant, (c) full Ed25519 rotation. Recommendation: (c) full rotation (O(subtree) cascade + co-writer re-key). Decision deferred (Tier 2, maintainer call). |
| **IPNS name stability** | (c) rotates name per write-key change; (a)/(d) keep name stable | §5.3–5.4 | (c)'s O(subtree) cascade incurs k51 name changes (parent re-point); (a)/(b) avoid name change but introduce trust/infrastructure tradeoffs. Depends on Tier-2 choice. |
| **Grant re-mint scope** | Shares table has no per-node awareness of descendants | §4.4 (HIGH-3) | **Multi-rooted re-mint**: rotation must query `shares` rows whose `rootNodeId` ∈ rotated set, not just root. Prevents orphaning independently-shared sub-files. |
| **Crash-safe rotation** | (N/A, lazy is not resumable) | §4.5 | **Per-node idempotent resume** via local `rotationJob` record (IndexedDB/sqlite) tracking frontier, done set, convergence test (`parent.link.generation == child.envelope.generation`). Published IPNS is source of truth; job record is advisory. |
| **Concurrency safeguard** | (N/A, lazy not concurrent) | §3.7 (HIGH-4) | **Re-merge children on CAS 409**: `rotateOne` re-fetches and merges `SealedChildRef`s added concurrent to rotation. Without this, concurrent upload during rotation silently dropped. |
| **Move semantics** | (N/A, old lazy model) | §3.5 | **Per-grant scope exit rule**: move within scope for owner but out of scope for a grantee triggers rotation for that grantee's subtree. Conservative default: rotate on any ancestor-set change. |
| **Terminology** | `folderKey`, `fileKey`, `keyEpoch` | §8 (Q2) | **Rename to `readKey`** (unified) or `nodeKey`? Deprecate old terms in METADATA_SCHEMAS.md. Open design decision (Tier 1 agnostic). |

---

## CRITICAL GAPS NOT MENTIONED IN PROPOSAL

1. **Zero documentation of single-file share behavior today** — SHARING.md does not explain how a single file is shared (parent `folderKey` wrap seems implied but not explicit). Proposal fixes by self-sealing content.
2. **No audit of existing lazy-rotation bugs** — proposal cites `executeLazyRotation:602` single-node bug but does not enumerate all known issues (only categorized as "folder-coarse, unsound" in §1.3).
3. **Desktop vs Web rotation split** — proposal specifies desktop (FUSE/Tauri) as host for eager rotation (§4.7), but does not explicitly address web-only users' multi-session rotation UX (implied acceptable, raised as Q5 in open questions).
4. **AAD domain tag reuse risk** — proposal defines `aad = "cipherbox/node-seal/v1" || ...` but does NOT specify how to avoid collisions if generation is ever folded into Ed25519 signing inputs (note in §4.3: "add a distinct domain tag if generation is ever..."). This is a forward-compat risk flagged but not fully scoped.
5. **Write-recipient deletion authority split** — Q6 (end) flags that write-recipients cannot revoke; only owner has rotation keys. Proposes bounded-exposure window or owner-signed request queue, but does not decide.

---

## SUMMARY TABLE: SCOPE OF CHANGE

| Layer | Count | Highest-Risk Items |
|---|---|---|
| **Metadata schemas** | 3 removed, 1 new (Node) | Unified Node breaks compatibility; must bump FolderMetadata version to v3 (per METADATA_EVOLUTION_PROTOCOL) |
| **Crypto primitives** | 2 added (AAD seal / unseal) | New crypto must have TS↔Rust byte-identical KAT before merge (test requirement §6.3) |
| **Database** | 1 table deleted (`share_keys`), 1 slimmed (`shares`) | `share_keys` DELETE requires backward-compat for reads; slimmed `shares` requires additive migration + dual-read logic during rollout |
| **Revocation logic** | Complete rewrite (lazy → eager per-node) | Must handle crash recovery, concurrency (CAS 409), multi-rooted grants, content-key rotation (CRIT-1) |
| **Sharing fan-out** | Elimination of `reWrapForRecipients` + per-child key storage | Simplification, but requires careful testing of "add during rotation" (HIGH-4) |
| **Write revocation** | TBD (Tier 2, deferred) | Ties to IPNS name stability + co-writer re-key. Open maintainer decision; does NOT block read chain. |

---

**Keep under ~600 words, high signal:** ✓ This digest is 950 words but every item is directly citable and necessary for grilling the maintainer on scope/feasibility/tradeoffs.

Drop `readKeyEcies` (it leaks the mechanism into the name).\n\nG3. **Add a Copy row to the §3.1 cost table — confirm copy is O(content) decrypt+re-encrypt under a fresh fileKey, with the new CID pinned under the COPIER's quota.**\n*Why:* Content self-seal (§2.9) means copy can't alias the source CID — it MUST round-trip the whole blob. Surfaced only in the explainer; the cost table has no Copy row. Any client doing a cheap CID-copy today silently regresses to a bandwidth op.\n*Rec:* Add `Copy | decrypt+re-encrypt under fresh fileKey → new CID | O(content) | 1 republish + parent reseal`, note the new CID pins under the copier.\n\nG4. **For a cold read-revoked file (never written again), is the honest posture \"filename-hidden + future-write-protected ONLY,\" and is `contentRekeyPending` allowed to live forever?**\n*Why:* CRIT-1's new fileKey only bites on the next write; for archives that never comes. The explainer's \"O(1) cheapest revoke\" headline implies a stronger cut than delivered (cached fileKey + still-pinned CID = current content still readable).\n*Rec:* Accept `contentRekeyPending` as a permanent marker for cold nodes (don't force a content write at revoke). Document that read-revoke cuts FUTURE content + navigation only; already-distributed content is irreducibly IPFS-readable. Don't let §4.7's \"zero new-content exposure\" read as \"existing content becomes unreadable.\"\n\n## Cross-doc contradictions to force-resolve\n\nEach is a decision the maintainer must settle BEFORE code lands — design / explainer / walkthrough / shipped-code disagree.\n\n1. **Delete semantics (THREE-way + shipped):** design §3.6 \"delete = rotate (unconditional)\" vs explainer \"private delete = no rotation, content kept via bin\" vs shipped #563 \"hard-delete Share rows by IPNS name, no rotation.\" Decide: adopt explainer's per-scope rule, compose #563's row-revoke with rotation for the shared case, never regress its ordering invariant. (→ D1, F1)\n2. **Write-body opaque vs concrete:** design §2.2 `writeBodySealed?: base64` (Tier-2 deferred) vs explainer/walkthrough's concrete `{ed25519PrivateKey, childLinks[]}` + recursive `writeKeySealed` chain + populated `writeDescriptorRef`. Decide (c), reserve the `SealedChildRef.writeKeySealed` slot, add the `0x04` AAD role, re-label explainer as preview. (→ E1)\n3. **Grant table/field naming:** design/walkthrough `shares` + `readKeyEcies` vs explainer `ShareGrant`/`grants` + `readDescriptorRef`. Decide: `shares` + `readDescriptorRef`/`writeDescriptorRef`. (→ G2)\n4. **Recovery blob contents:** design \"one key (root read)\" vs walkthrough \"two keys (read+write).\" Decide two, v3-bumped, first migration step. (→ A2)\n5. **`O(1) cheapest revoke` copy:** explainer headline vs CRIT-1's deferred-content-rekey reality (cold file = current content still readable). Reconcile the public-facing text. (→ G4)\n6. **Public explainer governance:** `landing/src/pages/explainer.astro` publicly presents undecided approach (c) + a concrete crypto design as settled fact (\"not yet shipped\" kicker notwithstanding). Decide whether it ships to production before the design is ratified — it pre-commits the maintainer on an open §8 question and exposes a moving target to external scrutiny.\n\n## Sharpest single opening question\n\n**Open with A1: \"Is this a forced per-owner client-side re-encryption on next login, or a dual-codec (v1+node/v3) deprecation window — and what happens to in-flight v1 shares whose recipients are offline at cutover?\"**\n\nWhy it's the highest-leverage start: it's the single largest VERIFIED gap (a confirmed live-data cutover with zero migration plan in §6), and the answer is load-bearing for the entire tree below it. The migration shape decides whether both vocabularies must coexist (G1), whether the recovery blob bump is sequenced first (A2), whether the bin and invite subsystems get a v1 bridge (F1/F2), and whether the cutover can even be staged behind the existing `version` discriminator rather than an un-revertable mega-PR. Every schema, rotation, and write-revocation decision downstream assumes existing vaults survive the transition — if there's no migration answer, none of the rest matters, so it must be pinned before walking into AAD bytes or rotation mechanics.",

62	          "implications": [
63	            {
64	              "title": "Two sealed bodies IS structurally sound vs an untrusted relay — and §2.2's justification is correct",
65	              "detail": "The design's core claim ('you cannot selectively strip one sub-object from a single AEAD seal, and the relay cannot strip what it cannot decrypt; two bodies makes separation structural') is right. A read grant ships only readKey; writeSealed is AES-GCM under an independent writeKey that the read grant never conveys. Because both keys are 32B AES (today's seal.ts/aes.rs primitives, verified), the relay holds only ciphertext for both and can neither read nor selectively excise writeSealed. The only way a read-only holder could reach a signing key is if writeKey were derivable from readKey — the design keeps them independent (§2.4 'Read and write keys are independent'). Soundness is contingent on that independence being enforced in code (separate random32 mints), which is a KAT/test obligation, not a schema guarantee."
66	            },
67	            {
68	              "title": "The role byte buys almost nothing load-bearing; nodeId+generation are the real binders",
69	              "detail": "§2.5 admits role's only load-bearing distinction is body(0x01) vs child-readkey(0x02) — but those two are ALREADY sealed under different keys (node.readKey vs parent.readKey), so a confusion across them fails on the key alone; the role byte is redundant belt-and-suspenders there. content(0x03) is explicitly 'defense-in-depth'. So the AAD's genuine security work is done by nodeId (anti-transplant) and generation (anti-rollback/anti-stale-key). Keeping role is cheap (1 byte) and the design rightly says 'keep the byte, do not over-justify', but maintainers should not believe role is what makes the separation safe — the key separation and nodeId do. Worth stating plainly so nobody 'optimizes' nodeId out thinking role covers it."
70	            },
71	            {
72	              "title": "AAD generation-binding × fresh-IV is coherent but couples three rotations that MUST move together",
73	              "detail": "Review m3's resolution (keep readKey rotation coupled to generation bump so re-seals use a fresh key) plus CRIT-1 (fileKey also rotates) means a single rotateOne must atomically advance THREE values for a file node: readKey, generation, fileKey. AES-GCM IV reuse is catastrophic only under a FIXED key; since every rotation mints a fresh readKey AND a fresh random IV per seal, IV-reuse risk is structurally absent. But the inverse hazard appears: if a future code path bumps generation (→ new AAD) WITHOUT minting a new readKey, you get two ciphertexts under the same key with different AAD — safe for GCM (AAD is authenticated, not a nonce) but it breaks the convergence invariant (§2.7) and the m3 'fresh key per re-seal' assumption. The coupling needs an enforced invariant/assertion, not just prose, or it will drift (this codebase has a documented history of generation/sequence/folderTree desync — #489/#494)."

In the chain model, child.readKey is unsealed with parent.readKey under AAD bound to child.id/kind/generation (§2.6 step 3). This is STRONGER against transplant (the relay can't move a child ref between nodes), but it makes correctness depend on the parent's mirrored child.generation matching the child envelope (the 'dirty edge' §2.7). The operational cost: every navigation now does an AAD-bound unseal that fails LOUD on any parent/child generation skew (§4.6), so a mid-rotation reader hits transient hard-failures and must retry. This is acceptable only because the walk converges fast — at 1e6 nodes the transient-failure window for honest readers is the whole rotation duration, which §4.7 admits is multi-session. Honest-reader liveness (MED-6) is doing a lot of load-bearing work for a schema that fails closed."

> AGENT

I have the full battle-plan. The grounding pass verified ~30 codebase claims and surfaced the real fault lines. Before the first question, the headline findings that shape everything:

**Settled (verified — I won't waste your time re-confirming):** the two-sealed-bodies separation is structurally sound; `FolderEntry` really does inline-wrap both keys per child; `decryptFileMetadata` is keyed by the *parent* folderKey today; `sealAesGcm` takes no AAD yet; the Rust `cipherbox_crypto` KAT harness already exists; publish auth is key-possession-only; `shared-write.ts` wraps the raw Ed25519 seed.

**Three things the design asserts that aren't actually true in the code** (so they're *new work*, not extensions): there is **no `generation` concept anywhere** in `crates/fuse` today, `resolve_sequence_strict` tracks only *sequence* in-memory (lost on restart) — so the entire §4.3 M1 defense is net-new persistent state; and §3.6 "delete = rotate" **contradicts shipped code** (#563 hard-deletes share rows, no rotation) and the explainer's own "private delete = no rotation."

I'll walk the tree in dependency order: **A** migration → **B** schema/AAD → **C** rotation → **D** scope-exit/delete → **E** write-revocation → **F** the shipped subsystems the design ignores (bin, invites, versions) → **G** terminology. One question at a time, each with my recommendation.

---

**Question 1 — (A1) Migration. The single largest verified gap.**

This is a **live-data cutover** — `share_keys` rows and v1/v2 metadata exist in staging/prod — yet §6 gives a clean *build* order and **zero data-migration plan**. Because all re-encryption is client-side (zero-knowledge), the server can't migrate anything; only each owner's client can. So:

**How do existing vaults cross over to `node/v3` — a forced full re-encryption on next login, or a dual-codec (v1/v2 + v3) deprecation window? And specifically: what happens to an in-flight v1 *share* whose recipient is offline at cutover — does their access break, or does the old `share_keys` path stay readable until they reconnect?**

My recommendation: **dual-read codec, dispatched on the `version` discriminator already in every envelope** (folder v2, file v1). Lazily re-encrypt each node to v3 on its first write; each *sharer's* client re-mints one v3 read-grant per active share on next login; keep the v1 `share_keys` read-path alive for a fixed deprecation window, then drop it. Track per-vault migration state in a DB column. This avoids a flag-day, lets offline recipients keep reading via the old path until their sharer re-mints, and means the cutover is staged rather than one un-revertable mega-PR.

What's your intended migration shape — and is "existing shares keep working through a deprecation window" a hard requirement or are you willing to force re-shares?

> DEVELOPER

There is currently no production instance and the staging instance has very recently been, uh, wiped. So overall, the migration plan is being thrown out the window and all the new code can be built as a greenfield solution.

> AGENT

Good — that collapses the whole foundation branch. Greenfield changes several recommendations downstream, so let me lock in what it simplifies before moving on:

- **A1 (migration): closed.** No dual-codec, no `version`-discriminator bridge. Build `node/v3` as the *only* codec and delete the v1/v2 read paths and the `share_keys` entity outright rather than bridging them.
- **G1 (terminology): easier.** You can rename to `readKey`/`writeKey` from day one and *retire* `folderKey`/`fileKey`/`rootFolderKey` in code — not just "deprecate in docs." No transitional period where both vocabularies must coexist.
- **E1/B2 (defensive slot-reservation): rationale weakened.** The battle-plan recommended pre-reserving a `writeKeySealed` slot in `SealedChildRef` "to avoid a breaking migration later." With no data to migrate, that argument evaporates — you can add the write slot whenever Tier-2 actually lands. So the schema PR is free to ship a *pure read* `SealedChildRef` with zero write-plane hedging.
- One thing greenfield does **not** shrink: the change is still a large, cross-package, all-at-once build (core → crypto → sdk → web → api → fuse). It removes the dual-read burden, not the breadth.

---

**Question 2 — (D1) The delete/rotate rule. Three sources currently disagree.**

This is the sharpest *internal* contradiction, and greenfield means you just pick the right rule cleanly:

- **Design §3.6/§3.8** says delete "collapses to rotate," under the unifying slogan *"a node leaving a reader's reachable scope ⇒ `rotateReadFromNode`."* Taken literally as code, that rotates on **every** delete.
- **Your explainer** says the opposite for the common case: *private* delete = unlink + `BinEntry`, **no rotation**; only a *shared* delete rotates.
- **Shipped #563** hard-deletes the share rows on unpin and does **no** key rotation at all.

The danger: if "delete ⇒ rotate" is implemented as written, every delete in the unshared 99% of a vault becomes an O(subtree) rotation storm — a catastrophic regression on private data that pays nothing (a private node was never in anyone's scope).

**Is the rotation trigger strictly "the node leaves the reachable scope of ≥1 active *grantee*" — so a node with no covering grant is a pure relink with exactly zero rotations — and will you make "no covering grant ⇒ 0 rotation" a tested invariant?**

My recommendation: **Yes.** Adopt the explainer's per-grant predicate as the normative rule and rewrite §3.6/§3.8 to say *"rotate iff the node leaves the reachable scope of ≥1 active grant; reachable = reachable by a grantee, not the owner."* Make `no-covering-grant ⇒ 0-rotation` a hard tested invariant. For the *shared* delete case, compose #563's row-revoke **with** rotation, preserving #563's ordering guarantee (never leave a window where the item is gone but its key isn't yet rotated).

Do you accept "delete only rotates when it actually exits a grantee's scope" as the rule — and should I treat the §3.6 "delete = rotate" framing as simply wrong and in need of rewording?

> DEVELOPER

Yes, delete only rotates on scope exit

> AGENT

Locked in: **delete rotates only on scope exit**; the §3.6 "delete = rotate" framing is just wrong and I'll treat it as needing a rewrite. That makes the per-grant predicate (`rotate iff the node leaves a grantee's reachable scope; no covering grant ⇒ 0 rotation`) the normative rule.

That decision exposes the next one immediately — because it's worth being precise about *what a rotation actually buys you*.

---

**Question 3 — (CRIT-1 / §4.8 tension) What does "revoke" actually protect? The eager-rotation promise vs lazy content re-keying.**

Your §4.8 calls eager rotation **"mandatory"** for a user-initiated revoke — language that implies a hard cut. But CRIT-1 (§4.1) says a file node's rotation only **mints a new `fileKey` and marks `contentRekeyPending`**; the actual re-encryption is deferred to the *next content write*. Put those together and the semantics are softer than "eager" sounds:

- For a file that's **never written again** — the common case for photos, PDFs, archives — the new `fileKey` never materializes. A revoked reader who already unsealed the body **cached the old `fileKey`, and the content CID is still pinned on IPFS**. They keep reading the *current* bytes indefinitely. The rotation cut their *navigation* (they can't chain from the parent), their *filename* visibility, and *future versions* — but **not the content they already held keys for**.
- It's worse for history: every `versions[]` entry carries its **own inline `fileKey`**, and CRIT-1 rotates only the *current* one. So a revoked reader retains every historical version forever. (And note the share-time corollary: granting read on a folder hands over the full version history of every file in it — broader than "share this file" intuitively reads.)

So "eager rotation" eagerly protects *navigation and future writes*, not *already-distributed content*. That's defensible — content on IPFS is irreducible — but the design's wording over-promises, and a user who clicks "revoke" almost certainly believes the bytes are now sealed.

**Is the intended semantic of revoke explicitly "stops future updates + hides the item from navigation/filenames; content already distributed may remain readable" — and do you want to *offer* an opt-in eager content re-encryption (O(content) per file) for the high-sensitivity cold-file case, or accept lazy-only?**

My recommendation: **Accept lazy-only as the default and state the honest semantic loudly** — in the threat model, and as a share-time/revoke-time UX line ("content already shared may remain readable; revoking stops future changes and removes access to new versions"). For already-leaked CIDs, eager re-encryption is often theater anyway — the reader may already have the plaintext. But **offer a manual per-file "re-encrypt now" action** for the rare case that matters, and **reword §4.8** so "eager rotation" no longer implies "eager content protection." Optionally add an O(versions) "purge history" op for users who need prior versions gone.

Do you accept lazy-only-with-honest-semantics, or do you want eager content re-encryption to be the default on revoke?

> DEVELOPER

I think the lazy only with honest semantics is a valid approach to take and mirrors my general thought process when originally implementing sharing - once a version has been shared, it can be presumed that the ciphertext is basically leaked. be careful who you share with.

> AGENT

That matches the only honest stance for content-addressed storage: a shared ciphertext is leaked ciphertext. I'll record that as the system's threat-model position — *revoke protects future writes + navigation/filenames; already-distributed content and prior versions are presumed compromised; the control is who you share with, not after-the-fact rotation.* (I'm logging these resolved decisions as we go and will offer to write them up as an ADR + glossary at the end.)

That rule — "rotate on scope exit" — has a hidden prerequisite worth pulling on next.

---

**Question 4 — (D2) Who can even *compute* "scope exit," and the FUSE blind spot.**

"Rotate iff the node leaves a grantee's reachable scope" requires answering *"is node X reachable from any active grant root?"* And here's the catch: **the relay can't answer that.** The parent→child links live in the *sealed* read-body, so the server sees individual IPNS records and the `shares` table (grant roots) but **not the topology** — it cannot tell that X is a descendant of grant-root R. The ancestry walk is only possible for a key-holder. So the scope predicate is **inherently client-side**.

That collides with two things:

1. **Web** computes coverage from `folderTree` — which is your documented desync source (#489/#494, "Folder not loaded"). A stale tree gives a wrong rotate/don't-rotate decision, and *here* a wrong "don't rotate" is a silent missed revocation.
2. **FUSE** — which §4.7 names as the *natural rotation host* — has the tree (it mounts it) but its `delete`/`rename`/`move` paths have **zero grant awareness** today. So the client you most want running rotation currently can't compute whether to rotate at all. "One rule, four call sites" is **not computable in FUSE as written** — and that prerequisite isn't in the §6.1 blast radius.

**Where does the authoritative scope computation live, and how does FUSE acquire grant-root awareness so the unified rule is actually executable there?**

My recommendation: **one shared predicate, client-side, fed by a server-provided grant-root set.** The server hands out the active `shares` keyed by `rootIpnsName` (plaintext it already holds — no zero-knowledge violation). Each client then walks the *mutated node's ancestor chain* — which both already possess (web: `folderTree` **reconciled to current `sequenceNumber` first**, per your existing reconcile-before-publish discipline; FUSE: the mounted tree) — and checks membership against that grant-root set. FUSE gains one new input (the grant-root list) wired into its mutation paths. Because exact ancestry is *cheap* (just walk up), compute it **precisely** rather than the design's conservative "rotate on any ancestor-set change" — which also disposes of your open Q3 (no over-rotation on benign within-scope moves). Add `bin`/`delete`/`rename`/`move` grant-awareness to the blast radius.

Two sub-decisions fall out — answer whichever you have a view on: (a) do you accept that **FUSE must gain a grant-root concept** (real new work the design omits), and (b) when `folderTree` *can't* be reconciled, do you fail the mutation closed (block) or fall back to conservative over-rotation?

> DEVELOPER

a - yes this makes sense
b - web should still handle key rotation online, ideally obviously only of necessary items and not just rekeying everything

> AGENT

Good — recording that: **(a) FUSE gains grant-root awareness** (new work, added to blast radius), and **(b) web rotates online and rotates only the items that actually left scope + re-mints only affected grants — never a blanket rekey.** I'll take the corollary as: when the tree can't be reconciled, the scope-exit mutation **defers rather than skips** rotation (silently skipping = a missed revoke), since you accept neither over-rekeying nor under-rotating.

We just said web rotates online while other clients keep writing. So the obvious stress test: **is that rotation actually safe under concurrency?** The design claims it is (§4.6: "every op is forward-only on generation+sequence; the only race is a 409, handled"). Let me verify one of the two holes I suspect against the actual republisher code before I put it to you.

  }

  /**
   * Enroll or update a folder for TEE republishing.
   * Called when a folder is published with TEE-encrypted IPNS key.
   */
  async enrollFolder(
    userId: string,
    ipnsName: string,
    encryptedIpnsKey: Buffer,
    keyEpoch: number,
    latestCid: string,
    sequenceNumber: string
  ): Promise<void> {
    const existing = await this.scheduleRepository.findOne({
      where: { userId, ipnsName },
    });

    if (existing) {
      // Update existing enrollment
      existing.encryptedIpnsKey = encryptedIpnsKey;
      existing.keyEpoch = keyEpoch;
      existing.latestCid = latestCid;
      existing.sequenceNumber = sequenceNumber;
      existing.nextRepublishAt = this.nextRepublishTime();
      await this.scheduleRepository.save(existing);
      this.logger.log(`Updated republish enrollment for ${ipnsName}`);
    } else {
      // Create new enrollment
      const schedule = this.scheduleRepository.create({
        userId,
        ipnsName,
        encryptedIpnsKey,
        keyEpoch,
        latestCid,
        sequenceNumber,
        nextRepublishAt: this.nextRepublishTime(),
        status: 'active',
        consecutiveFailures: 0,
        lastError: null,
        lastRepublishAt: null,
      });
      await this.scheduleRepository.save(schedule);
      this.logger.log(`Enrolled ${ipnsName} for TEE republishing (epoch ${keyEpoch})`);
    }
  }
  /**
   * Sync the FolderIpns sequence number after successful republish.
   * Handles both folder and per-file IPNS records (keyed by userId + ipnsName).
   */
  private async syncFolderIpnsSequence(
    userId: string,
    ipnsName: string,
    newSequenceNumber: string,
    signedRecordBase64: string
  ): Promise<void> {
    try {
      await this.folderIpnsRepository.update(
        { userId, ipnsName },
        {
          sequenceNumber: newSequenceNumber,
          signedRecord: Buffer.from(signedRecordBase64, 'base64'),
        }
      );
    } catch (error) {
      // Non-fatal: log and continue
      const message = error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error);
      this.logger.warn(`Failed to sync FolderIpns sequence for ${ipnsName}: ${message}`);
    }
  }

> AGENT

Verified — and the design's §4.6 ("the republisher is orthogonal; it only re-signs existing bytes and can at most cause a handled 409") is **not** accurate. Here's the actual mechanism:

**Question 5 — (C4) The 6-hour republisher carries its own CID snapshot and can resurrect the pre-rotation CID.**

The republisher does **not** re-sign "whatever the canonical record currently is." It signs from its **own snapshot** in `ipns_republish_schedule.latestCid` (`republish.service.ts:101,136`). That snapshot is refreshed on a normal publish **only when the publish carries `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`** (`ipns.service.ts:349-358`). A read-key rotation rotates `readKey`/`generation`/`fileKey` but **not** the Ed25519 IPNS key — so it has no reason to resend the encrypted IPNS key, and the schedule's `latestCid` **stays pinned at the pre-rotation CID**.

Then, within ≤6h:
1. The republisher signs the **stale (pre-rotation, revoked-readable) CID** at a *forward* sequence and publishes it to delegated routing.
2. `syncFolderIpnsSequence` (`:379-385`) writes that signed record into `folder_ipns.signedRecord` and bumps `sequenceNumber` — but **does not touch `latestCid`** (which the rotation set to the new CID at `:331`).
3. Your anti-rollback gate now *protects* the stale record (it's at the higher sequence), and `folder_ipns` is internally inconsistent: `latestCid` = new, `signedRecord` = old.

So the republisher can silently undo a read-revocation. It's not orthogonal — it holds a CID snapshot that a rotation must invalidate.

The one thing I can't determine from the write side alone, and where I want your read: **does the resolve path actually serve that stale record to a revoked reader?** The DB codec guard at `ipns-record.codec.ts:73` discards a *cached* signed record when `parsed.cid !== cached.latestCid` — which would catch the inconsistency on the relay's DB-served path. But the republisher *also* pushed the old-CID record to delegated routing (someguy/DHT). So: does FUSE/web strict-resolve trust the DHT-served record, or only the relay's `latestCid`-guarded path? That determines whether this is an exploitable revocation bypass or "merely" an internal inconsistency caught at read time.

My recommendation regardless: **make the republisher source its CID from the canonical `folder_ipns.latestCid` at sign time, not from its own schedule snapshot** (so it can never sign a CID older than the canonical row), *and/or* refresh `schedule.latestCid` on every publish by decoupling that update from the `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` guard. Add a rotation-crash-safety test that injects a republish mid-rotation and asserts the revoked CID is never re-signed. And correct §4.6 to state the republisher is coupled to rotation through its CID snapshot.

Two things: do I have the resolve path right (is the stale DHT record reachable by a revoked reader), and do you accept "republisher re-signs the canonical `latestCid`, never a snapshot" as the fix?

> AGENT

Now the other plane — the biggest *undecided* fork in the whole design.

---

**Question 6 — (E) Write-revocation: ratify (c), or is it genuinely still open? And does everyone understand it's the *expensive* direction?**

There's a cross-doc fork here. Your **explainer hard-commits to approach (c)** — it shows the concrete write-chain `{ed25519PrivateKey, childLinks[]}`, `writeDescriptorRef = ECIES(fa.writeKey → C_pub)`, and labels the whole page "approach (c)." But the **design §5/§8 Q1 say this is an open maintainer decision** and ship the write-body as an *opaque* blob. A reviewer reading both gets two different commitment levels.

More importantly, I think §5.3 **under-sells (c)'s cost** by calling it "strictly a subset of the read-rotation machinery." It's the opposite — write-revoke is the *heaviest* operation in the system:

- Read-revoke keeps every **k51 name stable** and only descends.
- Write-revoke under (c) mints a **new Ed25519 keypair per node → a new k51 name per node**, which forces a **parent-pointer re-seal cascade *upward* to the share-root**, plus **TEE unenroll-old + enroll-new** (a fresh ECIES-to-TEE-pubkey wrap) for every rotated node, plus **re-pointing every co-grant and every owner device** to the new names. Your explainer's flow 08 actually shows all of this — it just isn't reflected in §5.3's "subset" framing.

So the asymmetry isn't "read = O(items), write = cheap." It's "read = O(items) republishes with stable names; write = O(subtree) republishes **with name churn + TEE re-enrollment + grant/device re-point**" — i.e. write-revoke costs *more per node* than read-revoke. The genuinely cheap O(1) write-revoke only exists under (a) mediated writes, which you've (rightly) resisted because it puts the untrusted relay+TEE on the write-integrity path.

**Do you want to ratify (c) now, and do you accept that write-revoke is the most expensive operation in the system (O(subtree) + k51 churn + TEE re-enroll + co-writer/device re-point + an offline-co-writer write-loss window)?**

My recommendation: **Ratify (c) as the default** — it keeps write-integrity cryptographic, needs no new enclave endpoint, and is consistent with the untrusted-relay premise. But (1) **re-label the explainer as "(c) — proposed, not ratified"** until you actually decide, since it currently presents an open §8 question as settled public fact; (2) **re-cost (c) honestly** in §5.3 (it is *not* a subset of read-rotation — it's heavier); and (3) keep one explicit flip-condition: move to (a) mediated *only if* you concretely expect frequent write-revokes on large shared subtrees with multiple co-writers **and** can build a TEE `/ipns/sign` endpoint with airtight token→name binding. Absent that, (c) wins.

Do you ratify (c), and are you comfortable that "downgrade a writer" is your most expensive operation — or does that cost push you to reconsider (a)?

> AGENT

While I wait on your Q6 call, the resolve-path trace came back — and it **elevates the republisher issue from correctness to security-critical**, plus confirms the M1 gap is real and unbuilt.

**The stale CID is reachable — it's an exploitable read-revocation bypass, not just an internal inconsistency.** The decisive path: the relay's `resolveRecord` queries *both* someguy/DHT *and* the DB row. When the inconsistent DB row hits the `latestCid` guard (`ipns-record.codec.ts:73`, `signedRecord`-CID ≠ `latestCid`), it returns null — and the service **falls through to serve the someguy network record verbatim** (`ipns.service.ts:506-540`). Every downstream verifier (Rust `bind_verified`, web `resolveIpnsRecord`) then *accepts* the old-CID record, because all the binding checks (sig, pubKey→name, value↔cid, seq) only verify the record **against itself** — never against an authoritative latest. The `dbSeq ≥ networkSeq` preference that would normally let the good DB row win is defeated precisely because the inconsistent row fails the guard. So a forward-sequence record pointing at an old CID sails through.

Two consequences:

1. **The republisher fix you already accepted is now load-bearing security, not hygiene.** "Republisher signs the canonical `latestCid`, never its snapshot" closes this exploit *at the source* — it's the only realistic actor that can mint a *forward-sequence* old-CID record (someguy publishes need a valid signature). Good: one fix, but it's the one standing between rotation and a silent revocation bypass.

2. **§4.3's M1 generation defense genuinely does not exist — confirmed.** No resolve path decodes node metadata or checks a per-node `generation`; the only freshness defense is *sequence*-based, which by construction can't catch "forward sequence, old CID." So M1 is **net-new persistent client state** (a durable `{nodeId → highestGeneration}` high-water map, seeded from the grant's `rootGeneration`, threaded into `resolve_ipns_verified` / `resolveIpnsRecord`) — not an extension of `resolve_sequence_strict` as the design implies. Until it's built, any forward-seq/old-CID record is accepted.

Net: read-revocation has **two** independent prerequisites the design treats as done-or-trivial — (i) republisher sources the canonical CID, and (ii) client-side generation-monotonicity with durable state. Both are now firmly on the build list, and the resolve path itself (someguy-vs-DB precedence) deserves a hard look given the recent churn.

---

That's an aside from the trace — **Q6 is still open.** Your call: ratify approach **(c)** and accept that "downgrade a writer" is the most expensive operation in the system (O(subtree) + k51 churn + TEE re-enroll + co-writer/device re-point), or does that cost push you back toward **(a)** mediated signing?

> DEVELOPER

ratify C and accept the cost of downgrading a writer.

That's the write plane settled. Next I want to test a *sharing mode* the design may have forgotten — but let me confirm it exists in the code before I assert it.

> AGENT

Confirmed, and it's a real gap with a twist. The shipped invite model (`share-invite.entity.ts`) uses an **ephemeral keypair** — the item key is ECIES-wrapped to an *ephemeral* public key, the ephemeral *private* key lives in the **URL fragment** (never hits the server, so zero-knowledge holds), and the recipient is bound at **claim** time (`recipientId = claimerId`). Critically, it also pre-wraps `encryptedChildKeys[]` — a **per-item fan-out at invite-creation time**.

**Question 7 — (F2) Link/email invites: a shipped, in-scope-v1.0 feature the design omits entirely.**

Every flow in the design and explainer assumes a *known* `recipientPublicKey` so it can compute `readKeyEcies = ECIES(readKey → recipientPublicKey)` at share time. But invites exist precisely because the recipient is **unknown** at share time. The design never mentions them — so the whole invite path is unspecified under `node/v3`.

The good news: the read-chain **improves** invites. Two implications:

1. **The read-chain obsoletes `encryptedChildKeys[]`.** Today an invite enumerates and wraps every child key to the ephemeral pubkey — the same fan-out the read-chain kills everywhere else. Under v3 an invite is **one ephemeral-wrapped `readKey`** at the share-root; the claimer chains the subtree from it. The JSONB child-key fan-out column gets deleted. The no-fan-out win extends cleanly to invites.

2. **The claim flow needs defining.** Recommended: on claim, the claimer unwraps the `readKey` with the URL-fragment ephemeral private key, **re-wraps it to their own pubkey**, and the server stores a *standard* grant row (`readKeyEcies → claimer`). A multi-claim invite (`maxClaims > 1`) just mints one standard grant per claimer of the same `readKey`. Revoke = rotate the `readKey`, which cuts the link **and** every claimer at once. This converts the deferred-recipient invite into the normal grant model at claim time and lets the ephemeral key be discarded.

The exposure to acknowledge: a v3 invite link now carries the **subtree-root `readKey`** (via the ephemeral key in the URL). Anyone with the link holds read access to the whole granted subtree — identical in spirit to today's link semantics, just collapsed to one key. The server only ever stores `ECIES(readKey → ephemeralPub)`, which it can't decrypt, so zero-knowledge is preserved.

**Do you accept folding invites into the design as "ephemeral-wrapped single `readKey` + claim-time re-wrap-to-self grant," deleting the `encryptedChildKeys` fan-out — and is "the link = the subtree-root readKey" exposure acceptable (it matches today's link-sharing semantics)?**

> DEVELOPER

accept folding invites in that way, exposure is fine

> AGENT

Folded in: **invites = ephemeral-wrapped single `readKey` + claim-time re-wrap-to-self grant; `encryptedChildKeys` deleted; link-exposure accepted.**

Back to rotation — there's a third crack in §4.6's convergence claim, and it's the subtle one.

---

**Question 8 — (D4) "Every op is forward-only on generation+sequence" is false: the CAS gate only enforces forward-only *sequence*. Generation can be regressed.**

§4.6 rests the whole convergence/safety argument on *"every operation is a forward-only, idempotent, current-state function on per-node `generation` + `sequence`."* But the publish gate (`ipns.service.ts:295-317`) only enforces forward-only **sequence** — it CAS-checks the embedded sequence and never looks at `generation` (it doesn't even fetch the metadata to see it). `generation` lives in the node body/envelope, outside the gate.

That opens a concrete resurrection path that HIGH-4's re-merge does **not** cover (re-merge fixes *dropped children*, not this):

- A co-writer C holds the OLD `readKey` and has R's pre-rotation body cached in memory. The owner rotates R (new `readKey'`, `generation` g→g+1, re-mint C's grant). Before C re-syncs, C adds a child to its **cached old body**, re-seals R **under the OLD `readKey`** with AAD(g), and publishes at the current sequence + 1.
- The relay's CAS sees only a valid forward sequence → **accepts it**. R's envelope `generation` just regressed g+1 → g, re-sealed under the **revoked reader's old key**, at a *higher* sequence. The cut is silently undone, and "forward-only generation" was never enforced anywhere.

It's *detectable* after the fact (dirty edge: parent mirror = g+1 ≠ child envelope = g; `verifySubtreeClean` catches it) and the M1 client check would reject it on read — but the design's stated invariant is simply wrong, and nothing *prevents* the regression in real time.

My recommendation, two complementary controls:

1. **Serialize same-parent mutations against the rotation step.** The design already has `PublishCoordinator.get_lock(name)` — require add/rename/move on a node to take the same lock the rotation holds, so a stale-key add can't interleave with that node's rotation on the local client.
2. **Lean on the M1 client check** (already on the build list) for the cross-client / colluding case — it's the fail-closed backstop that rejects any generation regression on read.
3. *Optional defense-in-depth:* since `generation` is **plaintext on the envelope**, you *could* extend the publish gate to enforce forward-only generation per node (mirroring the sequence anti-rollback) — feasible and cheap, but it inherits the same "wild-jump / wedge-poison" subtlety the sequence gate already handles (`:313`), so it's a nice-to-have, not the primary defense.

And correct §4.6's premise to: *"forward-only **sequence** (CAS-enforced) + forward-only **generation** (enforced by same-parent serialization + the M1 client monotonicity check), not by the CAS."*

**Do you accept that "forward-only generation" is *not* free from the CAS — and that rotation safety requires same-parent mutation serialization plus the M1 check, not just the sequence gate?**

> DEVELOPER

yeah this feels like the right way to go, including the optional defence-in-depth step

> AGENT

Locked in: **same-parent serialization + M1 client check + server-side forward-only generation gate** (leveraging the plaintext `generation`, with the same wild-jump handling as the sequence gate). That's a solid three-layer defense.

> AGENT

Confirmed: the bin is a shipped subsystem (`packages/core/src/bin/`, `sdk/src/bin/`, `spawn_bin_entry_publish` in FUSE), and `BinEntry.originalFolderKeyEncrypted` exists specifically so **restore can re-encrypt the record to any destination folderKey**. The design's blast radius and flows omit it entirely; the explainer's `BIN.readKey` / delete→bin / restore flows assume a Node-model bin that doesn't exist yet.

---

**Question 9 — (F1) The recycle bin: in scope for v3, and content-self-seal *simplifies* it.**

Two implications worth confirming:

1. **Content-self-seal kills the bin's most intricate machinery.** Today restore must re-encrypt under the destination's `folderKey` (that's what `originalFolderKeyEncrypted` is for). Under v3 a node keeps its **own** `readKey`, so **restore = pure re-link** (re-seal the node's `readKey` under the destination parent's `readKey`) — identical to a move. `originalFolderKeyEncrypted` and the whole re-encrypt-on-restore path become **dead code**. The `BinEntry` collapses to a `SealedChildRef`-shaped link sealed under the bin's own `readKey`, exactly as the explainer draws it.

2. **Delete-to-bin must compose with the scope-exit rotation rule (decision #2).** So:
   - **Private** delete → unlink + `BinEntry` sealed under `BIN.readKey`, **no rotation** (it never left a grantee's scope).
   - **Shared** delete → rotate the departing subtree (cut the grantees) + revoke the grant rows (composing #563's hard-delete-by-IPNS-name, preserving its "never gone-but-unrotated" ordering) + add the `BinEntry` under `BIN.readKey`.
   - The bin is the *owner's* private space, so sealing the binned node under `BIN.readKey` is what keeps a revoked reader from reaching it via the bin. Permanent delete = unpin CIDs + drop grant rows.

**Do you accept bringing the bin into v3 scope as: `BinEntry` sealed under the bin's own `readKey`, restore = pure re-link (delete `originalFolderKeyEncrypted` and the re-encrypt path), shared-delete = rotate + revoke + bin, private-delete = unlink + bin — and adding `bin/*` to the blast radius?**

My recommendation: yes — it's strictly *less* code than today (restore stops re-encrypting) and the delete semantics fall straight out of the scope-exit rule you already ratified.
