Bin is in scope. That's 9 decisions settled, and we're into the schema/crypto details now. One of them is a conceptual correction worth its own moment, then I'll bundle the genuinely mechanical ones.

---

**Question 10 — (B2) What the AAD actually protects, and where its `generation` comes from.**

The design sells the AAD as transplant-proof: §2.6 says binding `nodeId` means *"a relay cannot transplant child-keys to another node."* That's **overstated**, and there's an ambiguity underneath it.

- The child-readkey AAD binds `childId‖child.kind‖child.generation‖role` — but **not `parentId`**. So a legitimate **move** re-seals the child's `readKeySealed` under the new parent's `readKey` with **byte-identical AAD**. Which means the AAD does *not* bind topology. What actually stops a relay from re-pointing a child to a different parent is that the relay **can't produce a valid `readKeySealed` under the new parent's `readKey`** — it lacks the key. So **topology is enforced by parent-key possession, not by the AAD.** The AAD's real job is narrower: prevent *stale-generation replay* and *cross-node-id confusion*. The §2.5/§2.6 wording should be corrected to say exactly that, so nobody trusts the AAD for a guarantee it doesn't provide.

- **Where does the AAD's `generation` come from on the read path?** §2.4 implies the node's own plaintext envelope; §2.6 step 3 says the child's generation **as mirrored in the parent's `SealedChildRef`**. Those disagree *precisely during rotation* (the dirty edge), and the choice decides whether a relay serving a stale child fails closed or is silently accepted.

My recommendation: **the reader's *expected* AAD generation comes from the parent's `SealedChildRef.generation` mirror** (the parent is reached through the signed-CID chain, so it's integrity-anchored) — and for a share-root, from the grant's `rootGeneration` (owner-vouched). The node's own *envelope* plaintext `generation` is used **only** for the M1 high-water monotonicity check and dirty-edge detection, never as the unseal key-material input. This makes a stale-child serve **fail closed**: the reader unwraps `child.readKey'` from the current parent (mirror = g+1), then tries to unseal the stale child body (sealed under the old key with AAD g) using the new key + AAD(g+1) → both key and AAD wrong → hard fail.

**Do you accept (a) re-wording the AAD's transplant claim to "prevents stale-generation replay + cross-node-id confusion; topology is enforced by parent-readKey possession," and (b) the rule that the unseal AAD's `generation` is sourced from the parent mirror / grant `rootGeneration`, with the envelope generation used only for monotonicity + dirty-edge detection?**

> DEVELOPER

accept both of these

> AGENT

Both accepted. AAD semantics corrected, generation-sourcing rule fixed.

We've now individually grilled the load-bearing decisions (migration, scope-exit, revoke semantics, republisher/M1, write-revoke cost, invites, concurrency, AAD semantics, bin). What remains are mechanical "ratify the obvious" items — I'll bundle them so we don't spend ten more rounds on byte tables. Two consolidated decisions, each with a recommendation; flag any sub-item you want to open up.

---

**Question 11 — Schema/crypto freeze (B1 + B3 + B4).** Ratify these as a package?

- **B1 — AAD byte encoding (blocks the KAT, so freeze it first).** `kind`: `folder=0x01, file=0x02, root=0x03`. `nodeId`: the raw **16 RFC-4122 bytes** (`uuid.as_bytes()`, canonical field order) — *not* a hash. `generation`: 4-byte big-endian. `role`: `{0x01 body, 0x02 child-readkey, 0x03 content}`, and **reserve `0x04 child-writekey`** for the ratified (c) write-chain so Tier-2 needs no AAD-version bump. Pin all of it as the first vector in the existing `crates/crypto/tests/cross_language.rs` KAT, asserted by `packages/crypto` too.

- **B3 — content cipher (a real choice, not just a confirm).** Today content is `GCM` *or* `CTR`, and CTR powers range reads on large files (`aes_ctr`). v3's content schema (§2.3) omits `encryptionMode` entirely. **Recommendation: keep `encryptionMode` in `content` *and* in each `VersionEntry`, retaining CTR** — dropping to GCM-only would regress large-file range reads. (If you'd rather simplify to GCM-only and accept that regression, that's the alternative — your call.)

- **B4 — `fileKeyEncrypted → content.fileKey` is a *semantic type change*, not a rename.** It goes from an ECIES hex *string* to a raw 32-byte key *inside the sealed body*, and the same flip applies to **every `VersionEntry`**. Document it as a type change in `METADATA_SCHEMAS.md` so nobody ships it as a string rename and creates a key-confusion bug.

My recommendation: accept the package as written (retain CTR for B3). Anything you want to pull out and discuss?

> DEVELOPER

definitely retain encryptionMode

> AGENT

Retained. B1/B3/B4 ratified.

---

**Question 12 — Terminology + the one missing cost row (G1 + G2 + G3).** Last bundle — ratify as a package?

- **G1 — key names.** Adopt **`readKey` / `writeKey`** (not `nodeKey` — it's ambiguous about read-vs-write). Add `readKey`, `writeKey`, `generation` to the CLAUDE.md terminology table, and add a **"Counters" sub-table** that pins the three distinct clocks people will conflate: `sequenceNumber` (bumps every publish), `keyEpoch` (TEE-pubkey rotation, write-plane), `generation` (per-node read-key rotation) — and ban "epoch"/"version" as synonyms for `generation`. Since it's greenfield, **fully retire** `folderKey`/`fileKey`/`rootFolderKey` from code, not just "deprecate in docs."

- **G2 — grant row naming.** Keep the table name **`shares`** (it's the existing `@Entity('shares')`, zero reason to churn it). Use **`readDescriptorRef` / `writeDescriptorRef`** for the wrapped-key fields — symmetric and mechanism-agnostic. Retire the design's `readKeyEcies` (it bakes the ECIES mechanism into the field name) and the explainer's `ShareGrant` table name. This resolves the design-vs-explainer naming fork.

- **G3 — add the missing Copy row to the §3.1 cost table.** Content self-seal means a copy **can't alias the source CID** — it must decrypt and **re-encrypt under a fresh `fileKey` → new CID**. So copy is **O(content)**, not a metadata-only op, and the new CID pins under the **copier's** quota. The cost table currently has no Copy row; any client assuming a cheap CID-alias copy silently regresses to a bandwidth op.

My recommendation: accept as written. Anything to pull out?

> DEVELOPER

that all makes sense - in terms of terminology, there's a table named folder_ipns which actually contains all the ipns records that are created by the system. I think this should be renamed.

> AGENT

Good catch — and it's a genuine domain-model smell, doubly so under the unified Node model: `folder_ipns` holds the IPNS records for **files, the vault root, the bin, and the vault-key blob** too, not just folders. The name lies about its contents, and "folder" is exactly the vocabulary the Node unification is retiring.

**Question 13 — Rename `folder_ipns`.**

My recommendation: **`ipns_records`** (entity `IpnsRecord`, `ipnsRecordRepository`). Rationale:
- It's accurate — the table is the canonical published state of *every* IPNS identity the system manages (name → `latestCid` + `sequenceNumber` + `signedRecord` + `publicKey` + `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` + `keyEpoch` + owner), regardless of node kind.
- It's terminology-aligned: your CLAUDE.md table already canonicalizes **`ipnsRecord`** for "the data structure" and explicitly tells us to **avoid "IPNS entry"** — so `ipns_entries` is out, and `ipns_records` is the on-vocabulary choice. (`node_ipns` is tempting but slightly wrong, since the vault-key blob's IPNS isn't a Node.)
- Greenfield makes it **free**: just name it correctly in the baseline schema. The rename cascades to the entity, the repository injection, and all `folderIpnsRepository` references — but no data migration.

Add `ipnsRecord`/`ipns_records` to the terminology table and mark `folder_ipns`/`folderIpns*` as a retired misnomer.

One **related** cleanup while you're in that table: the nullable `public_key` column is a known footgun (shared rows leave it null; two Phase-60 strict-resolve regressions traced to depending on it). The k51 `ipnsName` already encodes the Ed25519 public key — `publicKeyFromIpnsName` recovers it — so under v3 that column is **derivable and arguably removable**. Worth deciding in the same pass: keep it as a cache, or drop it and always recover from the name?

Confirm `ipns_records` (or name your preference), and tell me your call on the `public_key` column — and with that we've walked the whole grilling tree. I'd then like to capture everything: a **decision log + ADRs** (the write-revocation (c) call and the revocation-semantics stance are the two genuinely ADR-worthy ones), a **`CONTEXT.md` glossary** of the terms we pinned, and a **design-doc errata list** (the §3.6 delete-rule rewrite, §4.6 convergence correction, §5.3 write-revoke re-cost, §2.5/§2.6 AAD reword, plus the missing sections: migration-removed, invites, bin, copy row, and the FUSE/M1/republisher blast-radius additions).

333:      existing.publicKey = publicKey ? Buffer.from(publicKey) : existing.publicKey;
376:      signedRecord: Buffer.from(signedRecord),
377:      publicKey: publicKey ? Buffer.from(publicKey) : null,
461:    pubKey?: string;
478:        pubKey?: string;
509:        // pubKey (supplied from the validated publicKey column). The network record parsed
514:        // 60-05's enrich removal dropped — the strict client re-checks pubKey→name binding
```

> AGENT

Checked — and no, it's **not** the user's CipherBox publicKey. `folder_ipns.public_key` is the **raw 32-byte Ed25519 IPNS public key** for that name. The publish path proves it:

- `ipns.service.ts:72` — `publicKey must be a raw 32-byte Ed25519 public key` (rejects anything not 32 bytes).
- `ipns.service.ts:77` — `deriveIpnsName(publicKey) === ipnsName` is enforced; it's literally the key the k51 name derives from.

Your CipherBox user `publicKey` is a **secp256k1** key — 65 bytes, `0x04` + 128 hex (that's the `lookupUser` regex `/^0x04[0-9a-fA-F]{128}$/` in `shares.controller.ts`). Different curve, different length, different purpose. The *creator/owner* of the record is tracked separately by the **`userId`** column on the same row; `public_key` is purely the IPNS signing identity for the name.

So my earlier point holds: since the k51 `ipnsName` *is* the encoding of that Ed25519 key (`deriveIpnsName` / `publicKeyFromIpnsName` are inverses), the column is a **cache of a derivable value** — which is why nullable rows are safe to leave null and why depending on it caused those Phase-60 regressions.

My recommendation: **drop it and always recover the Ed25519 pubkey from the name** — it removes the null-row footgun for the cost of a cheap derivation on resolve. Keep it only if you've measured resolve as hot enough to want the cache. Your call.

---

=== domain-modeling format files ===
# ADR Format

ADRs live in `docs/adr/` and use sequential numbering: `0001-slug.md`, `0002-slug.md`, etc.

Create the `docs/adr/` directory lazily — only when the first ADR is needed.

## Template

```md
# {Short title of the decision}

{1-3 sentences: what's the context, what did we decide, and why.}
```

That's it. An ADR can be a single paragraph. The value is in recording *that* a decision was made and *why* — not in filling out sections.

## Optional sections

Only include these when they add genuine value. Most ADRs won't need them.

- **Status** frontmatter (`proposed | accepted | deprecated | superseded by ADR-NNNN`) — useful when decisions are revisited
- **Considered Options** — only when the rejected alternatives are worth remembering
- **Consequences** — only when non-obvious downstream effects need to be called out

## Numbering

Scan `docs/adr/` for the highest existing number and increment by one.

## When to offer an ADR

All three of these must be true:

1. **Hard to reverse** — the cost of changing your mind later is meaningful
2. **Surprising without context** — a future reader will look at the code and wonder "why on earth did they do it this way?"
3. **The result of a real trade-off** — there were genuine alternatives and you picked one for specific reasons

If a decision is easy to reverse, skip it — you'll just reverse it. If it's not surprising, nobody will wonder why. If there was no real alternative, there's nothing to record beyond "we did the obvious thing."

### What qualifies

- **Architectural shape.** "We're using a monorepo." "The write model is event-sourced, the read model is projected into Postgres."
- **Integration patterns between contexts.** "Ordering and Billing communicate via domain events, not synchronous HTTP."
- **Technology choices that carry lock-in.** Database, message bus, auth provider, deployment target. Not every library — just the ones that would take a quarter to swap out.
- **Boundary and scope decisions.** "Customer data is owned by the Customer context; other contexts reference it by ID only." The explicit no-s are as valuable as the yes-s.
- **Deliberate deviations from the obvious path.** "We're using manual SQL instead of an ORM because X." Anything where a reasonable reader would assume the opposite. These stop the next engineer from "fixing" something that was deliberate.
- **Constraints not visible in the code.** "We can't use AWS because of compliance requirements." "Response times must be under 200ms because of the partner API contract."
- **Rejected alternatives when the rejection is non-obvious.** If you considered GraphQL and picked REST for subtle reasons, record it — otherwise someone will suggest GraphQL again in six months.
========== CONTEXT-FORMAT ==========
# CONTEXT.md Format

## Structure

```md
# {Context Name}

{One or two sentence description of what this context is and why it exists.}

## Language

**Order**:
{A one or two sentence description of the term}
_Avoid_: Purchase, transaction

**Invoice**:
A request for payment sent to a customer after delivery.
_Avoid_: Bill, payment request

**Customer**:
A person or organization that places orders.
_Avoid_: Client, buyer, account
```

## Rules

- **Be opinionated.** When multiple words exist for the same concept, pick the best one and list the others under `_Avoid_`.
- **Keep definitions tight.** One or two sentences max. Define what it IS, not what it does.
- **Only include terms specific to this project's context.** General programming concepts (timeouts, error types, utility patterns) don't belong even if the project uses them extensively. Before adding a term, ask: is this a concept unique to this context, or a general programming concept? Only the former belongs.
- **Group terms under subheadings** when natural clusters emerge. If all terms belong to a single cohesive area, a flat list is fine.

## Single vs multi-context repos

**Single context (most repos):** One `CONTEXT.md` at the repo root.

**Multiple contexts:** A `CONTEXT-MAP.md` at the repo root lists the contexts, where they live, and how they relate to each other:

```md
# Context Map

## Contexts

- [Ordering](./src/ordering/CONTEXT.md) — receives and tracks customer orders
- [Billing](./src/billing/CONTEXT.md) — generates invoices and processes payments
- [Fulfillment](./src/fulfillment/CONTEXT.md) — manages warehouse picking and shipping

## Relationships

- **Ordering → Fulfillment**: Ordering emits `OrderPlaced` events; Fulfillment consumes them to start picking
- **Fulfillment → Billing**: Fulfillment emits `ShipmentDispatched` events; Billing consumes them to generate invoices
- **Ordering ↔ Billing**: Shared types for `CustomerId` and `Money`
```

The skill infers which structure applies:

- If `CONTEXT-MAP.md` exists, read it to find contexts
- If only a root `CONTEXT.md` exists, single context
- If neither exists, create a root `CONTEXT.md` lazily when the first term is resolved

When multiple contexts exist, infer which one the current topic relates to. If unclear, ask.
========== existing docs/adr ? ==========
(no docs/adr yet)
========== existing CONTEXT.md ? ==========
(no CONTEXT.md yet)
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box/docs/adr/0001-write-revocation-full-ed25519-rotation.md",
  "content": "---\nstatus: accepted\ndate: 2026-06-26\n---\n\n# Write-revocation uses full Ed25519 rotation (approach c)\n\nRevoking a write delegate must make the signing key they have already cached unusable. Because the relay is untrusted and IPNS publish authorization is key-possession only (`apps/api/src/ipns/ipns.service.ts` performs no ownership/share check), the only way to deny a holder's future writes is to change the signing key itself. We rotate the affected subtree's Ed25519 IPNS keypair(s) — approach (c) — accepting that this changes each node's k51 `ipnsName`, cascades a parent-pointer re-seal up to the share root, re-enrolls the new names with the TEE, and re-points every surviving co-grant and owner device.\n\n## Considered options\n\n- **(a) Mediated relay → TEE signing** — rejected as the default. It turns the untrusted relay into a confused-deputy signing oracle (a token-validation bug, SSRF, or auth bypass would forge IPNS records under the owner's identity), requires a new synchronous `/ipns/sign` enclave endpoint with airtight token-to-name verification, and couples write-time to TEE/relay liveness. It is, however, the only option that cheaply serializes the IPNS sequence race and yields an O(1) write-revoke, so it remains the documented flip target.\n- **(b) Per-grant ephemeral subkey** — dominated by (c): same k51-name break and subtree cascade, plus an extra key indirection, with no compensating benefit (all writers to one mutable node share a single IPNS identity).\n- **(d) Hybrid (owner self-signs, delegated writes mediated)** — rejected: it does not serialize the sequence race (two signing paths contend on one per-row counter) and still drags the mediated trust base in for the delegated subset.\n\n## Consequences\n\nWrite-revocation is the most expensive operation in the system: O(subtree) republishes + a fresh keypair and k51 name per node + a parent re-point cascade to the share root + TEE unenroll/re-enroll (a fresh ECIES-to-TEE-pubkey wrap per node) + re-mint of all surviving co-grants and owner-device pointers + a co-writer re-key. A co-writer offline during rotation cannot write until they re-fetch the re-wrapped key. The read schema is unchanged — the write-body is sealed under an independent `writeKey` the read grant never conveys — so this decision did not affect the read chain.\n\nFlip to (a) only if all three hold: frequent write-revokes on large shared subtrees become a measured cost; a trustworthy TEE sign-endpoint with token-to-name binding can be delivered; and write-time TEE/relay liveness coupling is acceptable.\n"
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box/docs/adr/0002-read-revocation-protects-future-content-only.md",
  "content": "---\nstatus: accepted\ndate: 2026-06-26\n---\n\n# Read-revocation protects future writes and navigation, not already-distributed content\n\nContent is encrypted client-side and stored content-addressed on IPFS. Once a reader has held a node's `readKey` and seen a content CID, any IPFS node serves that ciphertext indefinitely, and the reader may already hold the plaintext. We therefore define read-revocation to rotate `readKey` + `generation` (and mint a fresh `fileKey` for files, applied lazily on the next content write — the `contentRekeyPending` marker), which cuts the revoked party's navigation (chaining from the parent), filename visibility, and access to future versions — but we do **not** eagerly re-encrypt already-published content, and we do not touch prior versions. The threat-model stance is explicit: a shared ciphertext is presumed leaked; the control is who you share with, not after-the-fact rotation.\n\n## Why\n\nEagerly re-encrypting an entire subtree on every revoke is brutal at scale and, for already-distributed CIDs, usually theatre — the revoked reader may already have the bytes. Lazy content re-keying with honest semantics is the only coherent stance for content-addressed storage.\n\n## Consequences\n\nGranting read on a folder hands over the full version history of every file in it (each `VersionEntry` carries its own inline `fileKey`). Revoke and share UX must state plainly that content already shared may remain readable, and that revocation stops future changes and removes access to new versions. For high-sensitivity cases, offer an opt-in per-file \"re-encrypt now\" and an O(versions) \"purge history\" operation. The design's \"eager rotation\" (§4.8) therefore means an eager cut of navigation and future writes, not eager content protection — wording corrected in the design amendments.\n"
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box/CONTEXT.md",
  "content": "# CipherBox\n\nA zero-knowledge encrypted IPFS/IPNS vault. The server is an untrusted relay that never sees plaintext or any unwrapped key. This glossary pins the vocabulary for the metadata, key-chaining, and sharing model (the `node/v3` design). It complements — does not replace — the terminology table in `CLAUDE.md`.\n\n## Keys\n\n**readKey**:\nA per-node 32-byte AES key that seals a node's read-body (its children and, for a file, its content). Chained parent-to-child: a parent seals each child's `readKey` under its own. Holding a node's `readKey` unwraps its whole subtree with symmetric AES.\n_Avoid_: folderKey, fileKey, rootFolderKey, nodeKey\n\n**writeKey**:\nA per-node 32-byte AES key that seals a node's write-body (the Ed25519 signing material). A read grant never conveys it, so a read-only holder can never reach a signing key.\n_Avoid_: (none)\n\n## Counters\n\nThree distinct clocks; never conflate them.\n\n**generation**:\nA per-node `u32` that bumps only on a read-key rotation (revocation / scope exit). It is the AAD epoch and the rotation convergence witness; authoritative only on the child's own published envelope.\n_Avoid_: epoch, version (for this concept)\n\n**keyEpoch**:\nThe TEE public-key rotation counter (write-plane). Unrelated to read-key rotation.\n_Avoid_: generation, bare \"epoch\"\n\n**sequenceNumber**:\nThe IPNS record counter that bumps on every publish; enforced forward-only by the relay's CAS gate.\n_Avoid_: generation, \"seq\" in prose\n\n## Metadata model\n\n**Node**:\nThe single metadata object for a folder, file, or vault root, discriminated by `kind`. Carries an independently sealed read-body and write-body.\n_Avoid_: FolderMetadata, FileMetadata, FilePointer, FolderEntry (all v2-legacy, retired)\n\n**SealedChildRef**:\nA parent's link to one child, living inside the parent's read-body: the child's name, `ipnsName`, `generation` mirror, and the child's `readKey` sealed under the parent's `readKey`.\n_Avoid_: FolderChild, FolderEntry\n\n**ipnsRecord**:\nThe signed IPNS data structure (name to CID, sequence, signature) and the row that stores it.\n_Avoid_: \"IPNS entry\"; the table is `ipns_records`, not `folder_ipns`\n\n## Sharing\n\n**grant**:\nOne row in the `shares` table conveying read or write access to a share-root node — the wrapped key(s) for a single recipient. The only data-plane DB residue of a share.\n_Avoid_: ShareGrant (type name), share_keys (the deleted per-item table)\n\n**readDescriptorRef / writeDescriptorRef**:\nThe ECIES-wrapped `readKey` / `writeKey` on a grant row, sealed to the recipient's secp256k1 public key.\n_Avoid_: readKeyEcies, encryptedKey\n\n**scope exit**:\nThe condition where a node leaves the reachable scope of an active grantee. The only trigger for a read-key rotation; a node with no covering grant rotates never.\n_Avoid_: (none)\n\n**bin**:\nThe owner's recoverable-delete space. A deleted node is re-linked into it as a `BinEntry` sealed under the bin's own `readKey`, rather than purged.\n_Avoid_: trash, recycle bin (in code identifiers)\n\n**invite**:\nA claimable share to an as-yet-unknown recipient: the share-root `readKey` wrapped to an ephemeral key whose private half travels in the link, re-wrapped to the claimer's key on claim.\n_Avoid_: \"link share\" (in code identifiers — use invite)\n"
}

{

  "content": "# Read Key-Chaining Design — Amendments\n\nAmendments to `2026-06-26-sharing-read-keychaining-design.md`, produced from a grilling session with the maintainer on 2026-06-26. These supersede the cited sections of the design doc where they conflict. Treat this file as the authoritative delta until the base design doc is rewritten to absorb it.\n\nCross-references: [`docs/adr/0001-write-revocation-full-ed25519-rotation.md`](../../docs/adr/0001-write-revocation-full-ed25519-rotation.md), [`docs/adr/0002-read-revocation-protects-future-content-only.md`](../../docs/adr/0002-read-revocation-protects-future-content-only.md), and the root [`CONTEXT.md`](../../CONTEXT.md) glossary.\n\n## Decisions ratified\n\n1. **Greenfield.** No production instance; staging was wiped. No data migration, no dual-codec, no `version`-discriminator bridge. `node/v3` is the only codec; delete the v1/v2 read paths and the `share_keys` entity outright.\n2. **Delete/move/rename rotate only on scope exit.** A node with no covering grant is a pure relink — zero rotations. The §3.6 \"delete = rotate\" framing is wrong.\n3. **Revocation is lazy + honest** (ADR 0002). Revoke protects future writes + navigation + filenames; already-distributed content and prior versions are presumed leaked.\n4. **Scope computation is client-side**; FUSE gains grant-root awareness; the relay supplies the grant-root set; web rotates online and rotates only the items that left scope; reconcile-then-rotate, defer if the tree can't be reconciled.\n5. **Republisher signs the canonical `latestCid`, never its own snapshot** (security-critical). The resolve someguy-vs-DB precedence is a confirmed read-revocation bypass vector.\n6. **Write-revocation = approach (c)** full Ed25519 rotation (ADR 0001); it is the most expensive operation in the system.\n7. **Invites folded in** as an ephemeral-wrapped single `readKey` + claim-time re-wrap-to-self; delete the `encryptedChildKeys` fan-out.\n8. **`generation` is not forward-protected by the CAS gate**; safety needs same-parent serialization + the M1 client check + a server-side generation gate.\n9. **AAD does not enforce topology**; its `generation` is sourced from the parent mirror / grant `rootGeneration`.\n10. **Content keeps `encryptionMode`** (GCM/CTR) per node and per version.\n11. **Bin is in scope** and gets simpler under content self-seal.\n12. **Terminology pinned** (`readKey`/`writeKey`, the three counters, `shares` + descriptor refs, `ipns_records`).\n13. **Drop `folder_ipns.public_key`**; recover the Ed25519 pubkey from the k51 name.\n\n## §1 / §6 — Scope is greenfield (migration removed)\n\nThere is no production instance and staging was wiped, so the design's silent live-data-cutover assumption is void. Remove any migration framing. Build `node/v3` as the sole codec; delete v1/v2 read paths and the `share_keys` entity rather than bridging them. The vault-key recovery blob is re-designed (not migrated) to carry **two** keys — `ECIES(rootReadKey)` + `ECIES(rootWriteKey)` — since the root Node has both a read and a write key. Terminology can be renamed cleanly in code from day one (no transitional coexistence).\n\n## §3.6 / §3.8 — Delete rule rewrite (scope-exit only)\n\nReplace \"delete collapses to rotate\" with the per-grant predicate, stated as a tested invariant:\n\n> Rotate iff the node leaves the reachable scope of at least one active grant; \"reachable\" means reachable by a grantee, not the owner. A node with no covering grant is a pure relink (zero rotations).\n\n\"No covering grant ⇒ 0 rotation\" must be a hard test. Taken literally, the old wording would rotate on every private delete — an O(subtree) storm over the unshared 99% of a vault. For the shared case, compose the shipped `revoke-for-items` row-revoke (#563) **with** rotation, preserving its ordering invariant (never a window where the item is gone but its key is not yet rotated).\n\n## §3.5 / §4 — Scope computation and the FUSE blind spot\n\nThe scope predicate (\"is node X reachable from any active grant root?\") is **inherently client-side** — the relay cannot answer it, because parent-to-child links live in the sealed read-body and only a key-holder can walk ancestry. Amendments:\n\n- The relay supplies the active grant-root set (`shares` keyed by `rootIpnsName` — plaintext it already holds). The client walks the mutated node's ancestor chain against that set.\n- Web computes coverage from `folderTree` **reconciled to the current `sequenceNumber` first** (per the existing reconcile-before-publish discipline). A wrong \"don't rotate\" is a silent missed revoke, so when the tree cannot be reconciled the mutation **defers** rather than skips rotation.\n- **FUSE must gain a grant-root concept** in its `delete`/`rename`/`move` paths (new work; add to the blast radius). It already holds the mounted tree, so ancestry is cheap — compute exact per-grant scope rather than the design's conservative \"rotate on any ancestor-set change.\" This disposes of open Q3 (no over-rotation on benign within-scope moves).\n\n## §4.1 / §4.8 — Revocation semantics (lazy content rekey)\n\nSee ADR 0002. Reword §4.8: \"eager rotation\" means eager cut of navigation + future writes, **not** eager content protection. A file rotated on revoke gets a fresh `fileKey` only on its next content write (`contentRekeyPending`); a cold file that is never rewritten keeps its old `fileKey` valid, and the still-pinned CID remains decryptable by anyone who held the key. Every revoke flow must carry the caveat that already-distributed content and all prior versions stay readable. Optionally offer per-file \"re-encrypt now\" and O(versions) \"purge history\".\n\n## §4.6 — Convergence claim corrections\n\n§4.6's \"every op is a forward-only function on generation + sequence\" is **false**: the CAS gate enforces forward-only **sequence** only; `generation` lives in the body/envelope and is not gated. A stale-key holder can republish a cached pre-rotation body (re-sealed under the old `readKey`) at a forward sequence, regressing `generation` and silently undoing the cut. HIGH-4's re-merge covers dropped children, not this. Corrected invariant and controls:\n\n- **Same-parent serialization.** Add/rename/move on a node take the same `PublishCoordinator.get_lock(name)` the rotation holds, so a stale-key add cannot interleave with that node's rotation locally.\n- **M1 client check** (below) is the fail-closed backstop for the cross-client / colluding-relay case.\n- **Server-side generation gate (ratified, defence-in-depth).** Because `generation` is plaintext on the published envelope, extend the publish gate to enforce forward-only generation per node, mirroring the sequence anti-rollback and its wild-jump / wedge-poison handling (`ipns.service.ts:313`).\n\nRestate the invariant as: forward-only **sequence** (CAS-enforced) + forward-only **generation** (same-parent serialization + M1 client check + server-side gate), not by the CAS alone.\n\n## §4.3 — M1 generation monotonicity is net-new durable client state\n\nConfirmed against code: **no resolve path enforces a per-node `generation` check today.** `resolve_sequence_strict` (`crates/fuse/src/publish.rs:140`) tracks only `sequence`, in-memory, lost on restart; `VerifiedResolve` exposes `{cid, sequence_number}` and never decodes node metadata; web `resolveIpnsRecord` performs the same sequence-only checks. So §4.3's M1 defence is **new work**, not an extension:\n\n- Persist `{nodeId → highestGeneration}` durably (IndexedDB / sqlite, beside the sequence cache), seeded from the grant's `rootGeneration` (owner-vouched floor).\n- Thread it into `resolve_ipns_verified` (Rust) and `resolveIpnsRecord` (web); fail closed on generation regression. On a first-ever resolve with no high-water mark, cross-check the envelope generation against the parent's `SealedChildRef.generation` mirror.\n- Document the irreducible residual: a colluding relay can serve a victim a self-consistent OLD whole-subtree snapshot if it never lets them see any newer node (no signed generation closes this).\n\n## §4.6 — Republisher / resolve interaction (security-critical)\n\nThe 6-hour TEE republisher is **not** orthogonal to rotation. It signs from its own `ipns_republish_schedule.latestCid` snapshot (`republish.service.ts:101,136`), which is refreshed on publish only when `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` is supplied (`ipns.service.ts:349-358`). A read-key rotation does not rotate the Ed25519 key, so the snapshot stays at the pre-rotation CID; the next republish re-signs the **stale (revoked-readable) CID at a forward sequence**, and `syncFolderIpnsSequence` (`republish.service.ts:379-385`) writes it into `folder_ipns.signedRecord` without updating `latestCid`.\n\nConfirmed reachable: the relay's `resolveRecord` (`ipns.service.ts:456`) queries both someguy and the DB; when the inconsistent DB row trips the codec guard (`ipns-record.codec.ts:73`, `signedRecord`-CID ≠ `latestCid`) it returns null and the service falls through to serve the someguy network record verbatim (`ipns.service.ts:506-540`). Every downstream verifier accepts it because the old record is internally self-consistent. **This is an exploitable read-revocation bypass.**\n\nFix (ratified): the republisher sources its CID from the canonical `folder_ipns.latestCid` at sign time (never its own snapshot), and `schedule.latestCid` is refreshed on every publish (decouple from the `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` guard). Add a section to the design covering the rotation × republisher × resolve interaction (currently under-specified), and reconsider the someguy-vs-DB precedence in `resolveRecord` given recent churn.\n\n## §5 — Write-revocation ratified as (c)\n\nSee ADR 0001. Re-cost §5.3 honestly: (c) is **not** \"a subset of the read-rotation machinery\" — it is strictly heavier. Read-revoke keeps k51 names stable and descends; write-revoke under (c) mints a new keypair and k51 name per node, cascades parent re-points **upward** to the share root, re-enrolls/unenrolls the TEE per node, and re-points all co-grants + owner devices. The explainer's `(c)` framing and flow 08 are now correct; relabel nothing as \"preview\". Also fix the §5.4-vs-§8 inconsistency: full (a) uniquely serializes the sequence race (atomic sign + assign); the §8 \"no candidate solves the race, don't factor it in\" line wrongly erases (a)'s only real edge.\n\n## §2.2 / §2.6 — Write-body shape (resolved)\n\nApproach (c) is ratified, so the write-body is the structured recursive write chain the explainer shows: each node's write-body holds its Ed25519 signing material, and each parent write-body seals the child's `writeKey` (the explainer's `writeKeySealed: AES-GCM(child.writeKey, parent.writeKey)`). Reserve AAD `role = 0x04 child-writekey` for this. `SealedChildRef` stays read-only (one sealed field, `readKeySealed`); the write link lives in the parent write-body, not in `SealedChildRef`.\n\n## §2.5 / §2.6 — AAD semantics and byte encoding\n\n- **Transplant claim reworded.** The AAD does not bind `parentId`, and a legitimate move re-seals byte-identical AAD under a new parent — so the AAD does **not** enforce topology. State it as: the AAD prevents stale-generation replay and cross-node-id confusion; topology is enforced by parent-`readKey` possession.\n- **Generation source.** The reader's expected AAD `generation` comes from the parent's `SealedChildRef.generation` mirror (integrity-anchored via the signed CID chain), or, for a share-root, from the grant's `rootGeneration`. The node's own envelope plaintext `generation` is used only for the M1 high-water check and dirty-edge detection — never as unseal key-material input. This makes a stale-child serve fail closed.\n- **Byte encoding frozen** (blocks the KAT, so freeze first): `kind` = `folder 0x01 / file 0x02 / root 0x03`; `nodeId` = the raw 16 RFC-4122 bytes (`uuid.as_bytes()`, canonical field order), not a hash; `generation` = 4-byte big-endian; `role` = `{0x01 body, 0x02 child-readkey, 0x03 content, 0x04 child-writekey}`. Pin all of it as the first vector in `crates/crypto/tests/cross_language.rs`, asserted by `packages/crypto` too.\n\n## §2.3 / §2.9 — Content schema\n\nCarry `encryptionMode` (`'GCM' | 'CTR'`) in `content` **and** in each `VersionEntry` — CTR powers large-file range reads (`aes_ctr::decrypt_aes_ctr_range`); do not normalise to GCM-only. The `fileKeyEncrypted → content.fileKey` change is a **semantic type change** (ECIES hex string → raw 32-byte key inside the sealed body), applied to both content and every `VersionEntry`; document it as a type change in `METADATA_SCHEMAS.md`, not a rename.\n\n## New section — Invites (link/email sharing)\n\nInvites are shipped (`share_invites` table, `share-invite.service.ts`) and in-scope per `CLAUDE.md`, but the design omits them. Under `node/v3`:\n\n- An invite wraps the **single share-root `readKey`** to an ephemeral public key; the ephemeral private key travels in the URL fragment (never reaches the server — zero-knowledge holds). Delete the `encryptedChildKeys[]` fan-out (JSONB column) — the read chain obsoletes it.\n- On claim, the claimer unwraps `readKey` with the URL-fragment ephemeral private key, **re-wraps it to their own public key**, and the server stores a standard `shares` grant. A multi-claim invite mints one standard grant per claimer of the same `readKey`. Revoke = rotate the `readKey` (cuts the link and all claimers at once).\n- Accepted exposure: a v3 invite link carries the subtree-root `readKey`; anyone with the link reads the granted subtree — identical in spirit to today's link semantics.\n\n## New section — Bin (recoverable delete)\n\nThe bin is shipped (`packages/core/src/bin/*`, `sdk/src/bin/*`, `spawn_bin_entry_publish`) and absent from the design. Under `node/v3`:\n\n- A `BinEntry` becomes a `SealedChildRef`-shaped link sealed under the bin's own `readKey`. **Restore = pure re-link** (re-seal the node's `readKey` under the destination parent), identical to a move. `originalFolderKeyEncrypted` and the re-encrypt-on-restore path become dead code — delete them.\n- Private delete → unlink + `BinEntry`, no rotation. Shared delete → rotate the departing subtree + revoke the grant rows (composing #563) + `BinEntry`. Permanent delete → unpin CIDs + drop grant rows.\n- Add `bin/*` to the blast radius.\n\n## §3.1 — Cost table: add Copy\n\nContent self-seal means a copy cannot alias the source CID — it must decrypt and re-encrypt under a fresh `fileKey`, yielding a new CID. Add a row:\n\n> Copy | decrypt + re-encrypt under a fresh `fileKey` → new CID | no re-grant | no rotation | new node's own | O(content); new CID pins under the copier's quota\n\n## Terminology (see CONTEXT.md)\n\n- Adopt `readKey` / `writeKey` (not `nodeKey`). Add them and `generation` to the `CLAUDE.md` terminology table, with a Counters sub-table distinguishing `sequenceNumber` / `keyEpoch` / `generation`. Fully retire `folderKey` / `fileKey` / `rootFolderKey` from code (greenfield).\n- Grant row: keep table `shares`; use `readDescriptorRef` / `writeDescriptorRef`; retire `readKeyEcies` and the explainer's `ShareGrant` name.\n- Rename table `folder_ipns` → `ipns_records` (entity `IpnsRecord`, `ipnsRecordRepository`) — it holds the IPNS records for files, root, bin, and the vault-key blob too, not just folders. Free under greenfield.\n- Drop `folder_ipns.public_key`: it is the raw 32-byte Ed25519 IPNS pubkey (`ipns.service.ts:72-79` validates length 32 and `deriveIpnsName(pubkey) === ipnsName`), not the user's secp256k1 `publicKey` (the owner is tracked by `userId`). It is derivable from the k51 name via `publicKeyFromIpnsName`, so drop the nullable column and always recover from the name — removing the null-row footgun behind two Phase-60 regressions.\n\n## §6.1 — Blast-radius additions\n\n- FUSE grant-root awareness in `delete` / `rename` / `move`.\n- `bin/*` (core, sdk, FUSE `spawn_bin_entry_publish`).\n- M1 durable client state (high-water generation map) on both clients.\n- Republisher canonical-CID fix + `schedule.latestCid` refresh on every publish.\n- `resolveRecord` someguy-vs-DB precedence review.\n- `folder_ipns` → `ipns_records` rename (entity, repository, all references).\n\n## §6.3 — Test additions\n\n- Republisher re-signs a stale CID mid-rotation → assert the revoked CID is never re-signed and never served.\n- Stale-key add during rotation (generation regression) → assert it is rejected / re-converged.\n- M1 generation monotonicity → fail closed on a generation downgrade across resolves.\n- CTR content + a CTR version both decrypt under the v3 content schema.\n- Invite claim re-wraps the share-root `readKey` to the claimer; revoke (rotate) cuts the link and all claimers.\n- Bin restore is a pure re-link (no re-encrypt); shared delete rotates + revokes.\n\n## Citation corrections (verified against code)\n\n- The per-child ECIES unwrap the read chain replaces is at `crates/fuse/src/inode.rs:434,452` (also `:658,716`, `replay.rs:365`) — **not** `metadata.rs:428-453`, which is `spawn_bin_entry_publish`.\n- `spawn_file_meta_reencrypt` is defined at `metadata.rs:655`; callers are `write_ops/implementation/rename.rs:248` **and** `platform/windows/write_ops.rs:1182` (the WinFsp twin the design omits — killing it must touch both and round-trip the Windows CI gate).\n- The republisher lives at `apps/api/src/republish/republish.service.ts`, not under `ipns/`.\n"
}

450	  /**
451	   * Resolve an IPNS name to its current CID via delegated routing,
452	   * falling back to the DB-cached CID when delegated routing is unavailable
453	   * or when the record is not found in the DHT.
454	   * Returns null if the IPNS name is not found anywhere (404)
455	   */
456	  async resolveRecord(ipnsName: string): Promise<{
457	    cid: string;
458	    sequenceNumber: string;
459	    signatureV2?: string;
460	    data?: string;
461	    pubKey?: string;
462	  } | null> {
463	    const endTimer = this.metricsService.ipfsIpnsDuration.startTimer({
464	      operation: 'resolve',
465	    });
466	    let timerResult = 'success';
467	    let timerSource = 'network';
468	    const startTime = process.hrtime.bigint();
469	    let source = 'network';
470	    let resolveFound = false;
471	
472	    try {
473	      let result: {
474	        cid: string;
475	        sequenceNumber: string;
476	        signatureV2?: string;
477	        data?: string;
478	        pubKey?: string;
479	      } | null = null;
480	
481	      try {
482	        const recordBytes = await this.delegatedRouting.resolve(ipnsName);
483	        if (recordBytes) {
484	          result = await parseIpnsRecordBytes(recordBytes, this.logger);
485	          this.logger.debug(`IPNS name resolved successfully: ${ipnsName} -> ${result.cid}`);
486	        }
487	      } catch (error) {
488	        // Fall back to DB cache on BAD_GATEWAY (delegated routing failures)
489	        if (error instanceof HttpException && error.getStatus() === HttpStatus.BAD_GATEWAY) {
490	          this.logger.warn(`Delegated routing failed for ${ipnsName}, falling back to DB cache`);
491	          timerResult = 'error';
492	          source = 'db_cache';
493	        } else {
494	          timerResult = 'error';
495	          throw error;
496	        }
497	      }
498	
499	      // Always check DB cache — it's written synchronously during publish
500	      // and may be ahead of the network (delegated routing can serve stale records)
501	      const cached = await this.folderIpnsRepository.findOne({
502	        where: { ipnsName },
503	      });
504	      const cachedResult = await parseCachedRecord(cached, this.logger);
505	
506	      if (result && cachedResult) {
507	        // Both sources available. The DB-cached record is the authoritative, fully-signed
508	        // record the owner published — parseCachedRecord returns it complete, including the
509	        // pubKey (supplied from the validated publicKey column). The network record parsed
510	        // from delegated routing does NOT carry the signature fields a strict client needs
511	        // to verify, so prefer the DB record whenever it is at the same or a higher
512	        // sequence; only defer to the network record when it is strictly ahead (a newer
513	        // publish not yet in this DB). This restores the resolve-verifiability that Plan
514	        // 60-05's enrich removal dropped — the strict client re-checks pubKey→name binding
515	        // and the Ed25519 signature regardless.
516	        const networkSeq = BigInt(result.sequenceNumber);
517	        const dbSeq = BigInt(cachedResult.sequenceNumber);
518	        if (dbSeq >= networkSeq) {
519	          if (dbSeq > networkSeq) {
520	            this.logger.log(
521	              `DB cache has newer sequence (${dbSeq} > ${networkSeq}) for ${ipnsName}, using DB: ${cachedResult.cid}`
522	            );
523	            source = 'network_stale';
524	          } else {
525	            source = 'db_cache';
526	          }
527	          timerSource = 'db';
528	          resolveFound = true;
529	          return cachedResult;
530	        }
531	        timerSource = 'network';
532	        resolveFound = true;
533	        return result;
534	      }
535	
536	      if (result) {
537	        timerSource = 'network';
538	        resolveFound = true;
539	        return result;
540	      }
541	
542	      // Delegated routing returned null (404) or threw BAD_GATEWAY — try DB cache
543	      if (cachedResult) {
544	        this.logger.log(`Resolved ${ipnsName} from DB cache: ${cachedResult.cid}`);
545	        timerSource = 'db';
546	        source = 'db_cache';
547	        resolveFound = true;
548	        return cachedResult;
549	      }

1	import { HttpException, HttpStatus, Logger } from '@nestjs/common';
2	import { parseIpnsRecord, publicKeyFromIpnsName } from '@cipherbox/crypto';
3	import type { FolderIpns } from './entities/folder-ipns.entity';
4	
5	export interface IpnsRecordFields {
6	  cid: string;
7	  sequenceNumber: string;
8	  signatureV2?: string;
9	  data?: string;
10	  pubKey?: string;
11	}
12	
13	/**
14	 * Parse an IPNS record to extract CID and sequence number.
15	 * Backed by the `ipns` package via @cipherbox/crypto (parseIpnsRecord).
16	 */
17	export async function parseIpnsRecordBytes(
18	  recordBytes: Uint8Array,
19	  logger: Logger
20	): Promise<IpnsRecordFields> {
21	  try {
22	    const record = await parseIpnsRecord(recordBytes);
23	
24	    // Extract CID from the Value field (format: /ipfs/<cid>)
25	    const valuePath = record.value;
26	    const cidMatch = valuePath.match(/\/ipfs\/([a-zA-Z0-9]+)/);
27	    if (!cidMatch) {
28	      logger.error('Failed to extract CID from IPNS record value');
29	      throw new HttpException('Invalid IPNS record format', HttpStatus.BAD_GATEWAY);
30	    }
31	
32	    const cid = cidMatch[1];
33	    const sequenceNumber = String(record.sequence ?? 0n);
34	
35	    // Base64-encode signature fields if present
36	    const signatureV2 = record.signatureV2
37	      ? Buffer.from(record.signatureV2).toString('base64')
38	      : undefined;
39	    const data = record.data ? Buffer.from(record.data).toString('base64') : undefined;
40	    const pubKey = record.pubKey ? Buffer.from(record.pubKey).toString('base64') : undefined;
41	
42	    logger.debug(`Parsed IPNS record: cid=${cid}, sequenceNumber=${sequenceNumber}`);
43	    return { cid, sequenceNumber, signatureV2, data, pubKey };
44	  } catch (error) {
45	    if (error instanceof HttpException) {
46	      throw error;
47	    }
48	    logger.error(`Failed to parse IPNS record: ${error}`);
49	    throw new HttpException('Invalid IPNS record format', HttpStatus.BAD_GATEWAY);
50	  }
51	}
52	
53	export async function parseCachedRecord(
54	  cached: FolderIpns | null,
55	  logger: Logger
56	): Promise<IpnsRecordFields | null> {
57	  if (!cached?.latestCid) {
58	    return null;
59	  }
60	
61	  // D-06 (Plan 60-05): null signedRecord → return null (→ 404 to caller).
62	  // A row without a signed record cannot be served as authoritative; the client
63	  // cannot verify an unsigned CID and must not act on it.
64	  if (!cached.signedRecord) {
65	    return null;
66	  }
67	
68	  try {
69	    const parsed = await parseIpnsRecordBytes(cached.signedRecord, logger);
70	    // D-06 (Plan 60-05): discard on CID or sequence mismatch between the signed
71	    // record bytes and the DB columns — a mismatch means the row is inconsistent
72	    // and must not be served.  Warn and return null; the caller will 404.
73	    if (parsed.cid !== cached.latestCid) {
74	      logger.warn(
75	        `Cached signed record CID mismatch for ${cached.ipnsName}: signedRecord=${parsed.cid}, latestCid=${cached.latestCid} — discarding cached result`
76	      );
77	      return null;
78	    }
79	    // DB columns are authoritative for sequenceNumber (upsertFolderIpns always
80	    // increments it, while the embedded bytes reflect the client's pre-increment value).
81	    //
82	    // The Ed25519 IPNS record's pubKey is not re-extractable from the signed bytes by
83	    // parseIpnsRecordBytes, so supply it for the strict client. Precedence:
84	    //   1. the validated publicKey column — fast path, populated at publish time
85	    //      (publish enforced deriveIpnsName(publicKey) === ipnsName), then
86	    //   2. recover it from the ipnsName itself. For Ed25519 the k51... name encodes the
87	    //      public key, so it is the AUTHORITATIVE source and is always available even when
88	    //      the nullable publicKey column was never populated for this row — e.g.
89	    //      shared-folder records, whose column is null but whose signed record is valid.
90	    // The strict client re-checks deriveIpnsName(pubKey) === ipnsName, so deriving from the
91	    // name stays fail-closed.
92	    let pubKey =
93	      parsed.pubKey ?? (cached.publicKey ? cached.publicKey.toString('base64') : undefined);
94	    if (!pubKey) {
95	      try {
96	        pubKey = Buffer.from(publicKeyFromIpnsName(cached.ipnsName)).toString('base64');
97	      } catch (error) {
98	        // A malformed / non-Ed25519 name yields no recoverable key → unverifiable record.
99	        // Return null → the caller 404s (fail closed).
100	        const message = error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error);
101	        logger.warn(
102	          `Cached signed record for ${cached.ipnsName}: cannot recover pubKey from name (${message}) — discarding cached result`
103	        );
104	        return null;
105	      }
106	    }
107	    return { ...parsed, cid: cached.latestCid, sequenceNumber: cached.sequenceNumber, pubKey };
108	  } catch (error) {
109	    const message = error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error);
110	    logger.warn(`Failed to parse cached signed record for ${cached.ipnsName}: ${message}`);
111	    return null;
112	  }
113	}

1	/**
2	 * Republish Route
3	 *
4	 * POST /republish - Batch IPNS record signing endpoint.
5	 *
6	 * Receives encrypted IPNS private keys, decrypts with epoch-derived keys,
7	 * signs IPNS records, and returns signed records. Each entry is processed
8	 * independently -- one failure does not block others.
9	 *
10	 * SECURITY:
11	 * - IPNS private keys are zeroed immediately after signing
12	 * - No key material appears in logs or error messages
13	 * - Per-entry error handling with safe error messages
14	 */
15	
16	import { Router, type Request, type Response } from 'express';
17	import { decryptWithFallback, reEncryptForEpoch } from '../services/key-manager.js';
18	import { signIpnsRecord } from '../services/ipns-signer.js';
19	import { republishEntries } from '../middleware/metrics.js';
20	import { logger } from '../services/logger.js';
21	
22	const router = Router();
23	
24	/** Shape of each entry in the republish request */
25	interface RepublishEntry {
26	  encryptedIpnsKey: string; // base64-encoded ECIES ciphertext
27	  ipnsName: string;
28	  latestCid: string;
29	  sequenceNumber: string; // bigint as string
30	  currentEpoch: number;
31	  previousEpoch: number | null;
32	}
33	
34	/** Shape of each result in the republish response */
35	interface RepublishResult {
36	  ipnsName: string;
37	  success: boolean;
38	  signedRecord?: string; // base64-encoded marshaled IPNS record
39	  newSequenceNumber?: string;
40	  upgradedEncryptedKey?: string; // base64, present if re-encrypted for current epoch
41	  upgradedKeyEpoch?: number;
42	  error?: string;
43	}
44	
45	router.post('/republish', async (req: Request, res: Response) => {
46	  const { entries } = req.body as { entries: RepublishEntry[] };
47	
48	  if (!entries || !Array.isArray(entries)) {
49	    res.status(400).json({ error: 'Missing or invalid entries array' });
50	    return;
51	  }
52	
53	  const MAX_BATCH_SIZE = 100;
54	  if (entries.length > MAX_BATCH_SIZE) {
55	    res
56	      .status(400)
57	      .json({ error: `Batch too large: ${entries.length} entries (max ${MAX_BATCH_SIZE})` });
58	    return;
59	  }
60	
61	  const results: RepublishResult[] = [];
62	
63	  for (const entry of entries) {
64	    let ipnsPrivateKey: Uint8Array | null = null;
65	
66	    try {
67	      // 1. Decode encrypted IPNS key from base64
68	      const encryptedIpnsKey = new Uint8Array(Buffer.from(entry.encryptedIpnsKey, 'base64'));
69	
70	      // 2. Decrypt with epoch fallback
71	      const { ipnsPrivateKey: decryptedKey, usedEpoch } = await decryptWithFallback(
72	        encryptedIpnsKey,
73	        entry.currentEpoch,
74	        entry.previousEpoch
75	      );
76	      ipnsPrivateKey = decryptedKey;
77	
78	      // 3. Sign IPNS record with incremented sequence number
79	      const newSequenceNumber = BigInt(entry.sequenceNumber) + 1n;
80	      const signedRecord = await signIpnsRecord(ipnsPrivateKey, entry.latestCid, newSequenceNumber);
81	
82	      // 4. Check if re-encryption needed (used previous epoch, not current)
83	      let upgradedEncryptedKey: string | undefined;
84	      let upgradedKeyEpoch: number | undefined;
85	
86	      if (usedEpoch !== entry.currentEpoch) {
87	        const reEncrypted = await reEncryptForEpoch(ipnsPrivateKey, entry.currentEpoch);
88	        upgradedEncryptedKey = Buffer.from(reEncrypted).toString('base64');
89	        upgradedKeyEpoch = entry.currentEpoch;
90	      }
91	
92	      // 5. IMMEDIATELY zero the IPNS private key
93	      ipnsPrivateKey.fill(0);
94	      ipnsPrivateKey = null;
95	
96	      // 6. Build success result
97	      const result: RepublishResult = {
98	        ipnsName: entry.ipnsName,
99	        success: true,
100	        signedRecord: Buffer.from(signedRecord).toString('base64'),
101	        newSequenceNumber: newSequenceNumber.toString(),
102	      };
103	
104	      if (upgradedEncryptedKey) {
105	        result.upgradedEncryptedKey = upgradedEncryptedKey;
106	        result.upgradedKeyEpoch = upgradedKeyEpoch;
107	      }
108	
109	      results.push(result);
110	      republishEntries.inc({ result: 'success' });
111	    } catch (error) {
112	      // Ensure key is zeroed even on error
113	      if (ipnsPrivateKey) {
114	        ipnsPrivateKey.fill(0);
115	        ipnsPrivateKey = null;
116	      }
117	
118	      // Never include key material in error messages
119	      results.push({
120	        ipnsName: entry.ipnsName,
121	        success: false,
122	        error: error instanceof Error ? error.message : 'Unknown error',
123	      });
124	      republishEntries.inc({ result: 'failure' });
125	    }
126	  }
127	
128	  const successes = results.filter((r) => r.success).length;
129	
130	  logger.info('Republish batch complete', {
131	    total: entries.length,
132	    succeeded: successes,
133	    failed: entries.length - successes,
134	  });
135	
136	  res.json({ results });
137	});
138	
139	export default router;
140	
```

This is the crux of the user's explicit questions. The TEE route shows:
- **Where the TEE gets its data**: entirely from the relay's request body — `latestCid`, `sequenceNumber`, `encryptedIpnsKey` (`RepublishEntry`, lines 25-32). The TEE has no independent knowledge of the true latest CID or true sequence; it only proves key-possession.
- **Who increments the sequence**: the TEE, blindly — `newSequenceNumber = BigInt(entry.sequenceNumber) + 1n` (line 79). It signs whatever `(latestCid, claimedSeq+1)` the relay hands it.

The user's question "is it even necessary to increment the sequence on republish" hinges on whether the signer sets a fresh validity/EOL (the IPNS record-selection tiebreaker). Let me confirm.

1	/**
2	 * IPNS Record Signing Service
3	 *
4	 * Delegates to @cipherbox/core for IPNS record creation and marshaling.
5	 * TEE-specific: Uses 48-hour record lifetime (vs 24h default) to provide
6	 * comfortable margin with 6-hour republish interval.
7	 */
8	
9	import { createIpnsRecord, marshalIpnsRecord } from '@cipherbox/core';
10	
11	/** 48-hour IPNS record lifetime for TEE-republished records */
12	const TEE_RECORD_LIFETIME_MS = 48 * 60 * 60 * 1000;
13	
14	/**
15	 * Sign an IPNS record with an Ed25519 private key.
16	 *
17	 * Creates a V1+V2 compatible IPNS record pointing to the given CID,
18	 * with a 48-hour lifetime and the specified sequence number.
19	 *
20	 * @param ed25519PrivateKey - 32-byte Ed25519 private key (seed)
21	 * @param cid - IPFS CID string to point to (without /ipfs/ prefix)
22	 * @param sequenceNumber - Monotonically increasing sequence number
23	 * @returns Marshaled (protobuf-encoded) signed IPNS record bytes
24	 */
25	export async function signIpnsRecord(
26	  ed25519PrivateKey: Uint8Array,
27	  cid: string,
28	  sequenceNumber: bigint
29	): Promise<Uint8Array> {
30	  const record = await createIpnsRecord(
31	    ed25519PrivateKey,
32	    '/ipfs/' + cid,
33	    sequenceNumber,
34	    TEE_RECORD_LIFETIME_MS
35	  );
36	  return marshalIpnsRecord(record);
37	}
38

> AGENT

I now have the full republish + TEE signing flow grounded. Three facts pin down the rest of the grilling:

1. **The TEE is a blind signing oracle.** It signs whatever `(latestCid, sequenceNumber)` the untrusted relay puts in the request body (`republish.ts:25-32`), and it *itself* increments the sequence (`newSequenceNumber = BigInt(entry.sequenceNumber) + 1n`, `:79`). It proves key-possession and nothing else — it has no independent view of the true latest CID or true sequence.
2. **The signer always stamps a fresh 48h EOL** (`ipns-signer.ts:12,34`) — so the validity advances on every republish regardless of the sequence.
3. **The relay sources the CID+seq it hands the TEE from the republish-schedule snapshot** (`republish.service.ts:101-102`), and the client only ever publishes *through* the relay (`sdk-core/src/ipns/index.ts:93,138`), so the relay DB is written (CAS-gated) before anything reaches someguy.

That's enough to open. I'll walk the dependency tree one question at a time, each grounded so the model builds as we go. The root that the republish/TEE/sequence questions all hang off is the **resolve trust model**, so I'll start there.

---

## Q1 — Is the relay DB the *single canonical authority* for resolve, or can the network legitimately be ahead of it?

**What the code does today.** `resolveRecord` (`ipns.service.ts` ~`456-549`) queries someguy/delegated-routing first, then *always* reads the DB row through `parseCachedRecord`. The precedence (comment at `:507-515`) is: the DB record is "authoritative," and the relay returns the **network** record only when it is **strictly ahead** in sequence — justified as "a newer publish not yet in this DB."

**Why that justification doesn't hold here.** A record only legitimately reaches someguy *after* the relay's own `publish()` writes the DB row under the forward-only CAS gate, then pushes to someguy. The client never publishes directly to the DHT (`sdk-core/src/ipns/index.ts` goes through `ipnsControllerPublishRecord`/`Batch`), and there's no multi-instance/replication signal (sdk-core is stateless; one logical relay+DB). So for any record the relay *accepted*, **the DB is always at-or-ahead of the network.** The only producers of "network strictly ahead" are therefore:
- **(a)** the TEE republisher pushing a forward-seq record to someguy, and
- **(b)** an out-of-band publish straight to the DHT by anyone holding the Ed25519 key (a current or *revoked-but-cached* write-key holder) — which never passed the CAS gate.

In other words, the one branch that prefers the network is exactly the branch a rollback arrives on, and it bypasses the entire anti-rollback investment on the publish path.

**Recommendation.** Pin the resolve trust model in the design as: **the relay DB's `signedRecord` is the sole authority whenever it is present and valid; the network (someguy) is an availability-only fallback used solely for names the DB has no valid record for — never a sequence-based override.** Drop the `dbSeq >= networkSeq ? db : network` comparison entirely. A "network strictly ahead" condition stops being a normal case and becomes an alarm (log it; it means either a republisher/DB desync or an out-of-band publish). This closes both the republisher-stale-CID path and the direct-to-DHT forward-seq path in one move, and it costs nothing in a single-relay model because the legitimate "network ahead" case doesn't exist.

The one thing this leans on — that the network is never *legitimately* ahead — is true only while CipherBox stays single-logical-relay. If you ever foresee federated/multi-relay resolve (independent DBs sharing the DHT), the network-ahead case becomes real and we'd need a different defense (generation/M1 as the authority instead of sequence). 

**Do you accept "DB-canonical, network = availability-fallback-only" as the resolve trust model, or do you want to preserve a path where the network can override the DB?**

> DEVELOPER

Ideally I would much prefer the ipns network response to be the single source of truth, with a local fallback in case of transient network issues (which should not often happen if ipns records are published on a local dht node). This would reduce the propogation time of ipns records AFAIK.

The claims I need confirmed/refuted: publish writes the DB *before* the DHT push (→ DB is fresher, not slower); resolve queries someguy first and falls through to the network on a null cache; no client publishes directly to the DHT; IPNS record-selection's sequence/EOL tiebreak (load-bearing for the sequence-increment question); generation/M1 is absent from every resolve path; and the CAS gate is publish-only. It returns:\n//\n//   - -1 if a is older than b\n//   - 0 if a and b cannot be ordered (this doesn't mean that they are equal)\n//   - +1 if a is newer than b\n//\n// This function does not validate the records. The caller is responsible for\n// ensuring that the Records are valid by using [Validate].\nfunc compare(a, b *Record) (int, error) {\n\t// ... [signature version check] ...\n\tas, err := a.Sequence()\n\t// ...\n\tbs, err := b.Sequence()\n\tif as > bs {\n\t\treturn 1, nil\n\t} else if as < bs {\n\t\treturn -1, nil\n\t}\n\t// ... [on equal sequence, compare validity] ...\n\tat, err := a.Validity()\n\tbt, err := b.Validity()\n\tif at.After(bt) {\n\t\treturn 1, nil\n\t} else if bt.After(at) {\n\t\treturn -1, nil\n\t}\n\treturn 0, nil\n}"

126	          "quote": "// Anti-rollback: the incoming record's EMBEDDED sequence (covered by the\n    // signature, so tamper-evident) must not regress below the stored record's.\n    // Without this, anyone who observes the public IPNS record could replay an\n    // old-but-still-valid signed copy (no key needed) to roll the canonical row\n    // back to a stale CID — independent of the optional expectedSequenceNumber CAS.\n    // Equal sequences are allowed (idempotent re-publish of the current record).\n    // incomingParsed is set here when existing?.signedRecord is present (anti-rollback\n    // path) so S1 can reuse it below without a second parseIpnsRecord call.\n    let incomingParsed: { value: string; sequence: bigint } | null = null;\n    if (existing?.signedRecord) {\n      const [incoming, stored] = await Promise.all([\n        parseIpnsRecord(signedRecord),\n        parseIpnsRecord(existing.signedRecord),\n      ]);\n      if (incoming.sequence < stored.sequence) {\n        throw new ConflictException({\n          statusCode: 409,\n          message: 'IPNS record sequence regression rejected (rollback/replay)',\n          currentSequenceNumber: existing.sequenceNumber,\n        });\n      }\n      incomingParsed = incoming;\n    }"

131	          "quote": "export async function resolveIpnsRecord(\n  ipnsName: string,\n  ctx?: SdkContext\n): Promise<{ cid: string; sequenceNumber: bigint; signatureVerified: boolean } | null> {\n  return withPerf('ipns:resolve', async () => {\n    try {\n      const apiOptions = ctx?.axiosInstance ? > AGENT

All six claims came back **confirmed**, and the trace surfaced a live inconsistency the churn introduced. The load-bearing facts:

- **The DB is the *fresher* source, not the network.** `publishRecord` writes the DB synchronously *first*, then fires the someguy publish **fire-and-forget** — the comment is explicit: *"Save to DB first… DHT propagation via someguy takes ~10-30s per record… DB is the reliable source"* (`ipns.service.ts:106-144`). So in today's relay-mediated topology the DB leads the network by ~10-30s.
- **IPNS record-selection: higher sequence always wins; on *equal* sequence, later EOL wins** (verified against boxo/go-ipns `compare`). So a republish can refresh validity by re-signing the **same** sequence with a fresh EOL — no increment needed.
- **No `generation` check exists on *any* resolve/verify path** — relay, sdk-core, and Rust all verify sig + name-derivation + value↔cid + strict embedded-seq + expiry, but never generation. The anti-rollback authority today is purely the IPNS sequence number.
- **The CAS gate is publish-only.** A network record at `networkSeq > dbSeq` — even a wild jump (5→7) — is served by `resolveRecord` *without* re-passing the gate that `publishRecord` enforces (`:506-540` vs `:287-318`).

## Q1 resolved → the real principle: decouple *source-of-truth* from *anti-rollback authority*

Your network-as-source-of-truth ideal is reachable — but only if the rollback authority moves **off the sequence number and onto `generation` (M1)**. Here's why that's the unlock:

The IPFS network is permissionless. Its only anti-rollback is "higher sequence wins," and *anyone holding the Ed25519 key* can mint a forward-seq record over an old CID and PUT it. So any design that treats the network as authoritative on **sequence** inherits a rollback hole that the relay's strong publish-gate can't see. But `generation` only advances on read-key rotation, is AAD-bound, and is mirrored in the parent's `SealedChildRef` — so a client navigating from the parent **knows the expected generation** and rejects a lower-generation CID *regardless of which source served it.* Once generation is the authority, the source choice (DB / network / direct-DHT) becomes a pure latency+availability decision with **zero confidentiality consequence** — and your ideal flips on safely.

Two honest caveats on the ideal:
1. **Latency intuition is inverted in *today's* topology.** The DB is written 10-30s *ahead* of the DHT, and clients already resolve only through the relay. "Network-primary" here would *add* ~10-30s of staleness, not remove it. Your intuition is correct only in the *fully decentralized* topology (clients resolve the DHT directly, no shared relay DB) — which is a v2 architecture move, not a flag-flip.
2. **Generation protects cross-generation rollback (the confidentiality case), not within-generation freshness.** Two records at the same generation (ordinary edits, no rotation between) are ordered *only* by sequence. So network-primary still exposes "serve an older version of a file" as a **consistency/data-loss** risk — which is exactly what the shared, write-first, CAS-gated DB currently absorbs.

**Recommendation to ratify:** the design states *generation/M1 is the anti-rollback authority; resolve-source is a latency/availability layer beneath it.* Near-term: DB-canonical (it's both fresher and gated); "network strictly ahead of DB" stops being a normal branch and becomes an **alarm** + must independently pass the seq gate. Document network-primary as a post-M1 evolution gated on solving within-generation consistency. Push back if you'd weight it differently — otherwise I'll treat this as settled and it flows directly into the next question.

---

## Q2 — Should republish increment the sequence number at all?

This is your explicit question, and the verification gives a sharp, slightly surprising answer: **the relay already stopped incrementing on idempotent republish, but the TEE 6-hour republisher still does.**

- `publishRecord` now classifies `embeddedSeq === dbSeq` as an **idempotent republish** and *skips* the increment while still updating `latestCid`/`signedRecord` (`ipns.service.ts:306-317`, "D-09 / Pitfall 4").
- But the **TEE worker unconditionally does `newSequenceNumber = BigInt(entry.sequenceNumber) + 1n`** (`tee-worker/.../republish.ts:79`), and the republish service writes that back via `syncFolderIpnsSequence` — *bypassing* the gate. So every 6 hours the scheduler burns a sequence number on unchanged content.

That gratuitous increment is not just unnecessary (the EOL tiebreak already refreshes validity at the same seq) — it's **actively harmful**:
- It **races client writes for sequence numbers.** If the republisher bumps `dbSeq` N→N+1 underneath a client that still thinks `dbSeq=N`, the client's genuine new-CID publish at N+1 gets *misclassified as idempotent* (`embeddedSeq === dbSeq`), and the network ends up holding `sig(oldCID, N+1)` at the same sequence as the client's `sig(newCID, N+1)` — a same-seq fork resolved only by EOL timing.
- It widens the **replay-rollback window** (more validly-signed forward-seq copies of stale CIDs in circulation).

**Recommendation:** establish the invariant **"sequence advances if and only if the CID changes."** Republish — TEE and HTTP alike — re-signs the *current* sequence with a fresh 48h EOL; only a genuine content change consumes `dbSeq + 1`. Concretely: drop the `+ 1n` in the TEE signer (have the relay pass the exact sequence to sign, no implicit policy in the enclave), and route the scheduler's write through the same idempotent-republish path rather than `syncFolderIpnsSequence`. This makes the republisher purely a *validity refresher*, eliminates the seq-collision race, and keeps the enclave a pure signer.

**Do you accept "sequence advances iff CID changes; republish never increments," including moving the increment decision out of the TEE?**

> DEVELOPER

accept

> AGENT

Accepted — "sequence advances iff CID changes; republish never increments; increment policy leaves the enclave." I'll note one clean consequence before the next question: **that invariant alone closes the republisher-stale-CID rollback.** A re-signed stale CID stays at its old sequence, so any genuine client publish (higher sequence) dominates it — the rollback can't win on the network. Good.

> AGENT

Confirmed — and this is the heart of your "where does the TEE get its data" question. The current shape:

- **There are two tables holding the same record state.** `ipns_republish_schedule` carries its *own* `latestCid`, `sequenceNumber`, `encryptedIpnsKey`, `keyEpoch` (`republish-schedule.entity.ts:39-60`), separate from the canonical `folder_ipns` (→ `ipns_records`) row.
- **The TEE signs the *schedule's* snapshot.** `getDueEntries` reads the schedule table; the relay builds `RepublishEntry` from `entry.latestCid` / `entry.sequenceNumber` (`republish.service.ts:101-102`) and hands them to the enclave. So the TEE gets **100% of its signing inputs from the relay's request body**, sourced from the schedule snapshot — `encryptedIpnsKey`, `ipnsName`, `latestCid`, `sequenceNumber`, epoch (`republish.ts:25-32`).
- **The schedule's `latestCid` only refreshes on enrollment / key-rotation** (the `upsert` at `:214-247`, called when a publish carries `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`). A *normal content write* (new CID, key already enrolled) updates `folder_ipns.latestCid` but **not** the schedule → the schedule drifts stale.

Two things fall out of this, one conceptual and one a concrete bug:

**Conceptual — the TEE is a stateless blind signer, not an integrity oracle.** It decrypts the key in hardware, signs exactly the `(cid, seq)` it's told, zeroes the key, and keeps **no durable state** — so it cannot enforce monotonicity or validate that the CID is current. Its only two guarantees are **(i) IPNS-key confidentiality** and **(ii) record freshness**. A malicious or buggy relay can drive it to sign *any* `(cid, seq)` for any enrolled name. That's acceptable *only because* anti-rollback/anti-forgery is delegated entirely client-side (generation/M1 + parent-chain expected-CID, per Q1). The TEE is explicitly **not** part of the integrity boundary — worth stating plainly in the design, because the explainer's framing can read as if TEE republishing confers integrity.

**Concrete failure scenario (verified, live today).** Because the schedule's `latestCid` goes stale after an ordinary write, the republisher spends every 6h keeping the **old** CID's record fresh on the network while the **canonical new** CID's network record is never refreshed — so it expires ~48h after the client's one-time publish. DB-canonical resolve still serves the new CID (the DB row is correct), so it's masked today. But the instant you lean on the network — your transient-failure fallback, *or* the network-primary ideal — the network serves the stale CID: a rollback. The republisher is refreshing the wrong record.

**Recommendation:** make the **canonical `ipns_records` row the sole source of the TEE's signing inputs.** Greenfield, the clean move is to **collapse the duplicated state**: keep one row per IPNS name holding `latestCid`, `sequenceNumber`, `signedRecord`, `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey`, `keyEpoch`, and reduce the schedule to *scheduling metadata only* (`next_republish_at`, `consecutive_failures`, `status`, …) — or fold those columns into `ipns_records` and drop the schedule table entirely. The republisher then always re-signs the true current `(latestCid, sequenceNumber)` with a fresh EOL (per Q2, no increment), and the dual-source staleness — both the rollback and the availability bug — is structurally impossible, not just patched.

**Do you accept (a) the TEE framed as a stateless blind signer outside the integrity boundary, and (b) collapsing the republish-schedule's duplicated record state so the canonical `ipns_records` row is the only source of the TEE's signing inputs?**

> DEVELOPER

I accept both of these. is there any benefit of the TEE verifying that the CID it is republishing matches what the network currently resolves - i.e. the TEE can structurally never change the CID that an IPNS record resolves to.

> AGENT

Accepted. > AGENT

Confirmed the premise: `publishRecord` takes a **pre-signed** record — the client signs locally with the Ed25519 key it holds (`create-record.ts:17,31`; sdk-core `:28` *"signed locally using the Ed25519 private key"*), and the relay only *verifies* the signature (`ipns.service.ts:100`) plus checks the embedded value equals the CID (`:277-284`). The TEE is invoked **only** by the 6-hour schedule, never on the publish path. So clients originate every CID; the TEE is purely the background lease-renewer.

That changes the answer to your question.

## Yes to the goal — no to the network-check mechanism

The instinct is right: constraining the TEE so it can **never repoint a name** pulls the republisher out of the "relay drives the enclave to sign arbitrary `(cid, seq)`" hole I flagged in Q3, and brings the republish path back inside the integrity boundary. Worth doing. But "TEE verifies against what the network resolves" is the wrong way to get there, for three reasons:

1. **Circular trust.** For the check to mean anything, the TEE must learn "what the network resolves" from a source the relay can't control. Ask the relay (or relay-operated someguy/Kubo) → it lies → zero gain. An *independent* DHT view means giving the Phala CVM its own network egress and coupling republish liveness to DHT convergence — new dependency, new trust surface.
2. **It inverts the source of truth you just ratified.** The canonical `ipns_records` row is truth; the network is the *lagging, untrusted* replica (DB leads ~10-30s; DHT convergence is minutes). Gating the canonical signer on agreement with the lagging replica means that in the propagation window right after a legitimate write, the TEE sees the *old* CID on the network and either refuses to refresh the new record (availability hit) or — if it "signs what the network says" — refreshes the **old** CID, actively fighting the legitimate update.
3. **The network view is exactly what an attacker can perturb** — making it a signer input hands them a lever.

## The mechanism that gives you the exact property, network-free

Because clients self-sign every content change, **the TEE never legitimately needs to mint a new CID** — so lock it to lease-renewal of a record it can authenticate itself:

- The relay sends the TEE the **current marshaled `signedRecord`** (it already stores it), *not* `(latestCid, sequenceNumber)` scalars.
- The TEE: decrypt key → **parse and verify the signature on that record** → emit a new record with the **same embedded value (CID) and same sequence**, only a **later EOL** → re-sign.
- Since the TEE derives cid+seq from a record it cryptographically verified, the relay **cannot inject a different CID** — no enclave endpoint exists that mints a record for an arbitrary CID. The republisher can *only* extend the lease on something already validly signed.

The TEE's "source of truth" becomes the **signature on the record itself**, which is unforgeable without the key — strictly stronger than a network round-trip, and with zero added dependency. This also folds cleanly into Q2/Q3: the enclave contract collapses to a single operation — *"renew this record's validity"* — with no scalars, no increment, no policy.

**Residual, and why it's benign:** the relay could still replay an *old* validly-signed record for renewal. But post-Q1/Q2 that's harmless — it's renewed at its old, lower sequence, so it loses to the canonical higher-seq record by IPNS selection, and if it's cross-generation, M1 rejects it client-side. The relay can't elevate the old record's sequence (the TEE preserves the embedded seq and can't be made to forge a higher-seq old-CID record). The dangerous same-seq EOL-tiebreak repoint is also closed: the relay can never *obtain* a validly-signed `(attackerCID, sameSeq)` to renew, because neither the client nor the locked-down TEE will produce one.

**Recommendation:** change the TEE republish contract from "sign supplied `(cid, seq)`" to **"renew the validity of this verified existing record"** — derive cid+seq from the parsed, signature-checked record; extend EOL only. This delivers your "structurally cannot change the resolved CID" property without any network verification.

**Accept reframing the TEE as a record-lease-renewer (extends a verified existing record; cannot originate or repoint a CID)?**

> DEVELOPER

yes that sounds great - agreed.
