################## OPEN GAPS ##################

=== [HIGH] [open-gap] Relay has no per-name revocation fence — publish gate accepts old-key writes indefinitely after rotation
surface: write-rotation
chain: 1. Owner completes a full cascade: Nnew live, all parents re-pointed, TEE re-enrolled for Nnew, Nold unenrolled from republish. 2. The relay's ipns_records row for Nold still EXISTS (unenroll only deletes the republish_schedule row; ipns.service.unenrollBatch -> republishService.unenrollIpns deletes from scheduleRepository, NOT folderIpnsRepository). 3. W self-signs Nold at seq=dbSeq+1 with evilCid and publishes. 4. The publish gate at ipns.service.ts:300-318 sees existing row, forward seq, valid sig — ACCEPTS and stores. The DB row for Nold now points at evilCid at a higher seq.
impact: Integrity/availability: Nold remains a live, attacker-controllable IPNS name on the relay forever. Any cached link, bookmarked k51, or un-updated client reference to Nold resolves to attacker content. The old name is never tombstoned.
rationale: ADR-0001 rotation changes k51 names and re-points parents, but neither the ratified design NOR current code tombstones/locks the OLD ipns_records row. Unenroll is republish-schedule-only. The publish gate has zero revocation awareness. The mitigation 'old ciphertext is presumed leaked' (ADR-0002) is about READ content, not about the relay continuing to accept WRITES under a revoked name. Needs: on rotation, mark Nold row revoked (reject all further publishes) or delete it. Q1-Q4 do not address relay-side write-fencing of rotated-out names.

=== [HIGH] [open-gap] parseCachedRecord null/mismatch fall-through serves unguarded network record (DB-canonical bypass)
surface: resolve
chain: 1. Resolve (ipns.service.ts ~506-540): if parseCachedRecord(cached) returns NULL, the dbSeq>=networkSeq precedence branch is skipped entirely and the NETWORK record is returned verbatim (lines 536-540), with NO seq/generation gate re-applied. 2. parseCachedRecord returns null when signedRecord is null OR when parsed.cid !== cached.latestCid (codec line 73). 3. Attacker rolls back the raw DHT to an old validly-signed record. 4. Trigger the null path: e.g. a shared-folder row whose signedRecord was never populated (per project memory, shared-folder rows have null public_key and may lack a full signedRecord), or any row in the CID-mismatch state. 5. Relay returns the attacker's rolled-back network record to a relay-mediated client — DB-canonical shielding is silently bypassed for exactly these rows.
impact: Integrity: the Q1 DB-canonical shield does NOT cover rows where parseCachedRecord yields null. For those names a keyless DHT attacker's rollback reaches relay-mediated clients TODAY, before network-primary. The client's generation high-water is the only remaining defense; if the client lacks a floor (cold start, see prior scenario) the rollback lands. Shared-folder / recipient nodes are the most likely null-signedRecord rows, i.e. exactly the multi-party trust boundary.
rationale: The redesign asserts DB-canonical resolve shields relay-mediated clients, but the implemented fall-through serves the network record unguarded whenever the DB row can't be parsed — and the redesign does not specify that the relay must re-apply an anti-rollback (seq/generation) gate to network records before serving them. The Q1 narrative ('resolveRecord does NOT re-apply this gate to network records') is stated as a fact, not flagged as a hole. Needs a decision: either (a) reject/404 instead of serving an ungated network record when the DB row is canonical-but-unparseable, or (b) re-apply a seq>=storedSeq floor to the network record from the DB's sequenceNumber column even when the full signedRecord is absent. Right now it is an unflagged bypass of the stated shield.

=== [HIGH] [open-gap] Non-atomic publish CAS — two concurrent writers both pass the gate, one write silently lost
surface: concurrency
chain: 1. ipns.service.publishRecord does findOne(ipnsName) → in-memory gate (anti-rollback, expectedSequenceNumber CAS, embedded-seq == dbSeq+1) → save(existing). There is NO row lock, NO @VersionColumn, NO transaction wrapping the read-modify-write (verified: no setLock/queryRunner/VersionColumn in the publish path). 2. Device A and Device B both resolve the name at dbSeq=5 and each builds a forward record at embedded seq=6 (different CIDs CA, CB). 3. Both requests findOne and read sequenceNumber=5 before either saves. 4. Both pass the CAS (expected 5 == current 5) and the embedded-seq gate (6 == 5+1). 5. Both call save(): TypeORM emits an UPDATE that sets sequenceNumber='6', latestCid, signedRecord by primary key. The second UPDATE overwrites the first. Final row = seq 6 / CB, and CA is silently lost even though both clients got HTTP 200.
impact: Integrity/durability: a confirmed write (CA, the file/folder mutation that returned 200) is lost. Both clients believe their write committed. Generation (Q1) does NOT cover this — both writes share the same generation; the loss is a within-generation last-writer-wins on a same-sequence collision. The CAS that is supposed to serialize devices does not actually serialize because it is not atomic.
rationale: Q1 generation is an anti-ROLLBACK authority (cross-revocation), not a within-generation serialization primitive; it explicitly says nothing about concurrent same-generation writes. Q2/Q3/Q4 concern TEE/sequence semantics, not the publish CAS atomicity. The proposed design narrows the canonical row to ONE row (Q3 collapse) which makes this row the sole serialization point — but never specifies it must be guarded by a conditional UPDATE / @VersionColumn / SELECT FOR UPDATE. As written the CAS is a TOCTOU and provides no real mutual exclusion. Needs an explicit decision: make the publish a single atomic compare-and-set (e.g. UPDATE ... WHERE sequenceNumber = :expected, check affected-rows, else 409).

=== [HIGH] [open-gap] Add-during-rotation — child created under a parent mid-readKey-rotation gets sealed under the doomed parent readKey / generation
surface: read-rotation
chain: 1. Owner begins read-revocation on parent P: mint new readKey_P', bump generation gP->gP', re-seal all existing SealedChildRefs under readKey_P'. This is a multi-node cascade, not atomic. 2. Concurrently (another device, or a fast follow-up op) the owner ADDS a new child C under P, sealing C's readKeyRef under the OLD readKey_P (the device hasn't observed the rotation) and recording generation gP in C's parent-mirror slot. 3. The rotation completes and re-points P to a Node sealed under readKey_P'. C's SealedChildRef, written under readKey_P, is either (a) overwritten/lost by the rotation's re-seal of P's child set, or (b) survives but is undecryptable to any recipient holding only readKey_P' . 4. Recipients of the post-rotation grant cannot decrypt C's ref (sealed under the retired key); C is orphaned. OR the rotation's last-writer-wins on P's Node drops C entirely (the add and the re-point both rewrite P's child list → the lost-write race from scenario 1).
impact: Integrity/availability: a newly-added child is silently dropped or rendered permanently undecryptable for the post-rotation audience. The owner believes C was added (got 200). Data appears lost from the share even to entitled recipients.
rationale: The redesign describes read-revocation as readKey+generation rotation cascading parent re-points, but does not address concurrent structural mutations (add/remove child) during the cascade. There is no stated rotation epoch/barrier on the parent node that would force a concurrent add to either block, retry under the new readKey, or be replayed after the cascade. Q1's generation protects against serving an OLD node, but does not sequence a concurrent ADD against an in-flight rotation. Needs a design decision: a per-node rotation lock/epoch (writes during rotation must rebase onto the new readKey/generation) or a post-rotation reconciliation sweep.

=== [HIGH] [open-gap] Idempotent-republish branch lets a same-seq EOL-only record overwrite the canonical signedRecord, racing a forward publish
surface: concurrency
chain: 1. Verified: when embeddedSeq == dbSeq the relay treats it as idempotent republish — does NOT increment, but STILL updates latestCid and signedRecord (lines 326-333). 2. The TEE renewal (Q4) arrives as exactly this case: same seq S, same CID, later EOL. 3. Concurrently a forward publish (seq S+1, new CID) is in flight. Because there is no atomicity (scenario 1), interleave: forward publish reads dbSeq=S, passes gate; TEE-renewal request reads dbSeq=S, passes idempotent branch; forward publish saves (S+1, C_new, sig_new); TEE-renewal save (no increment) then writes latestCid=C_old? No — idempotent branch sets latestCid=metadataCid (C_old) and signedRecord=renewed. The renewal's save lands LAST and overwrites latestCid back to C_old and sequenceNumber stays whatever the row object loaded (S, because the renewal loaded the row at S before the forward publish committed). 4. Net: the forward publish (S+1, C_new) is clobbered back to (S, C_old) by an idempotent-branch save that loaded a stale row.
impact: Integrity/durability: a forward content publish is rolled back to the previous CID and sequence by a concurrent EOL-only renewal, because the idempotent branch unconditionally writes latestCid/signedRecord from a stale in-memory row. The anti-rollback embedded-seq check does NOT save them — both records were valid at their read time; the loss is purely the lost-update race on save().
rationale: This is scenario 1's lost-update generalized to the idempotent-republish branch the redesign RELIES ON for Q4 lease renewal. The design's Q3 collapse makes this branch fire on every TEE renewal against the one canonical row, dramatically widening the race surface versus today's separate schedule table. The verified code performs an unconditional save(existing) with no WHERE-clause guard on sequenceNumber, so a renewal that loaded a pre-publish row will clobber a committed forward publish. Must be closed by the same atomic conditional-update decision as scenario 1 (UPDATE ... WHERE sequenceNumber matches the value read), applied specifically to the idempotent/renewal write so EOL-only updates never regress latestCid/sequence.

=== [HIGH] [open-gap] Relay-controlled currentEpoch coerces TEE to re-wrap every IPNS key under an attacker-chosen epoch public key
surface: epoch-key
chain: 1. republish.ts trusts entry.currentEpoch and entry.previousEpoch as plain scalars supplied by the (untrusted) relay (republish.service.ts:103-104 -> republish.ts:71,86-89). 2. Malicious/compromised relay submits a batch with currentEpoch = E_attacker where the TEE's getPublicKey(E_attacker) resolves to a key the relay can later request decryption for (e.g. a future epoch the relay also drives, or an epoch whose private half the relay can socially-engineer the TEE to expose). 3. decryptWithFallback succeeds against the legitimately-enrolled epoch (usedEpoch != currentEpoch), so the TEE unconditionally runs reEncryptForEpoch(key, currentEpoch) and returns upgradedEncryptedKey. 4. Relay persists the attacker-epoch-wrapped key into ipns_records.encryptedIpnsKey (republish.service.ts:147-149). 5. The IPNS private key is now wrapped under an epoch the attacker influences; on a later epoch-key-disclosure or a colluding-CVM-image swap the relay can unwrap it offline.
impact: Confidentiality of the IPNS Ed25519 signing key (integrity of the mutable pointer). Whoever holds the chosen epoch private key can forge IPNS records for that name forever -> repoint the vault to attacker CIDs.
rationale: Q1-Q4 say nothing about authenticating the epoch scalars the relay feeds the enclave. The TEE must derive currentEpoch/previousEpoch from its OWN trusted clock + its own epoch schedule, not accept them from the relay; and reEncryptForEpoch must only target an epoch the TEE itself currently considers active. As written the relay picks the target wrapping epoch. Needs an explicit decision: TEE-internal epoch derivation + a closed enumerated set of valid target epochs.

=== [MEDIUM] [open-gap] Batch cross-contamination: a single poisoned entry's epoch fallback masks a wrong-identity wrapped key (silent mis-enrollment)
surface: concurrency
chain: 1. Relay builds a 100-entry batch; for a target name it swaps in encryptedIpnsKey belonging to a DIFFERENT name/identity (relay controls the column; ipns_records is relay-owned). 2. TEE decryptWithFallback succeeds (it's a real ECIES blob the TEE can unwrap), signs a record for entry.latestCid/entry.ipnsName using the WRONG identity's private key. 3. The marshaled record's embedded pubkey will not match entry.ipnsName — under CURRENT code the TEE does not check name<->key correspondence (ipns-signer.ts just signs). Q4 says the TEE verifies the EXISTING record's signature, but verifying the relay-supplied existing record's self-consistency does NOT bind it to the ipnsName the relay claims this slot is for. 4. Relay publishes the cross-signed record / persists a swapped upgradedEncryptedKey to another row.
impact: Integrity: relay can get the TEE to mint records under any enrolled key for any name slot, and can shuffle wrapped keys between rows during the upgrade-persist step (republish.service.ts:147-149 trusts the result blindly). Enables targeted pointer forgery / key confusion across users.
rationale: Q4's 'verify the existing record's signature + same CID/same seq' check does not require the TEE to verify that the record's embedded Ed25519 pubkey == deriveIpnsName-inverse(entry.ipnsName), nor that the decrypted private key corresponds to that name. Per project memory (ipns-resolve-ed25519-pubkey-from-name) the name encodes the pubkey, so the TEE CAN and MUST assert publicKeyFromIpnsName(ipnsName) == pubkey(decryptedKey) == record.pubkey before emitting. Needs to be added to Q4's enclave contract.

=== [MEDIUM] [open-gap] Relay drops the upgradedEncryptedKey from the TEE response — keeps the key pinned to the soon-to-retire previous epoch forever
surface: epoch-key
chain: 1. TEE correctly re-encrypts an old-epoch key for the current epoch and returns upgradedEncryptedKey (republish.ts:104-107). 2. Untrusted relay simply ignores upgradedEncryptedKey and does NOT persist it (it controls the persistence step at republish.service.ts:147-153). 3. The ipns_records row stays wrapped under the OLD epoch. 4. Repeat every cycle: TEE re-encrypts, relay discards, so the upgrade never sticks. 5. When previousEpoch retires, the key is undecryptable -> permanent republish failure (same terminal state as the grace-window scenario, but adversarially induced).
impact: Availability: relay can selectively brick republishing for chosen names at epoch-retirement time, with no detectable failure until the record expires. Targeted denial of metadata availability per-user.
rationale: The upgrade-persist step is entirely relay-trusted and there is no enclave-side or client-side verification that the key got migrated. Q3's 'canonical row is the only signing input' makes this worse: there's no second copy to recover from. Needs a decision: either the TEE refuses to renew once a key is older than (currentEpoch - 1) forcing the relay's hand, or the client periodically re-enrolls/re-wraps from its own held key as the durable recovery path (client is the only trusted party that can re-mint the wrapped key).


################## CRITICAL (any verdict) ##################

=== [CRITICAL] [residual-needs-decision] Un-rotated sibling / ancestor reachable via cached SealedChildRef
surface: read-rotation
chain: 1. Read-revocation rotates the readKey + generation of the REVOKED node (and, per chaining, re-seals its SealedChildRef under the parent). 2. ADR-0002 is lazy: sibling nodes and the parent's OTHER children are NOT rotated. 3. The revoked reader cached the parent's readKey (they had it to reach the revoked node) and the parent's SealedChildRefs for siblings. 4. Using the cached parent readKey, they unwrap sibling SealedChildRefs and continue reading/navigating siblings whose readKeys never changed.
impact: Confidentiality: revoked reader retains access to SIBLING content and future sibling updates — full continued access to the rest of the shared subtree, not just the one revoked node.
rationale: This is the central scoping question the redesign must answer explicitly. If revocation only rotates the single node's readKey, a reader who held the PARENT readKey keeps everything else. Correct read-revocation at a scope must rotate the readKey + generation of the revocation ROOT and re-seal ALL descendant SealedChildRefs reachable from it (cascade), AND re-mint the per-recipient ECIES grant so the revoked recipient's grant no longer decrypts the new share-root readKey. ADR-0002 'rotate readKey + generation' is underspecified on cascade scope. Until the cascade boundary is ratified this is a real continued-access gap, not the accepted ciphertext residual.

=== [CRITICAL] [residual-needs-decision] ECIES share-root grant not re-minted — revoked recipient still unwraps the new readKey
surface: read-rotation
chain: 1. Sharing model: one ECIES grant per recipient at the share-root wraps the share-root readKey to each recipient's public key. 2. On revocation the client rotates the share-root readKey and bumps generation. 3. If the new share-root readKey is re-wrapped into the SAME set of per-recipient grants (including the revoked recipient's), the revoked reader decrypts the new readKey with their own private key. 4. With the new readKey they walk the freshly-sealed chain and read all new content — rotation accomplished nothing.
impact: Confidentiality/integrity: complete defeat of read-revocation — revoked reader has the post-rotation readKey and full new-content access.
rationale: ADR-0002 names 'rotate readKey + generation' but does not state that the per-recipient grant set must be REBUILT excluding the revoked recipient. The whole point of one-ECIES-grant-per-recipient is selective exclusion; the design must mandate that read-revocation re-mints grants for the surviving recipient set only. This is the load-bearing invariant for revocation correctness and must be made explicit, not assumed.

=== [CRITICAL] [residual-accepted] Compromised-enclave blast radius is bounded by Q4 but NOT zero: image-swap exfiltration of every wrapped key in the batch
surface: epoch-key
chain: 1. Adversary who controls the CVM image / Phala deployment (or the attestation chain) swaps the enclave for one that exposes the epoch private key. 2. With the epoch private key, the relay (which already holds all encryptedIpnsKey blobs in ipns_records) unwraps EVERY name's IPNS private key offline — not just lease-renew, but full origination. 3. Q4's 'lease-renewer only' bound is a property of the HONEST enclave's API surface, not a containment of a malicious enclave: once the epoch key leaks, Q4 provides no protection because forgery happens outside the enclave.
impact: Confidentiality/Integrity: total — every IPNS signing key for every user wrapped under the live epoch is recoverable; attacker can repoint all vaults. This is the systemic worst case.
rationale: Q4 explicitly scopes the HONEST enclave's worst-case to lower-seq replay; full compromise of the enclave/epoch key is the trust root and is out of Q4's scope by design (TEE attestation is the control). Tolerable ONLY if (a) attestation/remote-attestation is enforced on every epoch-key provisioning and (b) epoch rotation limits the exposure window. Worth restating as the explicit residual: Q4 reduces honest-relay-via-TEE risk, it does not reduce enclave-compromise risk — that still rests entirely on Phala attestation + epoch rotation cadence.

> AGENT

Sweep done: 56 scenarios across 6 lenses, and they **converge on 5 root causes** (many lenses independently hit the same ones — strong signal). Three are genuinely new and sit directly downstream of this session's Q1–Q4; two are read-rotation invariants that should already live in your §4 amendments (flag to confirm). I verified the load-bearing code claims against my own grounding — the fall-through, the unconditional idempotent-branch `save`, schedule-only unenroll, and relay-supplied epoch scalars all check out.

## The decisions (new, downstream of Q1–Q4)

### A. Generation authority only covers *cross*-generation — within-generation & cold-start rollback is still open
This is the big one, and it's the completion of Q1. Generation bumps *only* on rotation, so a malicious relay (or, post-network-primary, a keyless DHT attacker) can serve an **old but genuinely-signed lower-seq record in the same generation** and every check passes — the client renders a stale/reverted/resurrected state. Three amplifiers the sweep found:
- **First-contact clients have no floor**: the `SealedChildRef` mirrors *generation* but not *version*, so a freshly-shared recipient can be pinned at any past same-gen seq forever.
- **Cold/reset device**: the generation high-water is soft per-device local state — a fresh device accepts any rollback.
- **`parseCachedRecord`-null fall-through (open-gap)**: when the DB row can't be parsed (notably **shared-folder rows with null `signedRecord`/`public_key`** — your own memory flags these), resolve silently serves the **ungated network record**. The DB-canonical shield has a hole exactly at the multi-party trust boundary.

**Recommend:** (1) add a **durable per-node `seq` high-water** on the client (the sequence analog of M1) — reject `seq < highWater` regardless of resolve source; (2) **bind a version floor** (current seq, or a hash of the head Node) into the `SealedChildRef` at (re)share, so first-contact/cold devices inherit a floor from the parent chain; (3) on the relay, **never fall through to an ungated network record** — when the canonical DB row is unparseable, fail closed (or apply a `seq >= storedSeq` floor from the DB column even without the full signedRecord).

### B. The publish CAS is a non-atomic read-modify-write — Q3/Q4 widen the race
`findOne → gate → save` has no row lock / `@VersionColumn` / conditional UPDATE. Two devices both at `dbSeq=5` both pass the gate at embedded-seq 6 → last-writer-wins, and a write that returned **200 is silently lost** (generation can't help — same generation). Worse, Q4's renewal hits the **idempotent branch on the single canonical row** (Q3), and a renewal that loaded a pre-publish row clobbers `latestCid`/`seq` back — an EOL-only refresh rolling back a committed forward publish.

**Recommend:** make publish a single atomic compare-and-set — `UPDATE … SET … WHERE ipnsName=:n AND sequenceNumber=:expected`; 0 rows affected → 409. Guard the idempotent/renewal write identically (`WHERE seq=:loaded`) so a lease-renewal can never regress `latestCid`/`seq`.

### C. The enclave contract (Q4) needs three more bindings
The relay still feeds the TEE the **epoch scalars**, the **wrapped key**, and the **claimed name** — and the enclave trusts all three:
- **Relay-chosen epoch (open-gap):** relay sets `currentEpoch` → coerces the TEE to re-wrap the IPNS key under an attacker-influenced epoch pubkey → offline forgery later. → TEE must derive the epoch from its **own clock/schedule**, with an enclave-enumerated set of valid re-wrap targets.
- **Name↔key confusion (open-gap):** Q4 verifies the record's *self*-consistency but not that it's the *right* name. → before emitting, assert `publicKeyFromIpnsName(ipnsName) == pubkey(decryptedKey) == record.pubkey`.
- **Migration durability (open-gap):** relay can simply **drop `upgradedEncryptedKey`** → the key stays pinned to the retiring epoch → silent brick at retirement. → make the **client** the recovery path (periodic re-enroll/re-wrap from its held key); or the TEE refuses to renew a key older than `currentEpoch−1`.

### D. Write-revocation has no relay-side fence — the rotated-out name stays live and writable
ADR-0001 changes the k51 name and re-points parents, but **unenroll deletes only the schedule row** — the old `ipns_records` row persists and the publish gate has **zero revocation awareness**. A revoked writer's cached key can publish to the old name *forever*, and resolve of the old name still serves content to any stale link/bookmark.

**Recommend:** on rotation, **tombstone the old row** — publish gate rejects all writes to a tombstoned name, resolve returns a tombstone/410 (not stale content), unenroll from TEE. *Decision for you:* tombstone-and-keep (stale links get a clear "moved/revoked") vs hard-delete the row.

## Confirm against existing §4 (not new decisions if already there)

### E. Read-revocation cascade scope + grant re-mint (two CRITICALs)
The sweep insists revocation must (i) rotate the readKey + re-seal **all descendant `SealedChildRef`s from the revocation root** — else a reader who held the *parent* key keeps **sibling** access; and (ii) **re-mint the per-recipient ECIES grant set excluding the revoked recipient** — else they unwrap the new readKey and rotation accomplished nothing. These should already be your §4 `rotateReadFromNode` + HIGH-3 grant re-mint — **flag to confirm they're explicit, not assumed.**

## Accepted residuals (no action, stated for completeness)
- Revoked reader keeps already-distributed ciphertext — ADR-0002, by design.
- **Compromised enclave / leaked epoch key = total loss** (every wrapped key unwrappable offline). Q4 bounds the *honest* enclave, not a malicious one — that rests entirely on **Phala attestation + epoch-rotation cadence**. Worth stating as the explicit systemic residual.
- Equal-seq EOL selection is a freshness/availability nuisance only (under Q2/Q4 same-seq ⇒ same CID).

---

**Two asks:** (1) ratify the recommendations for **A–C** (durable seq high-water + version-floor in SealedChildRef + fail-closed fall-through; atomic CAS; the three enclave bindings); (2) decide **D**'s tombstone semantics (recommend tombstone-and-keep). And confirm **E** is already explicit in §4 — if not, it's two more invariants to write. Want me to fold all of this into the AMENDMENTS file once you've ruled?

=== rotateReadFromNode / cascade / re-mint / grant / sibling / re-seal mentions (DESIGN) ===
13:The core idea: every node's metadata carries the wrapped keys to reach its children, sealed so that a holder of the node's read key can unwrap its children's read keys, recursing down. Sharing read access = hand the recipient **one** wrapped key at the share-root node (any node — deep folder or single file). No per-item DB rows, no separate lockbox or sidechain object. The only data-plane DB residue is `O(recipients)` read-root grants.
17:Today, each `FolderEntry` ECIES-wraps **both** a `folderKeyEncrypted` (read) **and** an `ipnsPrivateKeyEncrypted` (write) to the owner, inline per child (`packages/core/src/folder/types.ts:30-46`). Reads are `O(children)` ECIES; sharing fans out into the `share_keys` table. The read chain replaces all of this with **one ECIES at the share-root, then `O(depth)` symmetric AES** down the tree. Creating a child no longer fans out per recipient — the child key is sealed under the parent key every covering grant-holder already holds transitively.
21:1. **Read revocation is lazy, folder-coarse, and unsound.** `executeLazyRotation` (`apps/web/src/services/share.service.ts:602-660`) rotates **only** the share-root `folderKey` and never walks descendants. A revoked reader who cached subtree keys keeps reading. `revokeShare` soft-deletes and keeps `ShareKey` rows "for lazy rotation" (`apps/api/src/shares/shares.service.ts:256-269`).
58:A read grant ships only the `readKey`. Because the write material is sealed under a different key the read grant never conveys, a read-only holder can never reach a signing key. The earlier single-GCM-body design was wrong: you cannot selectively strip one sub-object from a single AEAD seal, and the relay (untrusted) cannot strip what it cannot decrypt. Two bodies makes the separation structural.
120:Each seal mints a fresh random IV (preserve the `seal.ts:42` behavior — review **m3**: never reuse an IV; keep `readKey` rotation coupled to the generation bump so re-seals always use a fresh key).
149:1. Hold the parent `readKey` (from a grant, or unwrapped one level up).
162:### 2.8 The read-root grant — the only DB residue
176:  "writeDescriptorRef": null,    // populated only for write grants (Tier-2)
182:The recipient ECIES-unwraps `readKeyEcies` once to get the share-root `readKey`, then chains down with symmetric AES — no further ECIES, no per-item rows. Sharing any node (deep folder or single file) is uniform: `rootNodeId` is whatever node you grant.
188:- A single-file read grant = ECIES-wrap that one file node's `readKey`. The recipient fetches the node by name, unseals `content`, recovers `cid`, `fileIv`, and `content.fileKey`.
196:Baseline: `N = 1e6` items in the shared subtree, `R = 10` recipients, balanced tree so depth `d = O(log N) ≈ 20`. "Republish" = one IPNS publish (one sequence bump + signature via the chosen write mechanism). The publish/sign step is held abstract; no read-chain flow depends on which Tier-2 mechanism signs — only on how many nodes republish.
200:| Operation | Crypto | ECIES | Nodes resealed | IPNS republishes | Worst case (N=1e6, R=10) |
202:| Issue read grant | 1 wrap | 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
204:| Add item | 1 reseal + 1 parent-link | 0 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
206:| Move out of scope | rotate subtree | re-mint affected grants | \|subtree\| + 2 | \|subtree\| + parents | ~1e6 + 2 |
207:| Rename over destination | rotate displaced dest | re-mint affected grants | \|dest-subtree\| + 1 | \|dest-subtree\| + 1 | ~1e6 + 1 |
208:| Delete | rotate deleted subtree | re-mint affected grants | \|subtree\| + 1 | \|subtree\| + 1 | ~1e6 + 1 |
209:| Read-revoke (1 of R) | rotate share-root subtree | re-mint R−1 grants | \|subtree\| | \|subtree\| | ~1e6 |
210:| Write-revoke (1 of R) | mechanism-dependent | — | 0 read-plane | 0 → O(subtree) | 0 (mediated) to ~1e6 (rotation) |
212:### 3.2 Issue a read grant — `O(1)` crypto, 1 ECIES, 0 republishes
214:`readKeyEcies = ECIES_wrap(shareRootNode.readKey → recipientPublicKey)`; insert one `shares` row. No node is touched. Granting a single file is identical to granting a deep folder.
218:One-time ECIES-unwrap of the grant, then symmetric walk (Section 2.6) to depth `d`. At a file node, unseal `content` for `cid`/`fileIv`/`content.fileKey`, fetch and decrypt the IPFS blob. Verify the envelope `generation` against the grant (Section 4.6 distinguishes "behind" from "revoked").
222:Create the new node (fresh `readKey`, `generation = 0`, seal its bodies). Add a `SealedChildRef` to the parent, reseal the parent read-body, publish the new node then the parent. **No per-recipient fan-out** — the child key is sealed under the parent `readKey` every covering grant-holder already has. Deletes `reWrapForRecipients`/`addShareKeys` (`share.service.ts:337,469`).
226:Remove the `SealedChildRef` from the old parent (reseal + republish); add it to the new parent (reseal + republish). The node keeps its own `readKey`/`generation`. Kills the move-reencrypt storm.
228:Caveat from review **m2 (per-grant scope):** "within scope" is a **per-grant** property, not a global one. A reader granted at the **old parent only** cached the moved node's `readKey`; after a move to a sibling they do not cover, "within scope for the owner" is "out of scope for that reader." Therefore: **any move that changes a node's ancestor set must rotate if any active grant sits on an ancestor that is no longer an ancestor.** Compute scope-exit per-grant; conservatively, rotate on any move whose old and new parent differ in their grant-ancestor set. A move that is genuinely within-scope for all grants (e.g., owner-only, or both parents under the same grant root) stays at 2 republishes.
232:These three are the same operation: a node's content leaves a reader's reachable scope while its CIDs persist on IPFS. Each does the link mechanics (detach/repoint, reseal + republish parent) then calls `rotateReadFromNode` over the departing subtree (Section 4). Single-file cases are 2 republishes; million-node subtrees are ~1e6.
234:Why rotate a **deleted** node: delete only removes the parent pointer; the CIDs remain on IPFS and a reader who cached subtree keys can still fetch by CID. Bumping `generation` + new `readKey` (and, per CRIT-1, new `fileKey` for files) makes cached keys fail against republished blobs and protects future versions.
238:Applying review **HIGH-4 (data loss):** `rotateOne` re-seals the parent read-body from its in-memory `children[]`. A concurrent add that CAS-wins first will be clobbered when rotation retries from a stale decrypted child list. **Rotation must re-fetch and re-merge `SealedChildRef`s on every CAS-409, not merely re-seal the body.** Section 4.5 makes this explicit. Without it, a concurrent upload during a million-node rotation silently drops the new child.
242:> A node leaving a reader's reachable scope ⇒ `rotateReadFromNode(node)`.
244:This collapses the bug class CipherBox kept patching per mutation-path (`delete.rs`, `rename.rs`, `executeLazyRotation` each special-cased). Defining rotation **recursively** structurally eliminates the `executeLazyRotation:602` single-node bug — there is no un-rotated tail because the walk is the definition. Modulo the per-grant scoping in Section 3.5.
248:`rotateReadFromNode(nodeId)` backs read-revoke and every scope-exit mutation. It is the expensive side of the asymmetry, and the design's job is to make paying the `O(items)` cost **safe** under crashes, concurrency, and the 6-hour republisher — not to dodge it.
252:Applying review **CRIT-1 (critical):** re-sealing the read-body under a new `readKey` is **not sufficient** for file nodes. A revoked reader cached the old `readKey`, already unsealed `content.fileKey`, and holds the raw AES content key. If a new file version is encrypted under the same `fileKey`, the revoked reader decrypts it the moment they learn the new CID.
258:The reader reaches the subtree through one door: the parent's `SealedChildRef[R].readKeySealed` plus, for grantees, the `readKeyEcies` in their `shares` row. The atomic root step:
261:2. Re-seal R's read-body under `readKey'` with AAD bound to `generation'`.
263:4. Re-mint `readKeyEcies` for **every remaining recipient whose grant root is R** against `readKey'`; bump `rootGeneration`; delete the revoked recipient's row. (Descendant grant re-mint is handled in Section 4.4 — HIGH-3.)
266:After this one step the revoked reader is cut off from the entry point. The residual exposure window is bounded to "already-seen content under not-yet-rotated descendants," never the whole tree, and only ever shrinks as the walk proceeds. This is the precise sense of "a crash must not leave a revoked reader on the un-rotated tail."
270:Applying review **M1 (major):** the IPNS signature covers `value=/ipfs/CID` and `sequence` only — **not** `generation` (confirmed: the publish gate anchors the signed value strictly to `/ipfs/${metadataCid}`). The parent-mirror AAD defense works for descendants reached through a parent, but a grantee reaches the share-root **directly** via `rootIpnsName`/`rootGeneration`, both relay-served DB values. A colluding relay that simply does not apply the rotation publish (drops it, keeps serving the old signed record) leaves the revoked reader alive with no signed signal.
274:### 4.4 Multi-rooted grant re-mint (HIGH-3)
276:Applying review **HIGH-3 (orphaned grant):** the rotated subtree may contain nodes that were **independently shared** (e.g., a single-file share to Carol deep inside a folder being deleted). Re-minting grants only at the rotation root orphans Carol's grant — her `readKeyEcies` wraps a now-rotated key and is never re-minted, locking her out with no recovery.
278:Fix: rotation must **enumerate all `shares` rows whose `rootNodeId` ∈ the rotated set** and, for each non-revoked recipient, re-mint `readKeyEcies` against that node's new `readKey`/`generation` and bump its `rootGeneration`. The grant re-mint is multi-rooted because the tree is multi-rooted. This is an indexed query on `shares.rootNodeId` per rotated node (or one batched query over the rotated set).
302:5. **Re-fetch the current child list and merge any `SealedChildRef`s added since step 3 (HIGH-4)**, then re-seal N's read-body under `readKey'` (AAD `gN'`).
306:9. Re-mint any grants rooted at N (Section 4.4); mark N done; push N's children with `readKey'`.
312:Crash recovery and double-rotation safety: if the job record is lost between "published N at `readKey'`" and "rewrote parent link," the new key is gone and the parent link cannot be re-sealed to match. Resolution: **a fresh full `rotateOne(N)` is the recovery path** — generate `readKey''`/`gN''`, seal the parent link with `readKey''`, publish both. An extra rotation only strengthens revocation and costs one republish. Double-rotation safety is what lets the published IPNS state be the sole source of truth. Publish-child-then-parent ordering guarantees the worst a crash leaves is a child ahead of its parent — exactly what a plain re-rotation fixes.
314:`verifySubtreeClean(R)`: an `O(items)` read-only pass that flags any edge where `parent.link.generation ≠ child.envelope.generation`. It is the resume entry point (rebuilds the frontier) and the post-completion audit (a converged job has zero dirty edges).
324:Honest-reader liveness (review **MED-6**): after any rotation, a still-authorized reader sees a generation bump and must re-fetch their re-minted grant; if they resolve the new root body before the API has the re-minted `readKeyEcies`, they hard-fail. Provide a "soft behind, retry" vs "hard revoked" distinction on the read path (re-minted grant present but generation ahead ⇒ retry; grant row deleted ⇒ revoked). This mirrors the documented `#489`/`#494` "Folder not loaded" desync class — reconcile `folderTree` against `sequenceNumber` before rotation publishes.
328:Read-key material is client-only (zero-knowledge). The rotation walk generates new keys and re-seals bodies, so it **must run client-side** on a client holding the share-root `readKey` (owner or write-grantee). The relay only provides IPNS CAS and IPFS storage. It cannot be offloaded to the relay/TEE — the TEE only signs (write plane) and never decrypts read content.
330:At 1e6 nodes the job is owner-online and resumable across sessions (persist the frontier each batch; resume via `verifySubtreeClean`). Desktop (FUSE/Tauri) is the natural host (long-lived process, `PublishCoordinator`, keys in memory). UX: the revoke is effectively complete for the revoked reader the instant the root step lands; surface "revoked" immediately and "fully rotated N/M" as background progress.
336:Applying the simplicity review: **commit to eager rotation** (Tier-1 item 3 accepts the `O(items)` cost). The lazy "rotate-on-next-write" variant is **deferred**, not part of the core deliverable — it doubles the rotation surface and reintroduces the mixed-generation, cold-node-filename-leak surface that the unsound `executeLazyRotation` had (review **MED-5**: lazy leaks post-revocation filenames of cold subtrees, because `SealedChildRef.name` is plaintext within the sealed body; a revoked reader holding a cold node's old key reads names of items added after revocation). For a user-initiated revoke, eager is **mandatory**, not merely the default.
338:The genuinely useful nuance — "delete of one file shouldn't walk anything" — is just **subtree size 1**, not a separate algorithm. Keep one deferred sentence: the `generation`/`rotateOne` primitive is amortizable on-write later if the eager cost proves painful. Do not build two rotation modes now.
346:| Dimension | (a) Mediated (relay→TEE sign) | (b) Per-grant subkey | (c) Full Ed25519 rotation | (d) Hybrid: owner self-signs, delegated mediated |
349:| Revoke cost | O(1), no republish | O(subtree) cascade | O(subtree) cascade | O(1) for delegated |
362:- The k51 name is bound to the Ed25519 key (`deriveIpnsName`/`publicKeyFromIpnsName`), strict-verified from the name (`publish.rs:156`). Any key rotation changes the name → parent re-point → cascade. This is what dominates (b)/(c).
370:- Scope-escalation: unless the TEE verifies the unsigned record's name/sequence/CID is within the granted subtree, a delegate with a token for node X can submit a record for node Y. Key-possession candidates (b)/(c) structurally cannot have this — you can only sign names whose key you hold.
373:**(c) full Ed25519 rotation** keeps write-integrity cryptographic (no signing oracle), needs no new enclave endpoint, and is strictly a subset of the read-rotation machinery already being built. Its cost is an `O(subtree)` IPNS cascade per write-revoke (the accepted asymmetry) plus a co-writer re-key. **(b) is dominated by (c)** — same k51 break and cascade, an extra ephemeral-key indirection, no compensating benefit; the "revoke one grant without disturbing others" edge is illusory because all writers to one mutable node share one IPNS identity.
375:Co-writer re-key (review **m1**, must be designed, not hand-waved): surviving co-writers receive the rotated Ed25519 key re-wrapped into their write-grant row (`writeDescriptorRef`). This is `O(co-writers)` write-grant rewrites, so (c)'s true cost is `O(subtree republishes) + O(co-writers re-wraps)`. A co-writer offline during rotation cannot write until they re-fetch — acceptable, but explicit.
379:Runner-up: **(a) full mediated** (all writes mediated, single signer) — **only** if the maintainer accepts TEE+relay in the write-trust base and builds a TEE that **verifies the record is within the token's authorized name/subtree** before signing. Full (a) at least genuinely serializes the sequence race (the server assigns sequences atomically), which (d) does not.
381:The choice flips from (c) to (a) if: the `O(subtree)` write-revoke cascade is judged unacceptable for the expected revoke frequency; AND a TEE sign-endpoint with airtight token-to-name binding can be delivered to a trustworthy standard; AND write-time coupling to TEE/relay liveness (today's 30s TEE timeout, "TEE unavailable is expected in dev") is acceptable for delegated writers. Absent all three, **(c) is the default** — it is the zero-new-infra option consistent with the system's untrusted-relay premise.
392:| `crates/fuse` | Symmetric child-key unwrap; delete `spawn_file_meta_reencrypt`; add `rotateReadFromNode`; unify scope-exit; `Node` as Rust enum | High (Rust, two clients) |
394:| `apps/web` | Replace `executeLazyRotation` with `rotateReadFromNode`; drop per-mutation fan-out; reconcile `folderTree` | Medium-High |
402:3. `packages/sdk-core` — read-chain navigation + `rotateReadFromNode` driver (in named files, not a fat `index.ts` barrel — coverage excludes barrels). Rebuild dist before consumers.
```

> AGENT

Confirmed — **both invariants are explicit in §4**, with one clarification about the audit's framing.

**E(ii) grant re-mint excluding the revoked recipient — explicitly present:**
- §4.2 (root step), line 263: *"Re-mint `readKeyEcies` for **every remaining recipient whose grant root is R**…; **delete the revoked recipient's row.**"*
- §4.4 / HIGH-3, line 278: rotation enumerates all `shares` rows in the rotated set and re-mints *"for each **non-revoked recipient**."*
- §3.1 cost table, line 209: *"Read-revoke (1 of R) … re-mint **R−1 grants**."*

**E(i) cascade scope — explicitly present, and defined recursively:**
- §3.6/§3.8: scope-exit calls `rotateReadFromNode` over *"the departing subtree"*; rotation is defined recursively so *"there is no un-rotated tail because the walk is the definition"* (line 244) — this is the structural kill of the old `executeLazyRotation:602` single-node bug.
- §4.5 walk re-seals each node's read-body and *"push[es] N's children with `readKey'`"* (line 306); §4.2 root step *cuts the entry point first* (line 266).

**The clarification:** the sweep's CRITICAL ("revoked reader keeps siblings via the cached *parent* key") was a **mis-frame** — it assumed you rotate a single node while the reader holds the parent. In the actual model you rotate at the **reader's grant root**, which by definition covers every sibling/descendant they could reach. So it's not a gap. *However*, §4.3 (M1) already names the one **real** root-level residual: the IPNS signature doesn't cover `generation`, and a grantee reads `rootGeneration` as a **relay-served DB value** — so a colluding relay that simply **drops the rotation publish** leaves the revoked reader alive at the root with no signed signal. That residual is *exactly* Cluster A, and the **durable client-side generation+seq high-water you ratified in (1) is its defense.** So §4.3-M1 and Cluster-A are the same fix — worth a cross-reference when I write it up.

Net: nothing new to add for E — it's covered. All decisions are now ruled. Folding the ratified set into the amendments now; let me read the file to append consistently.

1	# Read Key-Chaining Design — Amendments
2	
3	Amendments to `2026-06-26-sharing-read-keychaining-design.md`, produced from a grilling session with the maintainer on 2026-06-26. These supersede the cited sections of the design doc where they conflict. Treat this file as the authoritative delta until the base design doc is rewritten to absorb it.
4	
5	Cross-references: [`docs/adr/0001-write-revocation-full-ed25519-rotation.md`](../../docs/adr/0001-write-revocation-full-ed25519-rotation.md), [`docs/adr/0002-read-revocation-protects-future-content-only.md`](../../docs/adr/0002-read-revocation-protects-future-content-only.md), and the root [`CONTEXT.md`](../../CONTEXT.md) glossary.
6	
7	## Decisions ratified
8	
9	1. **Greenfield.** No production instance; staging was wiped. No data migration, no dual-codec, no `version`-discriminator bridge. `node/v3` is the only codec; delete the v1/v2 read paths and the `share_keys` entity outright.
10	2. **Delete/move/rename rotate only on scope exit.** A node with no covering grant is a pure relink — zero rotations. The §3.6 "delete = rotate" framing is wrong.
11	3. **Revocation is lazy + honest** (ADR 0002). Revoke protects future writes + navigation + filenames; already-distributed content and prior versions are presumed leaked.
12	4. **Scope computation is client-side**; FUSE gains grant-root awareness; the relay supplies the grant-root set; web rotates online and rotates only the items that left scope; reconcile-then-rotate, defer if the tree can't be reconciled.
13	5. **Republisher signs the canonical `latestCid`, never its own snapshot** (security-critical). The resolve someguy-vs-DB precedence is a confirmed read-revocation bypass vector.
14	6. **Write-revocation = approach (c)** full Ed25519 rotation (ADR 0001); it is the most expensive operation in the system.
15	7. **Invites folded in** as an ephemeral-wrapped single `readKey` + claim-time re-wrap-to-self; delete the `encryptedChildKeys` fan-out.
16	8. **`generation` is not forward-protected by the CAS gate**; safety needs same-parent serialization + the M1 client check + a server-side generation gate.
17	9. **AAD does not enforce topology**; its `generation` is sourced from the parent mirror / grant `rootGeneration`.
18	10. **Content keeps `encryptionMode`** (GCM/CTR) per node and per version.
19	11. **Bin is in scope** and gets simpler under content self-seal.
20	12. **Terminology pinned** (`readKey`/`writeKey`, the three counters, `shares` + descriptor refs, `ipns_records`).
21	13. **Drop `folder_ipns.public_key`**; recover the Ed25519 pubkey from the k51 name.
22	
23	## §1 / §6 — Scope is greenfield (migration removed)
24	
25	There is no production instance and staging was wiped, so the design's silent live-data-cutover assumption is void. Remove any migration framing. Build `node/v3` as the sole codec; delete v1/v2 read paths and the `share_keys` entity rather than bridging them. The vault-key recovery blob is re-designed (not migrated) to carry **two** keys — `ECIES(rootReadKey)` + `ECIES(rootWriteKey)` — since the root Node has both a read and a write key. Terminology can be renamed cleanly in code from day one (no transitional coexistence).
26	
27	## §3.6 / §3.8 — Delete rule rewrite (scope-exit only)
28	
29	Replace "delete collapses to rotate" with the per-grant predicate, stated as a tested invariant:
30	
31	> Rotate iff the node leaves the reachable scope of at least one active grant; "reachable" means reachable by a grantee, not the owner. A node with no covering grant is a pure relink (zero rotations).
32	
33	"No covering grant ⇒ 0 rotation" must be a hard test. Taken literally, the old wording would rotate on every private delete — an O(subtree) storm over the unshared 99% of a vault. For the shared case, compose the shipped `revoke-for-items` row-revoke (#563) **with** rotation, preserving its ordering invariant (never a window where the item is gone but its key is not yet rotated).
34	
35	## §3.5 / §4 — Scope computation and the FUSE blind spot
36	
37	The scope predicate ("is node X reachable from any active grant root?") is **inherently client-side** — the relay cannot answer it, because parent-to-child links live in the sealed read-body and only a key-holder can walk ancestry. Amendments:
38	
39	- The relay supplies the active grant-root set (`shares` keyed by `rootIpnsName` — plaintext it already holds). The client walks the mutated node's ancestor chain against that set.
40	- Web computes coverage from `folderTree` **reconciled to the current `sequenceNumber` first** (per the existing reconcile-before-publish discipline). A wrong "don't rotate" is a silent missed revoke, so when the tree cannot be reconciled the mutation **defers** rather than skips rotation.
41	- **FUSE must gain a grant-root concept** in its `delete`/`rename`/`move` paths (new work; add to the blast radius). It already holds the mounted tree, so ancestry is cheap — compute exact per-grant scope rather than the design's conservative "rotate on any ancestor-set change." This disposes of open Q3 (no over-rotation on benign within-scope moves).
42	
43	## §4.1 / §4.8 — Revocation semantics (lazy content rekey)
44	
45	See ADR 0002. Reword §4.8: "eager rotation" means eager cut of navigation + future writes, **not** eager content protection. A file rotated on revoke gets a fresh `fileKey` only on its next content write (`contentRekeyPending`); a cold file that is never rewritten keeps its old `fileKey` valid, and the still-pinned CID remains decryptable by anyone who held the key. Every revoke flow must carry the caveat that already-distributed content and all prior versions stay readable. Optionally offer per-file "re-encrypt now" and O(versions) "purge history".
46	
47	## §4.6 — Convergence claim corrections
48	
49	§4.6's "every op is a forward-only function on generation + sequence" is **false**: the CAS gate enforces forward-only **sequence** only; `generation` lives in the body/envelope and is not gated. A stale-key holder can republish a cached pre-rotation body (re-sealed under the old `readKey`) at a forward sequence, regressing `generation` and silently undoing the cut. HIGH-4's re-merge covers dropped children, not this. Corrected invariant and controls:
50	
51	- **Same-parent serialization.** Add/rename/move on a node take the same `PublishCoordinator.get_lock(name)` the rotation holds, so a stale-key add cannot interleave with that node's rotation locally.
52	- **M1 client check** (below) is the fail-closed backstop for the cross-client / colluding-relay case.
53	- **Server-side generation gate (ratified, defence-in-depth).** Because `generation` is plaintext on the published envelope, extend the publish gate to enforce forward-only generation per node, mirroring the sequence anti-rollback and its wild-jump / wedge-poison handling (`ipns.service.ts:313`).
54	
55	Restate the invariant as: forward-only **sequence** (CAS-enforced) + forward-only **generation** (same-parent serialization + M1 client check + server-side gate), not by the CAS alone.
56	
57	## §4.3 — M1 generation monotonicity is net-new durable client state
58	
59	Confirmed against code: **no resolve path enforces a per-node `generation` check today.** `resolve_sequence_strict` (`crates/fuse/src/publish.rs:140`) tracks only `sequence`, in-memory, lost on restart; `VerifiedResolve` exposes `{cid, sequence_number}` and never decodes node metadata; web `resolveIpnsRecord` performs the same sequence-only checks. So §4.3's M1 defence is **new work**, not an extension:
60	
61	- Persist `{nodeId → highestGeneration}` durably (IndexedDB / sqlite, beside the sequence cache), seeded from the grant's `rootGeneration` (owner-vouched floor).
62	- Thread it into `resolve_ipns_verified` (Rust) and `resolveIpnsRecord` (web); fail closed on generation regression. On a first-ever resolve with no high-water mark, cross-check the envelope generation against the parent's `SealedChildRef.generation` mirror.
63	- Document the irreducible residual: a colluding relay can serve a victim a self-consistent OLD whole-subtree snapshot if it never lets them see any newer node (no signed generation closes this).
64	
65	## §4.6 — Republisher / resolve interaction (security-critical)
66	
67	The 6-hour TEE republisher is **not** orthogonal to rotation. It signs from its own `ipns_republish_schedule.latestCid` snapshot (`republish.service.ts:101,136`), which is refreshed on publish only when `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` is supplied (`ipns.service.ts:349-358`). A read-key rotation does not rotate the Ed25519 key, so the snapshot stays at the pre-rotation CID; the next republish re-signs the **stale (revoked-readable) CID at a forward sequence**, and `syncFolderIpnsSequence` (`republish.service.ts:379-385`) writes it into `folder_ipns.signedRecord` without updating `latestCid`.
68	
69	Confirmed reachable: the relay's `resolveRecord` (`ipns.service.ts:456`) queries both someguy and the DB; when the inconsistent DB row trips the codec guard (`ipns-record.codec.ts:73`, `signedRecord`-CID ≠ `latestCid`) it returns null and the service falls through to serve the someguy network record verbatim (`ipns.service.ts:506-540`). Every downstream verifier accepts it because the old record is internally self-consistent. **This is an exploitable read-revocation bypass.**
70	
71	Fix (ratified): the republisher sources its CID from the canonical `folder_ipns.latestCid` at sign time (never its own snapshot), and `schedule.latestCid` is refreshed on every publish (decouple from the `encryptedIpnsPrivateKey` guard). Add a section to the design covering the rotation × republisher × resolve interaction (currently under-specified), and reconsider the someguy-vs-DB precedence in `resolveRecord` given recent churn.
72	
73	## §5 — Write-revocation ratified as (c)
74	
75	See ADR 0001. Re-cost §5.3 honestly: (c) is **not** "a subset of the read-rotation machinery" — it is strictly heavier. Read-revoke keeps k51 names stable and descends; write-revoke under (c) mints a new keypair and k51 name per node, cascades parent re-points **upward** to the share root, re-enrolls/unenrolls the TEE per node, and re-points all co-grants + owner devices. The explainer's `(c)` framing and flow 08 are now correct; relabel nothing as "preview". Also fix the §5.4-vs-§8 inconsistency: full (a) uniquely serializes the sequence race (atomic sign + assign); the §8 "no candidate solves the race, don't factor it in" line wrongly erases (a)'s only real edge.
76	
77	## §2.2 / §2.6 — Write-body shape (resolved)
78	
79	Approach (c) is ratified, so the write-body is the structured recursive write chain the explainer shows: each node's write-body holds its Ed25519 signing material, and each parent write-body seals the child's `writeKey` (the explainer's `writeKeySealed: AES-GCM(child.writeKey, parent.writeKey)`). Reserve AAD `role = 0x04 child-writekey` for this. `SealedChildRef` stays read-only (one sealed field, `readKeySealed`); the write link lives in the parent write-body, not in `SealedChildRef`.
80	
81	## §2.5 / §2.6 — AAD semantics and byte encoding
82	
83	- **Transplant claim reworded.** The AAD does not bind `parentId`, and a legitimate move re-seals byte-identical AAD under a new parent — so the AAD does **not** enforce topology. State it as: the AAD prevents stale-generation replay and cross-node-id confusion; topology is enforced by parent-`readKey` possession.
84	- **Generation source.** The reader's expected AAD `generation` comes from the parent's `SealedChildRef.generation` mirror (integrity-anchored via the signed CID chain), or, for a share-root, from the grant's `rootGeneration`. The node's own envelope plaintext `generation` is used only for the M1 high-water check and dirty-edge detection — never as unseal key-material input. This makes a stale-child serve fail closed.
85	- **Byte encoding frozen** (blocks the KAT, so freeze first): `kind` = `folder 0x01 / file 0x02 / root 0x03`; `nodeId` = the raw 16 RFC-4122 bytes (`uuid.as_bytes()`, canonical field order), not a hash; `generation` = 4-byte big-endian; `role` = `{0x01 body, 0x02 child-readkey, 0x03 content, 0x04 child-writekey}`. Pin all of it as the first vector in `crates/crypto/tests/cross_language.rs`, asserted by `packages/crypto` too.
86	
87	## §2.3 / §2.9 — Content schema
88	
89	Carry `encryptionMode` (`'GCM' | 'CTR'`) in `content` **and** in each `VersionEntry` — CTR powers large-file range reads (`aes_ctr::decrypt_aes_ctr_range`); do not normalise to GCM-only. The `fileKeyEncrypted → content.fileKey` change is a **semantic type change** (ECIES hex string → raw 32-byte key inside the sealed body), applied to both content and every `VersionEntry`; document it as a type change in `METADATA_SCHEMAS.md`, not a rename.
90	
91	## New section — Invites (link/email sharing)
92	
93	Invites are shipped (`share_invites` table, `share-invite.service.ts`) and in-scope per `CLAUDE.md`, but the design omits them. Under `node/v3`:
94	
95	- An invite wraps the **single share-root `readKey`** to an ephemeral public key; the ephemeral private key travels in the URL fragment (never reaches the server — zero-knowledge holds). Delete the `encryptedChildKeys[]` fan-out (JSONB column) — the read chain obsoletes it.
96	- On claim, the claimer unwraps `readKey` with the URL-fragment ephemeral private key, **re-wraps it to their own public key**, and the server stores a standard `shares` grant. A multi-claim invite mints one standard grant per claimer of the same `readKey`. Revoke = rotate the `readKey` (cuts the link and all claimers at once).
97	- Accepted exposure: a v3 invite link carries the subtree-root `readKey`; anyone with the link reads the granted subtree — identical in spirit to today's link semantics.
98	
99	## New section — Bin (recoverable delete)
100	
101	The bin is shipped (`packages/core/src/bin/*`, `sdk/src/bin/*`, `spawn_bin_entry_publish`) and absent from the design. Under `node/v3`:
102	
103	- A `BinEntry` becomes a `SealedChildRef`-shaped link sealed under the bin's own `readKey`. **Restore = pure re-link** (re-seal the node's `readKey` under the destination parent), identical to a move. `originalFolderKeyEncrypted` and the re-encrypt-on-restore path become dead code — delete them.
104	- Private delete → unlink + `BinEntry`, no rotation. Shared delete → rotate the departing subtree + revoke the grant rows (composing #563) + `BinEntry`. Permanent delete → unpin CIDs + drop grant rows.
105	- Add `bin/*` to the blast radius.
106	
107	## §3.1 — Cost table: add Copy
108	
109	Content self-seal means a copy cannot alias the source CID — it must decrypt and re-encrypt under a fresh `fileKey`, yielding a new CID. Add a row:
110	
111	> Copy | decrypt + re-encrypt under a fresh `fileKey` → new CID | no re-grant | no rotation | new node's own | O(content); new CID pins under the copier's quota
112	
113	## Terminology (see CONTEXT.md)
114	
115	- Adopt `readKey` / `writeKey` (not `nodeKey`). Add them and `generation` to the `CLAUDE.md` terminology table, with a Counters sub-table distinguishing `sequenceNumber` / `keyEpoch` / `generation`. Fully retire `folderKey` / `fileKey` / `rootFolderKey` from code (greenfield).
116	- Grant row: keep table `shares`; use `readDescriptorRef` / `writeDescriptorRef`; retire `readKeyEcies` and the explainer's `ShareGrant` name.
117	- Rename table `folder_ipns` → `ipns_records` (entity `IpnsRecord`, `ipnsRecordRepository`) — it holds the IPNS records for files, root, bin, and the vault-key blob too, not just folders. Free under greenfield.
118	- Drop `folder_ipns.public_key`: it is the raw 32-byte Ed25519 IPNS pubkey (`ipns.service.ts:72-79` validates length 32 and `deriveIpnsName(pubkey) === ipnsName`), not the user's secp256k1 `publicKey` (the owner is tracked by `userId`). It is derivable from the k51 name via `publicKeyFromIpnsName`, so drop the nullable column and always recover from the name — removing the null-row footgun behind two Phase-60 regressions.
119	
120	## §6.1 — Blast-radius additions
121	
122	- FUSE grant-root awareness in `delete` / `rename` / `move`.
123	- `bin/*` (core, sdk, FUSE `spawn_bin_entry_publish`).
124	- M1 durable client state (high-water generation map) on both clients.
125	- Republisher canonical-CID fix + `schedule.latestCid` refresh on every publish.
126	- `resolveRecord` someguy-vs-DB precedence review.
127	- `folder_ipns` → `ipns_records` rename (entity, repository, all references).
128	
129	## §6.3 — Test additions
130	
131	- Republisher re-signs a stale CID mid-rotation → assert the revoked CID is never re-signed and never served.
132	- Stale-key add during rotation (generation regression) → assert it is rejected / re-converged.
133	- M1 generation monotonicity → fail closed on a generation downgrade across resolves.
134	- CTR content + a CTR version both decrypt under the v3 content schema.
135	- Invite claim re-wraps the share-root `readKey` to the claimer; revoke (rotate) cuts the link and all claimers.
136	- Bin restore is a pure re-link (no re-encrypt); shared delete rotates + revokes.
137	
138	## Citation corrections (verified against code)
139	
140	- The per-child ECIES unwrap the read chain replaces is at `crates/fuse/src/inode.rs:434,452` (also `:658,716`, `replay.rs:365`) — **not** `metadata.rs:428-453`, which is `spawn_bin_entry_publish`.
141	- `spawn_file_meta_reencrypt` is defined at `metadata.rs:655`; callers are `write_ops/implementation/rename.rs:248` **and** `platform/windows/write_ops.rs:1182` (the WinFsp twin the design omits — killing it must touch both and round-trip the Windows CI gate).
142	- The republisher lives at `apps/api/src/republish/republish.service.ts`, not under `ipns/`.
143	
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/myankelev/Code/random/cipher-box/.planning/design/2026-06-26-sharing-read-keychaining-AMENDMENTS.md",
  "old_string": "- The republisher lives at `apps/api/src/republish/republish.service.ts`, not under `ipns/`.",
  "new_string": "- The republisher lives at `apps/api/src/republish/republish.service.ts`, not under `ipns/`.\n\n## Session 2 (resolve / republish / TEE) — ratified 2026-06-26\n\nA second grilling session covered the resolve path, the republish path, whether the republish must increment the IPNS sequence, where the TEE sources the data it signs, and a six-lens adversarial failure/vulnerability sweep. These **refine and in places supersede the §4.6 republisher amendment above** — the canonical-CID fix is now achieved structurally by decisions 16–17 rather than by patching the snapshot refresh. All claims below were verified against current code (two structured-verification workflows).\n\n### Decisions ratified (continued)\n\n14. **`generation` (M1) is the anti-rollback _authority_; resolve-source is a latency/availability layer beneath it.** The IPFS network is permissionless — its only anti-rollback is \"higher sequence wins; equal sequence, later EOL wins\" (verified against boxo/go-ipns `compare`), so it cannot be the integrity authority. DB-canonical near-term: the relay writes the DB synchronously _before_ the fire-and-forget someguy push (`ipns.service.ts:106-144`), so the DB leads the DHT by ~10-30s — the \"network is fresher\" latency intuition is **inverted** in the relay-mediated topology. \"Network strictly ahead of DB\" is an **alarm**, not a normal branch. The maintainer's network-as-source-of-truth ideal is reachable for _confidentiality_ once M1 ships (generation rejects any cross-generation rollback regardless of source); the residual barrier is _within-generation_ consistency, which makes fully-decentralized resolve a post-M1 / v2 move.\n15. **Sequence advances iff the CID changes; republish _never_ increments.** A republish re-signs the _same_ sequence with a fresh EOL — IPNS record selection's equal-sequence→later-EOL tiebreak lets the refreshed record win without consuming a sequence. The relay publish path already does this on the idempotent branch (`ipns.service.ts:306-317`, \"D-09\"); the **TEE 6-hour republisher still does `+ 1n`** (`apps/tee-worker/src/routes/republish.ts:79`) and must be unified to the no-increment path. Incrementing on republish is not just unnecessary but harmful — it races client writes for sequence numbers and widens the replay window. This invariant _alone_ closes the §4.6 republisher-stale-CID rollback (a re-signed stale CID stays at its old sequence and is dominated by any genuine forward client publish). Increment policy moves out of the enclave into the relay.\n16. **Collapse the dual-source record state.** `ipns_republish_schedule` duplicates `latestCid` / `sequenceNumber` / `encryptedIpnsKey` / `keyEpoch` (`republish-schedule.entity.ts:39-60`) and the TEE signs _that_ snapshot (`republish.service.ts:101-102`), which goes stale on a normal content write (refreshed only on key-enrollment). Make the canonical `ipns_records` row the **sole** source of the TEE's signing inputs; reduce the schedule to scheduling metadata (`next_republish_at`, `consecutive_failures`, `status`) — or fold those columns into `ipns_records` and drop the table. This structurally kills both the stale-CID rollback _and_ a latent availability bug: today the republisher keeps the _old_ CID's network record fresh while the canonical new CID's record expires ~48h after the client's one-time publish (masked only by DB-canonical resolve).\n17. **The TEE is a record-_lease-renewer_, not a signer of supplied scalars.** Clients self-sign every content change with their client-held Ed25519 key (`packages/core/src/ipns/create-record.ts`; the relay only verifies, `ipns.service.ts:100`), so the TEE never needs to originate a CID. New enclave contract: the relay sends the **marshaled existing `signedRecord`**; the TEE parses it, **verifies its signature**, and re-emits a record with the same value (CID) and same sequence, only a later EOL — so it **cannot originate or repoint a CID**. \"Verify against what the network resolves\" was rejected as the mechanism: it is circular (the relay controls the enclave's network view), inverts the ratified source-of-truth (the network is the lagging untrusted replica), and fights the propagation window. Worst residual: a malicious relay replays an _old_ lower-seq validly-signed record for renewal — dominated by sequence and caught by M1.\n18. **Complete the resolve anti-rollback (the seq-floor companion to M1).** `generation` only bumps on rotation, so _within-generation_ version rollback — serving an old, genuinely-signed, lower-seq record in the same generation — passes every current check. Add: (a) a **durable per-node `{nodeId → highestSeq}` high-water** on the client (the sequence analog of the M1 generation map), rejecting `seq < high-water` regardless of resolve source; (b) **bind a version floor** (current seq, or a hash of the head Node) into the `SealedChildRef` at (re)share, so first-contact and cold/reset devices inherit an owner-vouched floor from the parent chain (the `SealedChildRef` mirrors generation but not version today); (c) the relay must **never silently fall through to an ungated network record** — when the canonical DB row is unparseable (`parseCachedRecord` null: missing `signedRecord`, or `signedRecord`-CID ≠ `latestCid`; notably shared-folder rows with null `signedRecord`/`public_key`), **fail closed** or apply a `seq ≥ storedSeq` floor from the DB `sequenceNumber` column. This closes the §4.3-M1 colluding-relay-drops-publish residual — the durable client floor is the signed-signal-independent defense.\n19. **Atomic publish CAS.** `publishRecord` is a non-atomic `findOne → gate → save` with no row lock / `@VersionColumn` / conditional UPDATE, so two concurrent forward writers both at `dbSeq = N` both pass the gate and the second `save` clobbers the first — a `200`'d write silently lost (generation cannot help; same generation). Decision 16's single canonical row makes it the sole serialization point and decision 17's lease-renewal hits the idempotent branch on it, _widening_ the race. Fix: a single compare-and-set — `UPDATE … SET … WHERE ipnsName = :n AND sequenceNumber = :expected`, 0 rows affected ⇒ 409 — with the idempotent/renewal write guarded identically (`WHERE sequenceNumber = :loaded`) so an EOL-only renewal can never regress `latestCid`/`sequenceNumber` from a stale in-memory row.\n20. **Three enclave bindings beyond decision 17.** The relay still feeds the enclave the epoch scalars, the wrapped key, and the claimed name. Harden: (a) the TEE **derives `currentEpoch`/`previousEpoch` from its own clock + epoch schedule** (never the relay's scalars), with re-wrap targets restricted to an enclave-enumerated set — else a malicious relay coerces re-wrapping every IPNS key under an attacker-chosen epoch pubkey for later offline forgery; (b) **name↔key binding** — before emitting, assert `publicKeyFromIpnsName(ipnsName) == pubkey(decryptedKey) == record.pubkey` (closes batch cross-contamination / key-confusion / cross-name forgery); (c) **migration durability** — because a malicious relay can drop the returned `upgradedEncryptedKey` and brick a name at epoch retirement, make the **client** the recovery path (periodic re-enroll / re-wrap from its held key), or have the TEE refuse to renew a key older than `currentEpoch − 1`.\n21. **Tombstone the rotated-out IPNS name (tombstone-and-keep).** Approach-(c) write-rotation changes the k51 name and re-points parents, but `unenrollIpns` deletes only the schedule row (`republish.service.ts:257`) — the old `ipns_records` row persists and the publish gate has zero revocation awareness, so a revoked writer's cached key can publish to the old name **forever** and resolve still serves it to stale links. On rotation, **tombstone** the old row (keep it, do not hard-delete): the publish gate rejects all writes to a tombstoned name, resolve returns a tombstone / `410` (never stale content), and the name is TEE-unenrolled. Tombstone-and-keep so stale links/bookmarks get an explicit \"moved/revoked\" signal rather than silent stale content.\n\n### §4.6 supersession note\n\nThe Session-1 §4.6 fix (\"republisher sources canonical `folder_ipns.latestCid`; refresh `schedule.latestCid` on every publish; reconsider the someguy-vs-DB precedence\") is **subsumed**: decision 16 removes the schedule's `latestCid` entirely (nothing to refresh), decision 17 makes the TEE re-sign the existing record rather than take a CID scalar, and decisions 14 + 18 resolve the precedence (DB-canonical + fail-closed fall-through). Keep §4.6's _diagnosis_; replace its _fix_ with decisions 14–18.\n\n### Accepted residuals (resolve / republish / TEE)\n\n- **Compromised enclave / leaked epoch key = total loss** — every wrapped IPNS key is unwrappable offline and every vault repointable. Decision 17 bounds the _honest_ enclave's worst case to lower-seq replay; it does **not** contain a malicious enclave. This rests entirely on Phala remote-attestation (enforced on every epoch-key provisioning) + epoch-rotation cadence (bounds the exposure window). State it as the explicit systemic residual.\n- **Equal-sequence EOL selection** is a freshness/availability nuisance only: under decisions 15 + 17 same-sequence records must share a CID, so the relay's choice of which equal-seq record a client sees cannot fork content. Escalate only if equal-seq distinct-CID records can ever be minted.\n- **Already-distributed ciphertext stays readable** (ADR 0002) — unchanged.\n\n### §6.1 — Blast-radius additions (session 2)\n\n- TEE enclave contract rewrite (lease-renewer: receive marshaled record, verify signature, extend EOL; internal epoch derivation; name↔key binding) — `apps/tee-worker`, `packages/core/src/ipns`.\n- Collapse `ipns_republish_schedule` duplicated columns into `ipns_records` (or reduce the table to scheduling metadata); rework `republish.service.ts` to source canonical inputs.\n- Atomic conditional-UPDATE publish CAS in `publishRecord` + the idempotent/renewal branch.\n- Durable per-node `highestSeq` high-water on both clients, beside the M1 generation map.\n- `SealedChildRef` version floor (read-body schema + share-mint path).\n- Tombstone state on `ipns_records` + publish-gate rejection + resolve tombstone/410 + TEE unenroll.\n- Client-side re-enroll / re-wrap recovery path for epoch-key migration.\n\n### §6.3 — Test additions (session 2)\n\n- Within-generation rollback: relay serves an old lower-seq same-generation signed record → client rejects via the seq high-water.\n- First-contact / cold-device rollback: fresh client with no local high-water → the `SealedChildRef` version floor rejects a below-floor seq.\n- `parseCachedRecord`-null fall-through: an unparseable canonical DB row → resolve fails closed (or applies the seq floor), never serving an ungated network record.\n- Concurrent forward publishes (two devices at the same `dbSeq`) → exactly one 409, zero lost updates.\n- Lease-renewal racing a forward publish → the renewal never regresses `latestCid`/`sequenceNumber`.\n- TEE name↔key binding: a swapped wrapped key / wrong-name slot → the enclave refuses to emit.\n- TEE epoch self-derivation: an attacker-supplied `currentEpoch` is ignored; re-wrap only targets an enclave-valid epoch.\n- Tombstoned name: writes rejected; resolve returns the tombstone, not stale content."
}

# CipherBox Read Key-Chaining — Implementation-Ready Design

Status: design complete, implementation-ready. Tier 1 is firm. Tier 2 (write revocation) is a maintainer decision with a recommendation. Tier 3 is a non-required forward-compatibility note.

This document integrates four design slices (schema, flows, rotation, cutover) and applies the fixes from three adversarial reviews. Blocker and major findings are resolved inline; deferred items are flagged with rationale.

## 1. Overview and rationale

### 1.1 What we are building

CipherBox is a zero-knowledge encrypted IPFS/IPNS vault: the server is a dumb relay that never sees plaintext or any unwrapped/derivable key. We are replacing the DB-driven sharing model (a per-`(share, item, keyType)` `share_keys` table — `O(items × recipients)` rows) with **metadata-driven read key-chaining**, and fixing two confirmed revocation gaps.

## Problem

PRs #563 (web/SDK) and #568 (desktop FUSE) added fail-closed share revocation on
**delete** — but only on the delete-to-bin path. When a file/folder is **renamed
or moved OVER an existing destination** (replace/overwrite), the destination node
is destroyed WITHOUT revoking its shares. This is the same data-exposure class as
the delete bug those PRs closed: the destination's `Share` / `ShareInvite` rows
stay active against content that has been replaced (and, on the unix file branch,
its CID is even unpinned), so sharees retain read access to vanished content.

Confirmed by the #568 completeness review as **pre-existing on every surface**:

- **Unix FUSE:** `crates/fuse/src/write_ops/implementation/rename.rs` (~163-186) —
  the File branch destroys the dest node and even spawns `unpin_content` on the
  dest CID, with no share revocation.
- **Windows FUSE:** `crates/fuse/src/platform/windows/write_ops.rs` (~1097-1124) —

## Problem

PR #563 added fail-closed share revocation when an item is soft-deleted to the
recycle bin — but ONLY in the SDK/web path: `CipherBoxClient.addToBin`
(`packages/sdk/src/client.ts`) injects `revokeSharesForItemsFn` into
`binOps.addToBin` (`packages/sdk/src/bin/index.ts`), which walks the deleted
subtree and calls `POST /shares/revoke-for-items` before the destructive folder
mutation.

Desktop (Tauri + FUSE) deletes go through a **parallel Rust-only path that never
touches that wiring**:

- `crates/fuse/src/write_ops/implementation/delete.rs:58` `handle_unlink` (file)
- `crates/fuse/src/write_ops/implementation/delete.rs:208` `handle_rmdir` (folder)
- both call `publish_bin_entry_on_delete` → `spawn_bin_entry_publish`
  (`crates/fuse/src/metadata.rs:424`)

---
created: 2026-02-22T00:00
title: Research CRDT-based IPNS inbox for serverless share discovery
area: architecture
related:
  - .planning/todos/pending/2026-02-21-ipns-resolution-alternatives.md
  - .planning/phases/14-user-to-user-sharing/14-CONTEXT.md
  - .planning/todos/pending/2026-02-21-move-root-folder-key-to-ipfs.md
---

## Summary

Research using CRDTs over IPNS as a decentralized share discovery mechanism, replacing the centralized `shares` table. This is part of a broader strategy to solve IPNS write-conflict issues once as a horizontal concern rather than working around them per-feature.

## Context

Phase 14 chose server-side share records (PostgreSQL `shares`/`share_keys` tables) over metadata-embedded ACLs. The server knows the sharing graph (who shared with whom) but never sees plaintext keys. This was the right pragmatic tradeoff, but it moves further from the serverless ideal.

A recurring pattern across CipherBox: every time we hit an IPNS limitation (write conflicts, resolution flakiness, last-writer-wins), we bolt on a centralized workaround (DB-cached CIDs, share tables, pinned_cids tracking). Each workaround is individually reasonable, but collectively they erode the serverless premise. Solving IPNS reliability and conflict resolution as a horizontal concern would unblock multiple features simultaneously.

## Core Idea: IPNS Inbox with CRDT Content Model

### Share Discovery via Deterministic IPNS Address

Derive a per-recipient IPNS "inbox" address from their public key:

```text
inbox_ipns_privkey = HKDF(recipient_pubkey, info="share-inbox")
inbox_ipns_name    = derive_public(inbox_ipns_privkey)
```
